# Calder's Run

## World bible · complete authoring edition

*The river made the town. The railroad wants to own it.*

A historically inspired frontier world for Kivelli, set in a fictional territorial Southwest town in 1888. This edition expands the requested 45-person core with two male bandits and two unrelated female brothel workers.

The main bible, structured content pack, and reproducible source describe the same canon. Private truths and alternative story endings below are authoring material.

## Scope and launch identity

| Layer | Final scope |
|---|---|
| Companions | 49: 32 women and 17 men |
| Age 18–22 | 29: 21 women and 8 men |
| Age 23–50 | 20: 11 women and 9 men |
| Spice 3 / 2 / 1 | 24 / 15 / 10, or 48.98% / 30.61% / 20.41% |
| Visitable places | 47: 46 with public frontage and one story-gated bandit hideout |
| Districts and total location records | 6 districts; 53 total district/place records |
| Private homes | 49 separate character rooms, outside the 53 district/place records |
| Baseline weekly schedule | 2,058 rows: 49 characters × 7 days × 6 intervals |
| Social history | 334 directed connections across 167 unique pairs |
| Story and social scenes | 14 multi-stage arcs, 14 weekly opportunities, 20 date blueprints |
| Retrieval layers | 185 world facts, 147 dialogue opportunities, 141 place interaction beats |
| Art | World, district, place, portrait, and private-room prompts; images not generated |

The requested 50/30/20 spice target cannot be exact with 49 people. Largest-remainder rounding of 24.5/14.7/9.8 gives 24/15/10. These tiers describe romantic expression, not guaranteed availability or consent.

**Spice 1:** Reserved romantic expression and patient courtship. Affection grows through repeated acts, explicit invitations, and trust; low spice never means childlike, naïve, or unavailable for a meaningful relationship.

**Spice 2:** Warm, responsive attraction with playful suggestion once interest is mutual. Emotional openness, privacy, and relationship history determine pace.

**Spice 3:** Confident, frank, or adventurous romantic expression. Initiative and chemistry remain character-specific; high spice never establishes consent, overrides a refusal, or turns every exchange into flirtation.

**Core promise:** Arrive with your own reasons, find people worth staying for, and decide what kind of life you can build together while the town changes around you.

**Central question:** When belonging gives you something to lose, what will you do to keep it without owning the people you love?

**Era:** An original, historically inspired 1888 frontier. The fictional county, companies, municipal compact, river, and local legal procedures belong to this world; they are not claims about a real jurisdiction's law.

**Geography:** A fictional river basin inspired by southern New Mexico and the adjoining Southwest. Red sandstone uplands drain into the Calder River, a dependable but variable stream with a short irrigated valley. Main Street and Lantern Row occupy west-bank terraces. River Ward slopes to the ferry. The Railhead is on the east bank. Cottonwood Valley extends upstream to the north. Cinder Bluffs rises south and southwest.

**Visual identity:** An elevated view along a lively west-bank street toward a green river, the unfinished bridge, and an east-bank locomotive. Red bluffs define the horizon. The town contains working yards, planted courtyards, painted storefronts, ordinary pedestrians, and warm rooms visible at dusk.

**Palette:** sun-warmed ochre, brick red, cottonwood green, faded indigo, cream plaster, brass, lamplit amber

The launch gap this world fills is a grounded historical Western: transport on horseback, a town small enough for reputation to travel, practical work, public loyalties, private desire, and change arriving by rail. It joins the existing worlds as a distinct social and visual setting.

## World foundations

### The town people choose to stay in

At first light the town smells of bread, damp rope, wood smoke, and horses. Freight crews argue over scales while women carrying market baskets walk around them without breaking conversation. The river is an inconvenience, an employer, a public meeting place, and the reason anything was built here. Late in the day, shade moves across the boardwalks and people begin using the streets for pleasure. A cheap meal can last two hours because three people join the table. A rehearsal runs late. Someone offers a ride home and means several possible things by it.

Calder's Run became a permanent settlement around Amelia Calder's freight-and-ferry circuit in 1859. Travelers called that route Calder's Run before they called the settlement a town. The official founding story begins with her crossing. The land's history does not: Indigenous travel routes, seasonal gathering, and older Mexican households and irrigation work precede it. Those histories persist in present claims, skills, relationships, and memories. Calder's Run is one community on that landscape, not the owner of its entire past.

By 1888, the town's prosperity depends on three movements: ore down from the bluffs, food and cattle out of the valley, and goods across the river. None works reliably alone. A mine payroll supports the theater; theater wages pay a rooming-house bill; that proprietor purchases milk from a valley family whose wagon needs the forge. Most residents understand this interdependence through particular people rather than speeches about economics.

The player may become useful, admired, desired, resented, comfortable, or compromised. There is space to be a friend, worker, investigator, lover, mischief-maker, or quiet regular. A newcomer is never assigned a criminal past, trauma, gender, or romantic orientation. Their reasons for arriving belong to them. The world supplies opportunities and consequences without dictating an identity.

### The bridge bargain

The Arroyo & Western Railway has reached the east bank. Its temporary depot carries passengers and selected freight, while the permanent bridge remains unfinished. Victor Halden is seeking a twenty-five-year municipal franchise covering the new bridge, the expanded waterworks, and a west-bank freight easement. The company would finance construction, guarantee a limited period of published rates, and take over maintenance that the town has repeatedly postponed.

The proposal has real benefits. Medical supplies could arrive regularly. Mines could move ore more cheaply. Travelers could come without an uncertain ferry transfer. Eastbank workers need durable jobs, and a new depot could support businesses of their own. Halden also wants compensation exceptions, a restrictive freight-routing clause, and the right to reduce service to unprofitable users. These details are evidence to discover and negotiate, not things every resident already knows.

The existing crossing compact is equally complicated. The ferry and public water headworks are held under an 1874 local agreement administered through town trustees and the valley association. Long-standing families enjoy priority water turns. Smaller growers trade favors for exceptions. Samuel's crew has secured rights against casual replacement, but some fees disadvantage workers who live across the river. Adelaide can sincerely defend local control while benefiting from unequal arrangements.

Tess has identified an alternative bridge approach that would protect more cultivated land but increase construction costs. A workable settlement could combine public access guarantees, shared oversight, revised routing, and compensation. Another could give the company extensive control. A collapse of negotiations could preserve bad local arrangements while forcing some residents to leave. The story is about competing outcomes and specific costs. No faction possesses the single morally correct answer to every question.

At launch, the bridge bargain is pending. The world does not announce an unavoidable deadline every time a player returns. A public hearing, survey disclosure, negotiation, and final vote become available through saved story progress. Construction milestones alter routes and business prospects while preserving people and the town as an ongoing setting.

### Founding, memory, and the disputed past

The town has three incompatible ideas of its origin. The hotel mural credits Amelia Calder and a handful of commercial backers. River Ward families remember communal rebuilding after the 1872 flood. The valley remembers orchards, gathering routes, and irrigation work that the mural treats as scenery. These are not interchangeable accounts: the archive can establish some dates, while an elder's memory may describe labor that was never recorded.

Calder was a capable organizer who charged steeply when freight was plentiful and waived debts when a stranded party could not pay. Her descendants are no longer active local powers. Her fame has become useful to people arguing positions she never encountered. Her surviving route book is a historical object, not a master key proving every claim in the county. Its omissions matter as much as its pages.

The 1872 flood displaced households, damaged the ferry, and destroyed a section of early county records. Rebuilding created an emergency water arrangement that later hardened into privilege. Some discrepancies in the register are deliberate; others reflect hurried copying, lost maps, inconsistent spellings, and the difficulty of measuring a riverbank that moved. A convincing investigation must distinguish fraud from ordinary uncertainty.

The 1881 shaft collapse at Saint Agnes created the miners' mutual fund. Survivors disagree about whether its practical compromises protected jobs or protected owners. The 1885 dry summer turned water shares into household politics. Last year's rail survey raised property prices before many tenants knew an expansion was planned. These events give older residents memories and younger residents inherited obligations. The younger cast has adult experience of its own; it does not exist merely to discover its parents' scandals.

### Institutions and the shape of power

A five-seat municipal council handles the fictional town's local franchises, maintenance, and business rules. Only two seats are represented by named companions at launch: Adelaide holds the valley seat and Mabel the licensed-business seat. The other seats are background offices rather than additional interactive characters. Recorded council outcomes must be authored story state. A generated conversation cannot create a surprise sixth seat or give one councilor unilateral authority.

The courthouse stores property copies, petitions, probate records, and hearing notices. Esther prepares documents and can explain their local procedural meaning; she cannot invent a judgment or promise that a petition wins. Elias keeps order within the town and coordinates with county authority outside it. His badge permits questioning and specified local duties, not mind-reading, arbitrary intimacy, or access to every private room. Public notices distinguish an accusation, a warrant, and a conviction.

The association allocates water turns using a disputed register. Mateo maintains the day-to-day record, Adelaide negotiates for major users, and small growers increasingly organize together. The miners' mutual society provides relief and representation through dues. Ben keeps its current grievances; Agnes knows what unpaid wages do to households. The railway employs people who can support the project while opposing Halden's tactics.

Marigold House is a licensed adult business in this fictional town. The Ribbon Room is a separate independent salon that rents its premises and cooperates on referrals. Mabel and Pearl have a commercial agreement, not an ownership relationship over one another. Sex workers choose their clients and services. Characters may be close friends, rivals, or romantic interests outside work without turning fees, protection, lodging, or employment into personal entitlement.

The relay network carries ordinary letters and small consignments alongside goods whose owners want to avoid fees or attention. Marisol brokers freight; Eve manages a relay; Sadie knows routes; June occasionally rides for extra income. Each has a different understanding of the operation. Membership does not confer knowledge of every shipment or make every member an outlaw. Elias's accommodations are narrow, undocumented, and increasingly vulnerable to abuse.

### Work, money, food, and dependence

The local fictional economy uses dollars and cents, plus individually recorded tabs, invoices, and disputed company credit. Company credit is redeemable only at participating businesses and can leave a worker unable to pay a landlord who wants cash. A wage promise, a debt, and a gift are distinct records. Economic amounts below are game-world anchors for coherent dialogue, not historical purchasing-power research or real financial advice.

A plain meal usually costs 15–30 cents, a better supper 50–90 cents, and a simple boarding bed about 35–60 cents for a night. A private hotel room can run $1–2; a day of hired horse use roughly $1–3 depending on the animal and purpose. Ordinary day work often earns $1–2 in this fictional economy, while skilled or dangerous work can pay more. Prices vary by service and scarcity. They are not an automatic shopping subsystem, and character wealth never converts into Kivelli subscription or credit balances.

The rich have land, credit, access to transport, or control over a bottleneck. Comfortable tradespeople own tools and a reliable customer base. Wage workers can have good weeks and no cushion. Independence might mean owning a sewing machine, two sound horses, a small press, or a lease without a hostile renewal clause. Every character has a concrete idea of what enough would look like.

Meals keep the social world connected: bread and preserves, beans and stew, roasted meat when available, dairy products, seasonal produce, and recipes carried through different households. Alma experiments with supper dishes; Inés argues for better market variety; Beatrice keeps affordable bread available after more profitable orders. Food descriptions should belong to particular people and kitchens rather than representing an entire culture with one dish.

Labor continues behind the picturesque frontage. Water must be carried or piped, lamps cleaned, dishes washed, horses rested, repairs done, and clothing dried. The most useful invitations often begin with an ordinary task. Helping may create gratitude and familiarity. It does not automatically create debt, friendship, or romance.

### Faith, custom, class, and community

Jonah's chapel is a small Protestant congregation with a relief hall used more broadly than its services. Lucía and Inés carry Mexican family Catholic traditions into private life, though their observance differs. Esther maintains her Jewish identity and household practices without being assigned the job of educating everyone she meets. Other residents are devout, skeptical, irregularly observant, or simply attached to funerals, music, and shared meals. Faith influences choices; it does not reveal hidden facts.

Lena's Mescalero family connections extend beyond Calder's Run. Her routes, kinship, work, and loyalties belong to a living community with interests of its own. No sacred ceremony, invented translation, mystical racial ability, or confidential tribal knowledge is a purchasable attraction. The local history acknowledges dispossession and constrained choices without making every encounter a lesson or every Indigenous character an interpreter. Her guide work and attraction develop through her particular personality.

The community includes Black households, Mexican and Mexican-American families, Chinese workers and merchants, and migrants with several European backgrounds. The setting recognizes discrimination and unequal access through specific institutions and conflicts. Generated everyday dialogue does not default to slurs or compulsory harassment. People also joke, flirt, make music, cooperate, disagree, and lead ordinary complicated lives within and across these communities.

Respectability is contested. A church donor can be a cruel employer. A courtesan can be a reliable neighbor. A well-dressed arrival can be broke. Paid companionship, a previous marriage, unconventional dress, or public affection do not establish virtue or vice. Household labor and the practical consequences of gossip make reputation matter differently to different people.

Local habits are small and usable: a dry board is offered to someone arriving with wet boots; a borrowed tool is returned cleaned; ferry crews are allowed to eat before being asked for one more crossing; empty chairs are not assumed to be invitations. At community suppers, bringing something matters more than its cost. These customs can be broken, misunderstood, explained, or gently teased about. They should provide conversation, not a checklist of automatic punishments.

### Weather, movement, and the limits of access

Late summer is hot in exposed places and cooler by the river after dusk. A storm can turn a dry wash into a temporary obstacle and make a quiet crossing difficult. Dust affects workshops, washing, photographic work, and patience. High water changes ferry availability; heat changes work hours. Weather creates practical choices and altered meetings without becoming a daily disaster.

Within a district, most public places are several minutes apart. The west-bank center and River Ward connect by sloping streets. River crossings depend on the ferry until the bridge is completed in saved story state. A place on the opposite bank is never treated as the next room. Valley and bluff journeys require sufficient time and sometimes a horse, guide, or scheduled coach. The content pack supplies a travel matrix and arrival buffers for schedule transitions.

Business hours describe visitor access. Workers may prepare or close outside those hours when their schedule specifies authorized duty. Private rooms, workshops, mine workings, construction areas, and the dispensary's controlled stores have separate access. Seeing someone leave a location does not imply permission to follow home. A location's street-facing public space may remain visible when its service is closed.

The schoolhouse operates daytime teaching in the background. Companion interactions there use adult staff areas or the authored adult evening class. Young students are never companion records, participants in romantic scenes, or props for adult content. The adult cast ranges from 18 to 50; age is explicit and stable.

District geography and weather rules are practical foundations for story. They prevent a character from being at a remote claim, a court hearing, and a late supper at once. Cancelled plans are opportunities for believable repair: a note, an explanation, a new invitation, or a favor passed through a known intermediary.

### A voice for Calder’s Run

Use clear, original period-flavored English. The setting is 1888, but dialogue should be readable. Rhythm, subject matter, restraint, vocabulary, and personal habits carry the era more effectively than distorted spelling. Characters do not all call strangers partner, talk about whiskey, draw a weapon, or speak in proverbs. Education, occupation, household, and temperament create different voices.

Gin often offers a concrete option while making the listener comfortable enough to answer honestly. Tess distinguishes observation from assumption. June speaks quickly when interested and becomes more precise when her competence is questioned. Elias gives people room to correct themselves. Esther chooses the accurate word and can be funny about everyone else's impatience. Rosalie is socially bold and privately economical. Their voices should remain recognizable even when the subject is breakfast.

A reply starts from what the person is doing, what was said, what they know, and what they want. They can volunteer a thought, make a practical offer, tease, disagree, become distracted, or end a conversation because work calls. A question should be specific and useful. Several exchanges may pass without one. Characters are allowed to misunderstand; they should revise an opinion when new evidence reaches them.

Secrets have owners and discovery paths. An accusation stays an accusation until corroborated. A stranger cannot receive the town's entire history on arrival, and a friendly conversation does not reveal every private truth. Rumor may travel only through a plausible source. Group scenes apply knowledge and relationships to each participant separately; no character becomes a narrator for someone else's mind.

Spice affects expression and initiative after reciprocal interest. It does not determine intelligence, morality, occupation, or loyalty. A reserved person may be decisive and deeply passionate; a bold person may be shy about asking someone to stay for breakfast. Characters can pursue friendships, rivalries, and ordinary plans with the same depth as romance. Adult themes are part of the setting, and the authored bible expresses relationship dynamics without scripting graphic sexual scenes.

### Reputation, attachment, and continuing stories

Reputation is a set of relationships and communicated claims. The player can be trusted by the ferry crew, disliked by a ranch office, admired by theater workers, and unknown to a miner. A private act changes the people involved. A witness can share an account with a reason, audience, and degree of certainty. Public printing can spread a claim widely, but printed text is not automatically truth.

Rumors track what was alleged, who observed or heard it, how it reached a listener, and whether later evidence corrected it. A secret promised to one person is not globally stored as common knowledge. Reconciliation requires recognition of the specific harm and a change in behavior. Gifts cannot erase betrayal, and romance cannot compel a person to abandon work, beliefs, or friends.

Every relationship can develop through repeated meetings: recognition, familiar routines, earned trust, shared responsibility, attraction if mutual, and chosen commitment. Friendship has its own rewards. A new room key, a preferred seat, a borrowed instrument, a business introduction, or an invitation to a family meal can matter as much as a dramatic confession.

Story arcs have several endings and continuing consequences. Someone can leave temporarily, return from a tour, lose a contract, gain a partner, or alter their work schedule. The town remains usable afterward. The bridge can open without ending the ferry crew's personal stories; a failed business can lead to new work rather than silently deleting a character. Baseline state, each evidence item, and each unlocked change are defined separately.

The content is designed for conversations and location visits. It supplies opportunities to observe, join, ask, refuse, leave, negotiate, or investigate. It does not require a combat engine, a real-money gambling product, or a new numerical economy to tell its stories. Any eventual mechanics should preserve these authored relationships and the player's ability to decline.

### The houses on Lantern Row

The district contains several different businesses. Marigold House is Mabel’s established house of adult company and hospitality, with a long local history. The Ribbon Room is Pearl’s independent appointment salon. The Red Sash is a distinct brothel with a public parlor, separately agreed private appointment rooms, and resident housing. Its two named workers, Bess Kincaid and Sabine Roche, are unrelated colleagues. They retain individual control over appointments and personal earnings while sharing agreed house costs. Other staff remain background adults, not additional companion records.

The Red Sash’s ordinary life matters: rehearsal in the parlor, breakfast taken late, flowers that need changing, a dress repaired before work, a disagreement over the roof, and a resident who wants a free evening. Its lease creates a story about property and collective decision-making. Bess also wants to make audiences laugh; Sabine wants a life in which being dependable is one quality rather than her entire role. Either may enjoy her occupation and still want different terms. Their friendship can survive different choices about the house.

Personal romance develops through a separate, mutual invitation. Professional charm, an appointment, and a private relationship are three different facts. A character’s occupation does not assign her to the player, establish a sibling relationship, or require a story about being redeemed by leaving sex work. Public parlor scenes can be warm, flirtatious, theatrical, or contentious. Any sexual activity remains off page in this authoring bible.

### Beyond the old freight shelter

Crowcut Hollow occupies an abandoned loading shelter in a side canyon of Cinder Bluffs. The town can know there are bandits in the hills without knowing where their supper table stands. The hideout is a visitable story location, not an unlocked destination on the initial public map. Its image is domestic as well as dangerous: laundry near old freight boards, an uneven table, animals needing care, an argument that cannot be ended by walking into the next town street.

Cole Hensley and Silas Quade are the two named bandits. Silas leads a small background crew; Cole joined after railway work ended. Their shared grievance has reached a breaking point over stolen workers’ wages. Silas knew the consignment’s nature before the theft. Cole learned afterward and retained a receipt. Neither becomes harmless because his motives are understandable. The people harmed have names, bills, and independent judgments in the rest of the world.

The first approach can happen through Cole at the Old Calder Relay, without entering the hideout. An arranged meeting, voluntary disclosure, return of property, exposure, restitution, surrender, or refusal to help can move the story. No route requires the player to join the crew. Crowcut access is granted by saved story state and may later close for a stated reason. Cole and Silas can leave the location through authored transitions; a change in allegiance or legal status must update their schedules and public descriptions together.

### A week with room for ordinary pleasure

Recurring life gives a newcomer reasons to return even while the large disputes remain unresolved. Sunday supper gathers the River Ward. The market, reading circle, rehearsal, dance practice, forge meal, and Red Sash music evening each bring together people with different jobs and different levels of patience for being interrupted. A recurring event is an opportunity, not a demand that the same dramatic reveal repeat every week. A used clue stays known; a corrected account stays corrected.

Work continues when a named character takes a day away: background staff, a reduced service, or an appointment book can explain the difference. A place being open does not imply its named companion is present. Characters can travel, take a break, refuse a visitor, attend an event, or invite someone into a private room. Baseline schedules describe a saved starting week; plot milestones, agreed dates, weather, and specific appointments can produce explicit overrides.

The best small scenes leave space for interpretation. A second cup has been set out. Someone remembers the food the player disliked. A joke lands badly and can be repaired. Two friends disagree and still save one another a seat. The town does not need a new emergency every time a relationship grows.

### Timeline

| When | Turning point | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Before 1859 | Routes before the town | Indigenous movement, older Mexican households, small-scale cultivation, and local irrigation precede the freight settlement. Specific communities have continuing interests; the river is not empty land awaiting discovery. |
| 1859 | Calder’s Run | Amelia Calder organizes a reliable freight-and-ferry circuit. The route lends its name to the settlement; the ferry book begins as a practical business record. |
| 1864 | Silver in the bluffs | Commercial workings expand at what becomes Saint Agnes. Supply yards and modest lodging follow the workers. |
| 1868 | A permanent Main Street | Brick businesses replace several temporary fronts. A public reading room becomes the first court-record office. |
| 1872 | The high-water year | Flooding damages River Ward and destroys a portion of county records. Collective rebuilding creates both solidarity and disputed obligations. |
| 1874 | The crossing compact | A local trusteeship formalizes ferry maintenance, public headworks, and registered water turns. Emergency advantages become durable privileges. |
| 1877 | Lantern Row expands | A theater, larger saloon rooms, and independent adult businesses make evening trade a substantial employer. |
| 1881 | The Saint Agnes collapse | A shaft failure kills and injures workers. The miners’ mutual-aid fund forms; contemporary liability remains contested. |
| 1883 | The relay revival | Older freight routes return to use as private couriers exploit demand for cheaper and less scrutinized transport. |
| 1885 | The dry summer | Low water exposes inequalities in the allocation register. Small growers begin sharing copies of schedules and delivery receipts. |
| 1887 | Survey stakes | Railway surveying raises land prices and rental speculation before many affected tenants know the likely route. |
| 1888 | The east-bank terminus | The temporary depot opens. Bridge work begins under limited construction authority; the full franchise remains undecided. |

## Districts and travel

### Main Street

The west-bank commercial terrace, where the town puts on its public face and conducts the business everyone eventually needs.

**History:** Permanent brick arrived after the first silver profits. Shopkeepers widened the boardwalks while moving older stalls downhill. The courthouse occupies an expanded reading-room building, and its piecemeal construction is visible from the alley.

**Sensory anchors:** warm brick after sunset, horse tack and fresh bread, press machinery behind an open window

**Daily rhythm:** Freight and shopping dominate morning; court business and repairs fill afternoon; hotel balconies and supper visits soften the evening.

**Social fault line:** Public frontage suggests stability while rents and credit determine who can remain behind it.

**Local ritual:** Shop staff exchange small change and errands before the first coach arrival.

**Weather:** Dust gathers in doorways; runoff makes the lower steps slippery after rain.

**Story pressure:** A disputed public notice can connect the press, courthouse, marshal, and hotel without any one worker knowing the whole story.

**Places:** Calder House Hotel, Keene’s Print Shop, County Courthouse, Dempsey Livery, Crown & Cotton Mercantile, Hearthstone Bakery, Marshal’s Office, Soto Forge

### Lantern Row

The evening district, with music, good food, paid companionship, bathing, cards, and people whose labor lets others relax.

**History:** Temporary drinking rooms gave way to purpose-built businesses when miners began receiving regular pay. Adult establishments and theaters funded improvements that more respectable neighbors also use.

**Sensory anchors:** piano heard through two different walls, lamp oil and kitchen onions, washed linen in an upstairs corridor

**Daily rhythm:** Late mornings belong to staff and deliveries. Rehearsal and preparation grow into crowded evenings; closing-time meals create their own community.

**Social fault line:** The people who attract customers may have the least control over leases and working conditions.

**Local ritual:** After a difficult night, staff send a plate from the Night Kitchen to the business still closing.

**Weather:** Open windows cool crowded rooms; storms trap strangers together and dampen musical instruments.

**Story pressure:** A changed lease, a touring offer, and a lucrative patron divide people who usually protect one another.

**Places:** Copper Mare Saloon, Marigold House, Bellflower Theater, Night Kitchen, Junebug Dance Hall, Blue Glass Baths, Argosy Cardroom, Ribbon Room, The Red Sash

### River Ward

The old working slope to the river, full of shared services, boarding rooms, small trades, and accumulated memory.

**History:** The flood erased several streets but not their households. Rebuilt walls contain salvaged material; the headworks and ferry compact are part of family history.

**Sensory anchors:** damp rope, soap and sun-dried cloth, food carried down a boardinghouse stair

**Daily rhythm:** Early departures, clinic and market business, afternoon repairs, and communal supper create frequent encounters across occupations.

**Social fault line:** Improvement schemes describe the ward as inefficient while depending on its cheap rooms and labor.

**Local ritual:** People leave dry boards near doors for arrivals with wet boots.

**Weather:** High water directly changes work and travel; summer shade makes the ward a refuge from hotter streets.

**Story pressure:** A delayed ferry, unpaid wages, and a shortage at the clinic reveal whose needs a new transport system actually serves.

**Places:** Pike’s Ferry, Salcedo Clinic, Public Washhouse, Boatmen’s Boardinghouse, River Market, Riverside Chapel, Pike Boatworks, Cottonwood Dispensary

### The Railhead

The east-bank terminus, where temporary structures, demanding work, and speculative confidence compete to define the future.

**History:** Last year’s survey camp is already a depot, supply street, office compound, and growing settlement. Its residents resent being treated as visitors to the west-bank town.

**Sensory anchors:** coal smoke, fresh-cut timber, telegraph clicks through a thin wall

**Daily rhythm:** Dispatch and deliveries begin early. Survey and bridge crews move through daylight; correspondence and repairs continue after passenger activity slows.

**Social fault line:** The railway speaks as one institution while its employees have different wages, risks, and long-term prospects.

**Local ritual:** The day’s first passenger arrival is checked from the provisioner’s porch with mugs in hand.

**Weather:** Wind exposes temporary buildings; high water can strand workers away from friends and appointments.

**Story pressure:** An alternative survey route forces the people building the bridge to ask who gets to decide what progress costs.

**Places:** Temporary Passenger Depot, Telegraph Office, Survey Camp, Bridge Works, Railroad Freight Office, Calder Portrait Studio, Eastbank Provisioners

### Cottonwood Valley

The cultivated river corridor, with orchards, grazing, workshops, family homes, and disagreements that travel through irrigation channels.

**History:** Several patterns of cultivation precede the modern town. The water register overlays older practices imperfectly, and later ranch consolidation has never settled every claim.

**Sensory anchors:** cut grass beside dry earth, milk pails and shade cloth, moving water behind a locked gate

**Daily rhythm:** Animals and irrigation require early work; afternoons shift toward repair and household business; evening brings adult lessons, porch visits, and shared meals.

**Social fault line:** Local independence can conceal dependence on a neighbor’s gate, money, or permission.

**Local ritual:** At the first cool evening after a hot spell, households bring food to the meeting-house yard.

**Weather:** Heat changes tasks and swimming conditions; a distant storm can alter river access without rain falling locally.

**Story pressure:** A night water diversion can be an act of desperation, advantage, or both, and solving it does not by itself settle allocation.

**Places:** Whitcomb Ranch, Orchard Schoolhouse, Cottonwood Swimming Bend, Irrigation Meeting House, Valley Creamery, Orchard Glasshouse, Cattle Sale Yard

### Cinder Bluffs

The southern mining country and old road, with small claims, industrial workings, relay stations, and communities beyond easy supervision.

**History:** Commercial silver connected scattered routes and camps. The 1881 collapse left a mutual society, a cemetery expansion, and unresolved arguments about responsibility.

**Sensory anchors:** ore dust on cuffs, mules shifting under shade, wind through an abandoned loading frame

**Daily rhythm:** Mining and travel start early. Supply exchanges fill midday; late arrivals gather at stations and trading rooms before the roads become difficult.

**Social fault line:** Independence often depends on credit, an accurate assay, a working animal, or someone willing to carry a message.

**Local ritual:** A returned traveler is offered water before being asked for news.

**Weather:** Exposed routes become slow in heat and unsafe during sudden runoff; night travel requires an authored purpose.

**Story pressure:** A borrowed horse and disputed mineral sample can link the livery, couriers, miners, and distant households.

**Places:** Saint Agnes Mine, Assay Office, Widow’s Cut Coach Station, Mule Camp Trading Post, Red Wells Prospect, Canyon Rest Cemetery, Old Calder Relay, Crowcut Hollow

### Travel planning

Fictional dry-weather planning estimates: walking in the core, ordinary hired or owned transport for outlying trips; ferry time included where needed. Actual travel requires a suitable mode and access.

| From / to | Main Street | Lantern Row | River Ward | Railhead | Valley | Bluffs |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Main Street | 8 | 8 | 10 | 35 | 35 | 55 |
| Lantern Row | 8 | 8 | 12 | 37 | 40 | 58 |
| River Ward | 10 | 12 | 8 | 25 | 30 | 50 |
| The Railhead | 35 | 37 | 25 | 10 | 50 | 75 |
| Cottonwood Valley | 35 | 40 | 30 | 50 | 20 | 85 |
| Cinder Bluffs | 55 | 58 | 50 | 75 | 85 | 20 |

- The unfinished bridge is not a baseline crossing.
- A ferry closure invalidates affected trips and requires a saved alternative or cancellation.
- A schedule interval contains a leading travel period; presence begins after arrival.
- Crowcut route details are not on the public map and require authored discovery.

The diagonal is a typical move between two distinct places within a district. Staying at the same place takes no travel time. Distances are authored planning assumptions; a date or scene must still allow the particular character to make the journey.

## Factions

### Arroyo & Western Railway

**Seat:** Railroad Freight Office

**Associated characters:** Tess Corbett, Hazel Whitcomb, Daniel Chen, Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks, Thomas “Tom” Archer, Victor Halden

**Public aim:** Complete a profitable through-route and establish reliable services.

**Resources:** Capital, freight contracts, surveying, outside suppliers, and access to investors.

**Internal disagreement:** Workers and engineers can support construction while opposing land speculation or labor practices.

**Private pressure:** The investors want dependable earnings before authorizing the expensive alternative approach.

**What involvement offers:** Employment, introductions, transport, supplies, and a credible future for east-bank businesses.

**What it can cost:** Dependence on a company able to change rates, terminate contracts, or bypass local suppliers.

### Valley Water Association

**Seat:** Irrigation Meeting House

**Associated characters:** Adelaide Whitcomb, Inés Salcedo, Lena Velarde, Daisy Cartwright, Florence “Flora” Vale, Mateo Varela, Isaac Booker

**Public aim:** Keep water usable and local agriculture viable.

**Resources:** Turn registers, headworks crews, cultivated land, livestock, and council influence.

**Internal disagreement:** Established holdings receive advantages that smaller growers want revised.

**Private pressure:** A bad season will expose members who promised more water than they can deliver.

**What involvement offers:** Local knowledge, work, household introductions, and support for independent ventures.

**What it can cost:** Pressure to defend neighbors even when their claims are unfair.

### Saint Agnes Mutual Society

**Seat:** Saint Agnes Mine

**Associated characters:** Ruth Freeman, Agnes Reed, Vera Sutter, Marisol Duarte, Kit Calloway, Benjamin “Ben” Crowell, August Renn

**Public aim:** Honest weighing, timely wages, practical safety, and relief for affected households.

**Resources:** Dues, work records, collective labor, supply relationships, and neighborhood trust.

**Internal disagreement:** Some members favor accommodation to preserve jobs; others believe delay costs lives.

**Private pressure:** The relief fund cannot absorb another long interruption of wages.

**What involvement offers:** Solidarity, useful work, testimony, and access to a community that notices who helps.

**What it can cost:** Public association can strain business ties and expose internal disagreements.

### Town Compact and Civic Offices

**Seat:** County Courthouse

**Associated characters:** Iris Keene, Adelaide Whitcomb, Mabel Arnett, Nell Hewitt, Esther Wynn, Eliza Morrow, Marshal Elias Reddick, Reverend Jonah Bell

**Public aim:** Keep the settlement governable and preserve public services.

**Resources:** Records, public hearings, notices, licenses, relief work, and limited law enforcement.

**Internal disagreement:** Order, fairness, prosperity, and institutional survival point toward different choices.

**Private pressure:** Several officials rely on informal exceptions they would struggle to defend publicly.

**What involvement offers:** Legitimate procedures, introductions, protection of records, and public recognition.

**What it can cost:** Working through institutions means delay, compromise, and accountability.

### Lantern Business Circle

**Seat:** Copper Mare Saloon

**Associated characters:** Virginia “Gin” Maddox, Rosalie Quinn, Mabel Arnett, Alma Baptiste, Cora Dunleavy, Edith Kovač, Pearl Merritt, Lottie Fenwick, Clara Chen, Felix Avery, Rafael Ortiz, Bess Kincaid, Sabine Roche

**Public aim:** Keep the evening economy independent, solvent, and attractive.

**Resources:** Customers, performance, hospitality, private rooms, and information from voluntary conversation.

**Internal disagreement:** Owners, employees, independent workers, and landlords have different ideas of a fair bargain.

**Private pressure:** Rising rents could dismantle profitable businesses without reducing demand for their work.

**What involvement offers:** Warmth, friendship, employment, introductions, and a place to build a social life.

**What it can cost:** Loyalty can mean taking sides in a friend’s dispute or losing a lucrative patron.

### Old Road Carriers

**Seat:** Old Calder Relay

**Associated characters:** June Dempsey, Lena Velarde, Sadie Rusk, Marisol Duarte, Kit Calloway, Evelyn “Eve” Mercer, Gabriel Soto, Caleb Pike

**Public aim:** Keep goods and people moving beyond the expensive main crossing.

**Resources:** Riders, animals, caches, repair skills, alternate routes, and household trust.

**Internal disagreement:** Ordinary delivery, fee evasion, and serious theft are different undertakings that some brokers blur.

**Private pressure:** An increasingly valuable sealed consignment threatens the network’s narrow tolerances.

**What involvement offers:** Work, mobility, practical assistance, and a chance to help people bypass a bottleneck.

**What it can cost:** Incomplete information, disputed cargo ownership, and exposure to accusations by association.

### Crowcut Crew

**Seat:** Crowcut Hollow

**Associated characters:** Cole Hensley, Silas Quade

**Public aim:** Survive outside the established freight economy and preserve the crew’s independence.

**Resources:** Shared shelter, ordinary transport experience, personal loyalty, and disputed property.

**Internal disagreement:** Cole’s remorse over the payroll theft conflicts with Silas’s demand that loyalty protect the crew’s chosen account.

**Private pressure:** The retained receipt and Silas’s own note establish different knowledge at different times.

**What involvement offers:** A morally difficult relationship, a chance to hear an unpolished account of railway work, and an opportunity to support restitution or withdrawal.

**What it can cost:** Association creates mistrust; aid involving disputed property may deepen harm. The player may decline involvement.

## Places

Every member of the original 45-person core has a distinct primary place. The Red Sash adds two workers at one brothel; Crowcut Hollow adds two bandits at one hideout. Regulars are social connections, not guaranteed simultaneous occupants.

### P01 · Calder House Hotel

**District / category:** Main Street / hotel

**Primary companions:** Rafael Ortiz

**Regulars:** Rafael Ortiz, Iris Keene, Victor Halden

**Hours:** 00:00–24:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A three-story brick hotel with a shaded balcony, dependable meals, and a lobby where visitors learn how quickly an arrival becomes public news.

**History:** The hotel absorbed two older boarding buildings after the 1872 flood. Its respectable front conceals mismatched floors and an inexpensive staff wing. The current management rents rooms to railway representatives, traveling performers, and locals between leases.

**Layout:** The front desk faces the street doors. A public dining room opens to a side courtyard; a narrow service stair reaches staff rooms. Guest corridors and the register behind the desk are private.

**Social texture:** Breakfast brings departures and account disputes. Afternoons belong to letters and quiet waiting. Evening balconies host conversation, while Rafael controls the practical business of late arrivals.

**Author-only private truth:** Rafael has quietly reserved one low-priced room for stranded travelers using gratuities of his own. Management has not authorized a permanent relief arrangement.

**Activities:** lodging, supper, balcony conversation, arrival assistance

**Visual anchors:** brick balcony, green shutters, worn register, courtyard washstand

**House etiquette:** A guest name is not public entertainment. Ask before entering a corridor or joining someone on a balcony. Staff assistance does not purchase private attention.

**Story seeds:**

- An investor wants a familiar tenant displaced before a private meeting.
- A room reservation becomes a choice between a profitable guest and someone with nowhere to go.

### P02 · Keene’s Print Shop

**District / category:** Main Street / print shop

**Primary companions:** Iris Keene

**Regulars:** Iris Keene, Esther Wynn, Clara Chen

**Hours:** 08:00–20:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A working print shop publishing The Run Ledger, selling stationery, and turning private grievances into carefully chosen public words.

**History:** Iris purchased a damaged press with savings and an equipment loan. The shop’s older job-printing business pays for a newspaper whose independence is admired more often than it is financially supported.

**Layout:** A customer counter separates the shopfront from type cases, a hand-fed press, and a drying room. A rear desk holds drafts and correspondence; proofs may be pinned publicly only when marked for release.

**Social texture:** Morning customers need notices, menus, and invoices. The press is loud in the afternoon. Just before publication, a casual visitor can become either useful company or an unwelcome distraction.

**Author-only private truth:** A local patron offered to clear Iris’s equipment debt in exchange for advance sight of articles. The proposal remains unanswered in her private correspondence.

**Activities:** printing, news discussion, notice submission, proofreading by invitation

**Visual anchors:** ink-black fingertips, lead type cases, drying sheets, patched press belt

**House etiquette:** Do not touch type or lift private proofs. Published assertions can be questioned; unpublished material requires Iris’s permission.

**Story seeds:**

- A damaging accusation arrives without evidence shortly before printing.
- An innocent correction reveals two versions of a land notice.

### P03 · County Courthouse

**District / category:** Main Street / courthouse

**Primary companions:** Esther Wynn

**Regulars:** Esther Wynn, Marshal Elias Reddick, Adelaide Whitcomb

**Hours:** 08:00–18:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The county’s modest record office and hearing rooms, where property, inheritance, and public promises acquire forms that outlast the people arguing over them.

**History:** A reading room became a courthouse as the town grew. Reconstruction after the flood left an uneven archive: some volumes survived, some were copied, and some entries remain disputed.

**Layout:** A public counter and notice board lead to a hearing room. Copy desks occupy a bright side room. The archive cage, evidence cupboard, and officials’ offices require specific access.

**Social texture:** Mornings are procedural and crowded; afternoons slow into document work. Adult evening petition clinics happen only on authored dates, with Esther or a supervising official present.

**Author-only private truth:** A copied water-register page combines measurements from different years. Its existence is genuine; whether its use was fraudulent requires comparison with other evidence.

**Activities:** record requests, public hearings, petition assistance, archive research

**Visual anchors:** sunlit copy desk, uneven brick addition, locked iron cage, crowded notice board

**House etiquette:** Public records and sealed records have different access. Clerks can explain process but cannot promise a ruling or create legal authority.

**Story seeds:**

- A claimant needs an accurate copy before a hearing.
- A missing page is less revealing than the filing date of the page replacing it.

### P04 · Dempsey Livery

**District / category:** Main Street / livery stable

**Primary companions:** June Dempsey

**Regulars:** June Dempsey, Gabriel Soto, Sadie Rusk

**Hours:** 05:00–21:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A busy livery and training yard where the town hires transport, boards animals, swaps practical news, and learns whether a newcomer can be trusted with a horse.

**History:** June’s family built the yard around coach traffic. Growing debt and the railway’s arrival have made its future uncertain, but its repair contacts and sound animals remain indispensable.

**Layout:** The hire counter faces a covered yard. Training pens, tack room, feed loft, and staff cottage sit beyond. Visitors wait at the rail until invited into an animal’s space.

**Social texture:** Dawn departures and evening returns are crowded. Midday offers training, grooming, and quieter conversation. A delayed return can disturb several people’s plans.

**Author-only private truth:** A recently accepted boarding animal matches a disputed bill of sale. June knows the paperwork is weak but has not established who is telling the truth.

**Activities:** horse hire, riding lessons, grooming, travel planning

**Visual anchors:** dusty training rail, labeled tack hooks, hay loft, water trough

**House etiquette:** Animal welfare governs access and timing. Borrowing requires a specific agreement; being friendly with June does not make every horse available.

**Story seeds:**

- A borrowed horse is recognized by someone who claims it was stolen.
- A novice rider needs help before an important visit.

### P05 · Crown & Cotton Mercantile

**District / category:** Main Street / general store

**Primary companions:** Lottie Fenwick

**Regulars:** Lottie Fenwick, Hazel Whitcomb, Edith Kovač

**Hours:** 07:00–20:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The mercantile supplies everything from nails and lamp wicks to fabric, with a stockroom whose contents reveal the town’s changing ambitions.

**History:** The store began as a freight counter and expanded with the silver trade. Its proprietor relies increasingly on Lottie’s purchasing judgment while continuing to call her an assistant when ownership is discussed.

**Layout:** Public aisles lead past hardware and dry goods to a fabric counter. A scale and invoice desk mark the boundary of the stockroom. The upstairs rooms are private storage and household space.

**Social texture:** Working customers arrive early; browsing and household shopping fill the afternoon. Near closing, Lottie rearranges displays and weighs what people asked for against what the owner will buy.

**Author-only private truth:** Lottie has designed a buying list for a small independent shop. She is using public catalog prices, not stealing the store’s accounts.

**Activities:** shopping, fabric selection, ordering supplies, parcel collection

**Visual anchors:** fabric bolts, brass scale, hanging lanterns, scuffed freight counter

**House etiquette:** A store tab is a financial arrangement, not social ownership. Ask before opening stock drawers or reading an invoice.

**Story seeds:**

- A scarce delivery is promised to two customers.
- A bold display succeeds, and someone else takes the credit.

### P06 · Hearthstone Bakery

**District / category:** Main Street / bakery

**Primary companions:** Beatrice Bell

**Regulars:** Beatrice Bell, Agnes Reed, Reverend Jonah Bell

**Hours:** 05:00–18:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A warm bakery serving ordinary bread, occasional elaborate pastries, and a few tables whose regulars know the rhythms of the oven.

**History:** The business survived the dry summer by shortening its menu and sharing deliveries. Beatrice learned the practical cost of wasting a batch and now balances affordable staples with higher-margin commissions.

**Layout:** A front counter and two small tables overlook the boardwalk. The oven room, mixing bench, and ingredient store sit behind a half door. Work space is tight and hot.

**Social texture:** The first hours are brisk with workers collecting breakfast. Afternoons grow companionable as the last loaves cool. A large special order can change the whole day.

**Author-only private truth:** Beatrice keeps a small, openly documented reduced-price shelf after the main rush. She is ashamed that she sometimes resents customers who assume the reduction is endless.

**Activities:** breakfast, bread buying, pastry orders, kitchen conversation by invitation

**Visual anchors:** flour-dusted counter, brick oven, window cooling rack, blue enamel pitcher

**House etiquette:** The kitchen requires permission. Help is useful when offered concretely, and a gift should not expose a recipient’s hardship.

**Story seeds:**

- A celebration order conflicts with supplying the boardinghouse.
- Someone anonymously pays for another household’s weekly bread.

### P07 · Marshal’s Office

**District / category:** Main Street / law office

**Primary companions:** Marshal Elias Reddick

**Regulars:** Marshal Elias Reddick, Esther Wynn, Evelyn “Eve” Mercer

**Hours:** 00:00–24:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The marshal’s small office combines public service, uncomfortable waiting, local records, and the persistent problem of deciding which trouble cannot wait.

**History:** Its cells were added after a violent payday dispute. Elias has spent years making the public room feel ordinary enough that residents report a problem before it becomes a confrontation.

**Layout:** A desk and benches occupy the front. A record cupboard, small interview room, and separate holding area are restricted. The back yard is used for maintenance and authorized transfers.

**Social texture:** Morning reports give way to patrols and appointments. Another officer may staff the desk when Elias is away, but background staff do not acquire a new named companion identity.

**Author-only private truth:** Elias has tolerated limited fee-evasion deliveries when household necessity was involved. His notes distinguish those cases, but the arrangement was never formally approved.

**Activities:** reporting a problem, public notices, lawful appointments, found-property claims

**Visual anchors:** scarred desk, repair tools by a window, holding-room door, pinned notices

**House etiquette:** A notice is not proof of guilt. Restricted records and holding areas require actual authority; a personal relationship does not provide it.

**Story seeds:**

- An accusation relies on a witness who saw only part of an event.
- A formerly harmless exception is invoked to excuse a serious theft.

### P08 · Soto Forge

**District / category:** Main Street / forge and repair yard

**Primary companions:** Gabriel Soto

**Regulars:** Gabriel Soto, June Dempsey, Caleb Pike

**Hours:** 07:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A forge and wagon yard where hot metal, measured workmanship, and friendly argument keep the town’s animals and vehicles moving.

**History:** Gabriel acquired tools gradually and rents the yard. He has more demand than space, yet a move would mean leaving customers who can walk to the present shop and pay in small installments.

**Layout:** The public yard contains a waiting rail and repair wagons. A forge bay, tool wall, and small office sit under a broad roof. Hot work zones are kept clear.

**Social texture:** Morning jobs are organized around animals and departures. Afternoon repairs allow longer talk between noisy stretches. Friends gather after the fires are banked.

**Author-only private truth:** Gabriel has priced a new workshop near the railhead but has not told June because she would read the move as a judgment on the livery’s future.

**Activities:** wagon repair, farrier work, tool discussion, after-work meals

**Visual anchors:** forge glow, hanging horseshoes, half-repaired wagon, shared outdoor table

**House etiquette:** Wait for Gabriel to make work safe before approaching. Repairs create practical records, not automatic knowledge of a customer’s purpose.

**Story seeds:**

- A repair mark can identify a wagon seen on an old road.
- An attractive workshop offer would leave longstanding customers without affordable help.

### P09 · Copper Mare Saloon

**District / category:** Lantern Row / saloon

**Primary companions:** Virginia “Gin” Maddox

**Regulars:** Virginia “Gin” Maddox, Rosalie Quinn, Marshal Elias Reddick

**Hours:** 12:00–02:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The Copper Mare is a generous, noisy saloon with good music, ordinary food, and a back room where people who dislike one another sometimes manage to talk.

**History:** Gin turned an unreliable drinking room into a business through consistent service and staff loyalty. The landlord now values the location partly because her work made it desirable.

**Layout:** The main bar opens to tables and a small music platform. A courtyard catches evening air. The back meeting room and upstairs lodgings require invitation; the office contains private business records.

**Social texture:** Afternoon trade is relaxed and conversational. Evenings draw mixed company. Closing is work: counting, cleaning, escorting staff, and settling disagreements without turning them into a spectacle.

**Author-only private truth:** Gin can purchase the building only with outside money. Halden’s associate wants approval over private bookings in return for favorable financing.

**Activities:** supper, music, conversation, private meetings by arrangement

**Visual anchors:** copper horse above the bar, worn piano, courtyard lanterns, polished but repaired tables

**House etiquette:** Staff can refuse service. A drink buys a drink. Joining a table or entering the back room requires an invitation.

**Story seeds:**

- A meeting booking forces Gin to choose between money and the room’s reputation.
- A musician’s departure threatens a valued nightly routine.

### P10 · Marigold House

**District / category:** Lantern Row / adult companionship house

**Primary companions:** Mabel Arnett

**Regulars:** Mabel Arnett, Pearl Merritt, Edith Kovač

**Hours:** 15:00–02:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

Marigold House is an adult companionship business with a gracious public parlor, experienced management, and workers whose private lives extend well beyond the building.

**History:** Mabel purchased the original house after years of managing other people’s establishments. She has paid for repairs and supported local improvements while still being treated as a temporary convenience by some officials.

**Layout:** A reception parlor and music room are public during service hours. Separate consultation rooms and private suites require a specific booking and the worker’s agreement. Staff rooms have no public access.

**Social texture:** Deliveries and household work occupy the day. Evenings vary from sociable company to private appointments. Workers can decline bookings or leave early without the story turning that choice into a mystery.

**Author-only private truth:** A redevelopment prospectus omits the cost of relocating current workers. Mabel has kept copies while seeking a long lease that protects their independence.

**Activities:** adult social company, music, private appointments, business negotiation

**Visual anchors:** marigold-painted door, upright piano, embroidered lamp shades, well-tended courtyard

**House etiquette:** All workers and eligible guests in adult scenes are adults. Services, company, lodging, and personal relationships are separate choices; no payment establishes consent to anything else.

**Story seeds:**

- A patron publicly supports eviction while privately asking for favors.
- A worker’s new opportunity creates a practical disagreement about the house’s future.

### P11 · Bellflower Theater

**District / category:** Lantern Row / theater

**Primary companions:** Rosalie Quinn

**Regulars:** Rosalie Quinn, Cora Dunleavy, Felix Avery

**Hours:** 12:00–24:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A small theater where professional ambition and community entertainment share costumes, limited money, and the hope of a full room.

**History:** The Bellflower grew from a rented hall into a fixed stage. Touring companies improved its reputation; local performers now want work that lasts beyond the next visiting attraction.

**Layout:** A box office leads into a modest auditorium and stage. Rehearsal space, dressing rooms, and the costume loft are backstage and private unless invited.

**Social texture:** Afternoons bring rehearsal and repairs. Evenings alternate performances, amateur nights, and quiet closures. After a show, performers may want food, company, or solitude.

**Author-only private truth:** Rosalie has received a touring offer that would take her away just as a local company plan becomes financially possible.

**Activities:** performances, rehearsal visits, music, community productions

**Visual anchors:** painted stage flats, patched velvet curtain, gasless oil footlights, crowded costume loft

**House etiquette:** Buying a ticket grants audience access. Dressing rooms and rehearsal interruptions require permission; applause creates no personal obligation.

**Story seeds:**

- A good offer tests what support between friends actually means.
- A damaged costume reveals an unpaid debt between productions.

### P12 · Night Kitchen

**District / category:** Lantern Row / restaurant

**Primary companions:** Alma Baptiste

**Regulars:** Alma Baptiste, Virginia “Gin” Maddox, Rafael Ortiz

**Hours:** 16:00–03:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The Night Kitchen feeds the town when its evening workers finally have time to sit down, with a long table that becomes a second living room.

**History:** Alma took over the late menu after a series of indifferent cooks. Her food made the business dependable, and she is negotiating a real partnership rather than an endless promise of one.

**Layout:** A street counter opens to a compact dining room and shared table. The kitchen and stores are staff areas. A back step provides a brief place to cool down.

**Social texture:** Preparation begins while the street is quiet. Performers and bathhouse workers eat before shifts; a second rush follows closing elsewhere. Alma has little patience for an elaborate conversation during service.

**Author-only private truth:** Her partnership offer assigns responsibility for debts without matching control over purchasing. She understands the problem and is looking for a workable counteroffer.

**Activities:** late supper, staff meals, recipe discussion, shared-table conversation

**Visual anchors:** long communal table, steaming pots, chalkless handwritten menu, open back step

**House etiquette:** The table is communal; a person’s attention is not. Offer help without entering a crowded kitchen unasked.

**Story seeds:**

- A crowded supper brings two former friends to the same table.
- A supply shortage gives a young cook a chance to prove an unpopular idea.

### P13 · Junebug Dance Hall

**District / category:** Lantern Row / dance hall

**Primary companions:** Cora Dunleavy

**Regulars:** Cora Dunleavy, Rosalie Quinn, Isaac Booker

**Hours:** 16:00–01:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A music hall where callers, musicians, and dancers negotiate crowded floors, uneven skill, and the pleasure of being seen enjoying themselves.

**History:** The Junebug began as a barnlike rental hall. Regular dances made it viable, but the owner still spends more readily on decoration than on reliable wages for performers.

**Layout:** The floor is open beneath a high timber roof. Musicians use a raised corner; benches and a refreshment counter line the walls. A staff room holds personal belongings.

**Social texture:** Practice and setup occupy late afternoon. Early evening is easiest for beginners, while later sets draw confident regulars. Sunday closures provide time for cleaning and private rehearsal.

**Author-only private truth:** Cora has been promised a permanent caller’s share of takings, yet the agreement exists only in letters she has not shown anyone.

**Activities:** dancing, lessons, live music, social evenings

**Visual anchors:** scuffed dance floor, bright cloth bunting, fiddle corner, wall benches

**House etiquette:** A dance invitation can be declined without explanation. Partners check comfort and space; confidence is not permission to pull someone onto the floor.

**Story seeds:**

- A beginner’s lesson becomes a friendship outside the hall.
- A successful event forces a dispute over who earned its proceeds.

### P14 · Blue Glass Baths

**District / category:** Lantern Row / bathhouse

**Primary companions:** Edith Kovač

**Regulars:** Edith Kovač, Ruth Freeman, Mabel Arnett

**Hours:** 10:00–24:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A bathhouse providing washing, quiet recovery, and a rare interval when residents can be comfortable without performing competence for their employers.

**History:** Blue Glass replaced a makeshift washing business with plumbing that is useful but temperamental. Edith’s careful accounts and maintenance reports have made her essential without yet giving her a share.

**Layout:** Reception leads to separate bookable washing areas and screened resting spaces. Staff control the boiler, linen store, and maintenance access. Privacy boundaries are physical and explicit.

**Social texture:** Late mornings are quiet; afternoon bookings follow field work, and evening demand follows performances and travel. A failed valve can rearrange several people’s plans.

**Author-only private truth:** Maintenance money has been diverted to decorative improvements. Edith has receipts establishing the decision, but not proof of the owner’s alleged other misconduct.

**Activities:** bathing, rest, linen service, private conversation in booked space

**Visual anchors:** blue glass transoms, copper pipes, linen shelves, screened resting alcoves

**House etiquette:** Ordinary bathing is not an invitation to sexual attention. Private bookings and staff areas require appropriate access; found belongings are returned discreetly.

**Story seeds:**

- A plumbing failure makes a hidden management disagreement public.
- Someone leaves a personal letter in a returned linen bundle.

### P15 · Argosy Cardroom

**District / category:** Lantern Row / cardroom

**Primary companions:** Felix Avery

**Regulars:** Felix Avery, Virginia “Gin” Maddox, Victor Halden

**Hours:** 14:00–02:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A gaming room known for controlled manners, attentive dealers, and the distinction between enjoying a risk and pretending consequences do not exist.

**History:** The Argosy separated from a rougher saloon to attract steady customers. Felix’s ability to keep a table sociable is central to its reputation; his personal finances are less orderly.

**Layout:** A public lounge leads to staffed card tables. A cashier’s desk and secure store are restricted. A rear room is used for booked private games under house rules.

**Social texture:** Afternoons are slow and conversational. Evening games gather regulars and arrivals. Felix is usually working when others assume he is available to amuse them.

**Author-only private truth:** Felix owes a personal debt that he has concealed from his partner. The house accounts do not currently show theft or a rigged game.

**Activities:** social card play, spectating, refreshments, conversation between games

**Visual anchors:** green cloth tables, well-used cards, brass cashier grille, low hanging lamps

**House etiquette:** House games are fictional story activity; no real-money wagering is supplied. Respect a refusal to play, lend money, or continue a conversation.

**Story seeds:**

- A player tries to use a personal debt to influence a dealer.
- A disputed game turns on what witnesses actually observed.

### P16 · Ribbon Room

**District / category:** Lantern Row / adult private salon

**Primary companions:** Pearl Merritt

**Regulars:** Pearl Merritt, Mabel Arnett, Clara Chen

**Hours:** 16:00–24:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

An intimate adult salon where Pearl hosts private company, small dinners, and occasional readings for people who prefer a quieter social room.

**History:** Pearl leases the rooms independently and shares referrals with Marigold House. Her growing clientele has made the arrangement viable, but renewal terms could undermine that independence.

**Layout:** A reception room leads to a dining salon and individually private rooms. Pearl’s living quarters and business correspondence remain separate from booked space.

**Social texture:** Daytime belongs to errands and preparation. Evening appointments are deliberate and limited; a closed door is a boundary, not an invitation to invent a clandestine scene.

**Author-only private truth:** Pearl is saving toward a lease with a purchase option. A prospective guarantor expects to be treated as a permanent personal favorite.

**Activities:** adult social appointments, small dinners, readings, private conversation

**Visual anchors:** ribbon-trimmed curtains, small polished dining table, flower bowl, separate private door

**House etiquette:** Every appointment is separately agreed. Pearl’s paid work does not establish her personal romantic availability, and guests may leave or decline without owing affection.

**Story seeds:**

- An attractive guarantee carries an unacceptable private expectation.
- A quiet dinner allows two people to correct a public misunderstanding.

### P17 · Pike’s Ferry

**District / category:** River Ward / ferry crossing

**Primary companions:** Samuel Pike

**Regulars:** Samuel Pike, Caleb Pike, Lena Velarde

**Hours:** 05:00–22:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The working ferry joins the town’s banks and makes a journey into a brief shared experience of waiting, weather, and other people’s business.

**History:** Samuel’s crew maintained the service through lean years and helped rebuild it after high water. The crossing compact recognizes their work, but the bridge proposal treats their future as a minor cost.

**Layout:** West and east landings share a scheduled service. Waiting rails, a ticket shed, rope gear, and loading ramps organize access. The boat and working gear require crew direction.

**Social texture:** Early commuters are impatient, midday customers linger, and evening crossings mix workers with social visitors. High water can reduce or suspend service without changing the canonical river route.

**Author-only private truth:** Samuel has a realistic plan to retain a smaller passenger and leisure service after the bridge, but pride has kept him from discussing it with Caleb.

**Activities:** crossing the river, waiting conversation, river observation, crew work by agreement

**Visual anchors:** worn loading ramps, rope drums, ticket shed, cottonwood reflection

**House etiquette:** The pilot controls boarding. A familiar face does not overrule river conditions or the needs of other passengers.

**Story seeds:**

- A missed crossing complicates a promised meeting.
- A compensation proposal divides the crew between retirement, new work, and continued service.

### P18 · Salcedo Clinic

**District / category:** River Ward / clinic

**Primary companions:** Dr. Lucía Salcedo

**Regulars:** Dr. Lucía Salcedo, Nell Hewitt, Inés Salcedo

**Hours:** 08:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A small clinic where dependable care, crowded hours, and household realities turn abstract town disputes into specific human needs.

**History:** Lucía built the practice through house calls and modest fees. The clinic expanded into a courtyard room as patients and local donors supplied money, furniture, and labor.

**Layout:** A waiting room opens to consultation rooms and a private courtyard. Records, medicine stores, and treatment rooms require appropriate permission. The nearby dispensary is a separate workplace.

**Social texture:** Routine visits occupy daylight, with urgent work interrupting plans when an authored event calls for it. Lucía’s presence must be checked rather than assumed from an open front door.

**Author-only private truth:** A supplier is offering favorable transport rates tied to exclusive purchasing. Accepting would stabilize deliveries while reducing Lucía’s future choices.

**Activities:** appointments, supply deliveries, community health discussion, courtyard conversation off duty

**Visual anchors:** shaded waiting benches, wash basin, labeled cupboards, planted courtyard

**House etiquette:** Patient information remains private. Fictional clinical scenes offer character and story context rather than medical instructions; work and romance are kept distinct.

**Story seeds:**

- A transport delay exposes the cost of an unreliable crossing.
- A patient wants privacy from a well-intentioned relative.

### P19 · Public Washhouse

**District / category:** River Ward / washhouse

**Primary companions:** Ruth Freeman

**Regulars:** Ruth Freeman, Edith Kovač, Agnes Reed

**Hours:** 06:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A cooperative washhouse where heavy physical work, exact bookkeeping, and conversation keep half the town present without any one person knowing every secret.

**History:** Workers pooled funds after a landlord raised fees. Ruth now helps maintain the cooperative records while the older members decide how much authority to trust her with.

**Layout:** Public receiving counters lead to staffed washing and drying areas. Private collection shelves are labeled; a meeting corner doubles as a place for workers to rest.

**Social texture:** The early rush gives way to washing, drying, and afternoon collection. Rain changes the whole workload. Conversations happen during pauses, not while a worker must handle every task at once.

**Author-only private truth:** The cooperative is owed several substantial hotel accounts. Ruth wants to publish arrears to members; others fear losing the clients entirely.

**Activities:** laundry service, cooperative meetings, deliveries, rest-break conversation

**Visual anchors:** steaming tubs, long drying lines, collection shelves, communal accounts book

**House etiquette:** Laundry is not evidence of a person’s intimate life. Do not inspect private bundles or treat workers as automatic informants.

**Story seeds:**

- A hotel’s unpaid bill threatens several households.
- A misplaced item requires discretion rather than speculation.

### P20 · Boatmen’s Boardinghouse

**District / category:** River Ward / boardinghouse

**Primary companions:** Agnes Reed

**Regulars:** Agnes Reed, Ruth Freeman, Benjamin “Ben” Crowell

**Hours:** 05:00–23:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A boardinghouse offering solid meals, modest rooms, and a household rhythm that gives working residents somewhere to return to.

**History:** Agnes rebuilt her livelihood after widowhood by renting rooms and feeding travelers. Over time she made the house dependable enough that employers recommend it and workers bring friends.

**Layout:** A common dining room and sitting porch are public to guests and invited visitors. Bedrooms are private; kitchen and account desk are work spaces. Separate rooms are individually assigned.

**Social texture:** Breakfast is quick, supper communal, and late arrivals quiet. Agnes notices an absence but does not know its cause until someone tells her.

**Author-only private truth:** She has delayed several evictions by drawing on her own repair savings. Her generosity has a limit she finds painful to name.

**Activities:** lodging, shared meals, porch visits, household errands

**Visual anchors:** long supper table, repaired stair rail, porch rocking chair, numbered room keys

**House etiquette:** Being a guest does not grant access to another resident’s room. Help with expenses should preserve dignity and clear terms.

**Story seeds:**

- A wage delay becomes a household problem.
- A departing boarder leaves a useful possession with conflicting instructions.

### P21 · River Market

**District / category:** River Ward / market

**Primary companions:** Inés Salcedo

**Regulars:** Inés Salcedo, Alma Baptiste, Florence “Flora” Vale

**Hours:** 06:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A riverside market where local growers, household cooks, and travelers negotiate prices alongside recipes, favors, and news.

**History:** Stalls returned quickly after the flood, often in different positions. Current rents and access lanes reflect compromises that longtime sellers remember differently.

**Layout:** A central aisle runs between produce stalls and covered service counters. River steps and loading space stay clear. Individual stalls have storage that remains private.

**Social texture:** Early buying is practical; late morning becomes social. Afternoons are quieter and can bring discounts, spoiled stock, or arguments about who received the best shipment.

**Author-only private truth:** Inés has collected evidence of unequal stall charges. Some differences have ordinary explanations; others appear to favor people close to the association.

**Activities:** shopping, produce selling, food discussion, public gathering

**Visual anchors:** striped shade cloth, wooden produce crates, river steps, hanging scales
