**House etiquette:** A seller’s friendliness is part of a public interaction, not proof of private interest. Ask before handling produce or moving a stall’s goods.

**Story seeds:**

- Two sellers organize a fairer allocation of space.
- An unusual fruit delivery creates a small festival and a business risk.

### P22 · Riverside Chapel

**District / category:** River Ward / chapel and relief hall

**Primary companions:** Reverend Jonah Bell

**Regulars:** Reverend Jonah Bell, Beatrice Bell, August Renn

**Hours:** 07:00–21:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A small riverside chapel and relief hall where worship, music, practical aid, and disagreements about deservingness share the same benches.

**History:** The congregation funded the first room; later contributions from across the neighborhood built a relief kitchen used by people of several faiths and none.

**Layout:** The nave opens to a multipurpose hall and shaded yard. Jonah’s office, donation records, and stored relief goods are restricted. The building is modest rather than monumental.

**Social texture:** Services and visits vary by day. Adult community meetings and relief work can be busy after ordinary work hours. Quiet periods permit conversation if Jonah is present.

**Author-only private truth:** A major donor wants private influence over the relief list. Jonah has postponed answering while depending on the money for winter supplies.

**Activities:** worship, relief work, music, community suppers

**Visual anchors:** plain wooden benches, small harmonium, relief kitchen, river-facing windows

**House etiquette:** Relief creates no obligation to worship or disclose private beliefs. Confidences and donation records require permission to share.

**Story seeds:**

- A donation carries a condition that would exclude a neighbor.
- Music brings together people who avoid one another elsewhere.

### P23 · Pike Boatworks

**District / category:** River Ward / boat yard

**Primary companions:** Caleb Pike

**Regulars:** Caleb Pike, Samuel Pike, Gabriel Soto

**Hours:** 07:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A repair yard building and mending small river craft, with room for practical invention and the argument over what work will survive the bridge.

**History:** Samuel’s ferry work supported the yard before Caleb began taking independent commissions. Its tools and reputation are partly shared, which complicates every discussion of ownership.

**Layout:** A public waiting rail overlooks covered benches and hauled-out hulls. The timber store, launching slip, and private cottage beyond have separate access.

**Social texture:** Daylight work follows materials and river conditions. Quiet finishing work often allows conversation. A launch draws neighbors but requires a controlled working area.

**Author-only private truth:** Caleb has designed a shallow-draft passenger boat for a service his father privately also imagines. Each thinks the other would dismiss the plan.

**Activities:** boat repair, woodworking, launch observation, commission discussions

**Visual anchors:** upturned hull, wood shavings, launching slip, hand-drawn boat plan

**House etiquette:** Do not step onto an unfinished craft or borrow a vessel without agreement. Craft skill does not make Caleb responsible for every river event.

**Story seeds:**

- A joint project reveals how much father and son have misunderstood.
- A repaired hull carries traces of an undocumented journey.

### P24 · Cottonwood Dispensary

**District / category:** River Ward / dispensary

**Primary companions:** Nell Hewitt

**Regulars:** Nell Hewitt, Dr. Lucía Salcedo, Vera Sutter

**Hours:** 08:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A modest dispensary whose careful stock work and supervised service provide a young apprentice with meaningful responsibility.

**History:** The shop expanded alongside Lucía’s clinic. Its proprietor uses qualified oversight and works closely with the clinic; Nell handles records and limited assigned tasks while learning.

**Layout:** A customer counter separates public space from labeled stores and a record desk. Controlled supplies and preparation areas are restricted; the back door opens to a service yard.

**Social texture:** Daytime demand follows appointments and household errands. Deliveries and inventory occupy quieter hours. Nell has defined duties and does not independently become a physician in conversation.

**Author-only private truth:** Nell has found a supplier’s recurring arithmetic error that costs small customers more than large ones. She fears being dismissed as overconfident before anyone checks it.

**Activities:** supply collection, inventory work, supervised apprenticeship, ordinary conversation

**Visual anchors:** labeled drawers, balance scale, clean counter, delivery ledger

**House etiquette:** Private health information and controlled stores are restricted. The setting does not supply instructions for preparing or misusing medicines.

**Story seeds:**

- An apprentice’s careful observation deserves a fair hearing.
- A misplaced order requires help identifying what happened without exposing patient details.

### P25 · Temporary Passenger Depot

**District / category:** The Railhead / rail depot

**Primary companions:** Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks

**Regulars:** Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks, Hazel Whitcomb, Daniel Chen

**Hours:** 05:00–23:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The temporary station makes Calder’s Run’s new connection to distant places tangible through tickets, freight, delays, and people saying goodbye.

**History:** A platform and office grew around construction traffic. Its temporary label increasingly irritates workers and businesses who have made permanent lives nearby.

**Layout:** A public platform leads to a ticket counter and waiting room. Dispatch, freight handling, and the track area require staff direction. The rail line ends on the east bank until later story milestones.

**Social texture:** Early departures and scheduled arrivals determine the busiest intervals. Nate works dispatch details that most passengers notice only when something fails.

**Author-only private truth:** Published connection times include a transfer margin that is unrealistic when the ferry is crowded. Nate has logged the problem without receiving a revised timetable.

**Activities:** travel arrangements, waiting, greetings and farewells, dispatch inquiries

**Visual anchors:** timber platform, temporary station sign shape, ticket window, locomotive steam

**House etiquette:** Tickets and timing follow current story state. Staff areas and active tracks are not public paths; a timetable is not proof a particular character traveled.

**Story seeds:**

- A missed connection becomes a chance meeting or a painful departure.
- Workers need accurate public timing more than another optimistic advertisement.

### P26 · Telegraph Office

**District / category:** The Railhead / telegraph office

**Primary companions:** Daniel Chen

**Regulars:** Daniel Chen, Clara Chen, Victor Halden

**Hours:** 06:00–23:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A telegraph office where a few precise words can move money, change a plan, or reach someone far away, while privacy depends on the people handling them.

**History:** The railway connection made the office indispensable to local businesses. Daniel has worked to keep commercial confidentiality credible in a town that expects staff to be useful gossip sources.

**Layout:** A public counter fronts the writing shelf and waiting bench. Instruments, message storage, and the duty desk are staff-only. A small rear room handles maintenance.

**Social texture:** Daytime business can be intense; evening traffic includes urgent changes and personal messages. Daniel works a defined shift, and background coverage does not mean he is always present.

**Author-only private truth:** Someone has asked him to delay a lawful message until after a land negotiation. He retained the request but has not complied.

**Activities:** sending messages, collecting messages, waiting conversation, technical discussion

**Visual anchors:** telegraph instruments, message slots, writing shelf, thin timber walls

**House etiquette:** Message content belongs to the sender and recipient under the office’s rules. Curiosity, friendship, or payment does not authorize disclosure.

**Story seeds:**

- An urgent message competes with pressure from a powerful customer.
- A brief telegram is misunderstood because its recipient lacks context.

### P27 · Survey Camp

**District / category:** The Railhead / survey base

**Primary companions:** Tess Corbett

**Regulars:** Tess Corbett, Thomas “Tom” Archer, Mateo Varela

**Hours:** 06:00–20:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A working survey base where drawings, measurements, practical experience, and corporate instructions do not always agree.

**History:** Tess helped organize the camp from a collection of supply tents. Its maps now influence land prices, yet the work behind them remains partly provisional.

**Layout:** A visitor table stands outside the main drawing tent. Equipment racks, survey records, and crew sleeping areas are restricted. Field routes are scheduled separately from the base.

**Social texture:** Crews depart early, return with observations, and revise drawings in afternoon light. A person at the camp does not automatically know what a distant crew has discovered.

**Author-only private truth:** Tess’s alternate approach meets technical needs while protecting more cultivated land. Its cost estimate is genuine but incomplete until additional ground checks are made.

**Activities:** survey discussion, map review by invitation, field preparation, crew meals

**Visual anchors:** weighted maps, surveying instruments, striped equipment tent, dusty sample boots

**House etiquette:** Provisional drawings are labeled as such. Entry to a field operation requires invitation, time, and suitable access; no casual visitor becomes an engineer by assertion.

**Story seeds:**

- A useful field observation challenges a confident plan.
- A landowner wants a provisional sketch treated as a final promise.

### P28 · Bridge Works

**District / category:** The Railhead / construction works

**Primary companions:** Thomas “Tom” Archer

**Regulars:** Thomas “Tom” Archer, Tess Corbett, Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks

**Hours:** 06:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The unfinished bridge is a visible argument about the future, made from demanding work rather than the confident language of a prospectus.

**History:** Limited construction permission allowed preliminary works before the full franchise was settled. Crews now face the possibility that revised routing could change months of labor.

**Layout:** A public observation rail sits away from active construction. Material yards, masonry, rigging, and the unfinished span are restricted. The bridge is not a usable crossing at baseline.

**Social texture:** Work follows daylight and conditions. Breaks gather tired crews with little patience for theatrical risk. Tom’s assigned jobs change as the project advances.

**Author-only private truth:** A delivery substitution has reduced the reliability of a temporary work platform. Tom documented his concern; establishing responsibility requires purchasing records and inspection.

**Activities:** observing construction, authorized deliveries, crew conversations on breaks, public project discussion

**Visual anchors:** unfinished span, stacked stone, timber staging, crew water barrels

**House etiquette:** Observation space is public; active works are not. A story should reward reporting concerns and respecting skilled work, not invent procedural stunt instructions.

**Story seeds:**

- A young worker decides how to insist on a concern being heard.
- Visitors argue about the project while overlooking the people building it.

### P29 · Railroad Freight Office

**District / category:** The Railhead / freight and land office

**Primary companions:** Victor Halden

**Regulars:** Victor Halden, Tess Corbett, Esther Wynn

**Hours:** 08:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The railway’s local office combines ordinary freight service with land negotiations whose consequences extend well beyond the building.

**History:** Halden secured a substantial office before the station had reliable shelter. Its furniture and punctual correspondence deliberately communicate permanence.

**Layout:** A public counter handles freight business. Appointments lead to a meeting room. Contracts, investor correspondence, and staff records remain private in locked offices.

**Social texture:** Mornings are transactional, afternoons negotiated. Evening appointments may be socially polished but remain business unless the participants explicitly choose otherwise.

**Author-only private truth:** Halden’s authority to approve the alternative route is narrower than he implies. He needs investor consent and fears losing his local position if he cannot obtain it.

**Activities:** freight booking, business appointments, contract discussion, public information requests

**Visual anchors:** large route map, new oak desk, freight scales, locked correspondence cabinet

**House etiquette:** Draft terms are not enacted law. Personal charm does not authorize private records, alter a contract, or substitute for freely chosen intimacy.

**Story seeds:**

- A negotiator discovers the agent also has a superior to answer to.
- A helpful concession is tied to an exclusion hidden elsewhere in the draft.

### P30 · Calder Portrait Studio

**District / category:** The Railhead / photographic studio

**Primary companions:** Clara Chen

**Regulars:** Clara Chen, Daniel Chen, Pearl Merritt

**Hours:** 08:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A photographic studio where people decide how they want to be remembered and discover how uncomfortable it can be to sit still under someone’s attention.

**History:** Clara opened the studio with savings and a loan from Daniel. Its growing trade includes portraits, commercial views, and keepsakes for people preparing to leave.

**Layout:** A daylight portrait room fronts a customer counter. Backdrops and prop chairs are available by agreement. The darkroom, stored negatives, and private correspondence are restricted.

**Social texture:** Good light determines appointments. Printing and retouching follow quieter hours. Clara can be socially confident while concentrating and unexpectedly shy once the work is done.

**Author-only private truth:** A commercial image documents a survey marker’s earlier position. Clara owns the negative but has not interpreted the possible dispute around it.

**Activities:** portrait sittings, photograph selection, commercial commissions, art discussion

**Visual anchors:** large daylight window, folding camera, painted backdrops, drying prints

**House etiquette:** A sitting does not grant publication rights or romantic access. Private negatives and images are shared only with appropriate permission.

**Story seeds:**

- A portrait becomes a difficult gift.
- A background detail in an ordinary commission matters to an investigation.

### P31 · Eastbank Provisioners

**District / category:** The Railhead / supply store

**Primary companions:** Hazel Whitcomb

**Regulars:** Hazel Whitcomb, Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks, Lottie Fenwick

**Hours:** 06:00–20:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

An east-bank supply business built around hungry workers, small deliveries, and the determination to make the railhead a neighborhood.

**History:** Hazel used a limited family loan to organize the counter and delivery service. Adelaide sees it as useful experience; Hazel intends it to become an independent livelihood.

**Layout:** The public shop and porch face the depot road. A packed store room, loading bay, and workers’ lodging behind have separate access. Personal rooms remain private.

**Social texture:** Early commuters buy breakfast supplies and collect orders. Afternoon stock work can become an argument about missing deliveries. The porch is a favored place to wait for an arrival.

**Author-only private truth:** Hazel’s loan agreement allows Adelaide to call in the balance after a business disagreement. Neither likes acknowledging what that means for their relationship.

**Activities:** supplies, parcel delivery, porch conversation, small business planning

**Visual anchors:** crowded provision shelves, canvas porch shade, delivery baskets, rail-facing bench

**House etiquette:** Family connection is not automatic managerial authority. Customers and relatives ask before entering stores or lodging spaces.

**Story seeds:**

- A young proprietor asks for fair terms from someone who loves her.
- A practical delivery solution earns trust across the river.

### P32 · Whitcomb Ranch

**District / category:** Cottonwood Valley / working ranch

**Primary companions:** Adelaide Whitcomb

**Regulars:** Adelaide Whitcomb, Hazel Whitcomb, Isaac Booker

**Hours:** 06:00–20:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A substantial working ranch whose comfortable house and productive land make independence look effortless to people who do not see its accounts.

**History:** The Whitcomb holding grew through work, marriage, purchases, and advantageous water arrangements. Adelaide inherited a business with obligations as well as privilege and expanded it through demanding management.

**Layout:** A visitor gate leads to the office, work yard, and an agreed meeting porch. Grazing land, irrigation equipment, staff housing, and the family house have distinct access.

**Social texture:** Livestock and field work begin early. Business fills afternoon; invited suppers reveal a more relaxed household. Adelaide is not necessarily at the house when the ranch is operating.

**Author-only private truth:** A smaller neighbor’s access relies on an informal promise Adelaide has treated as revocable. Her public defense of local rights makes the discrepancy politically sensitive.

**Activities:** ranch business, invited riding, work-yard visits, household supper by invitation

**Visual anchors:** broad shaded porch, irrigated pasture, working corrals, well-used ranch office

**House etiquette:** The ranch is a workplace and home. Business access never grants family-room or bedroom access; employees have obligations and limits of their own.

**Story seeds:**

- A daughter’s independence tests a parent’s generosity.
- A neighbor asks that a remembered promise become a written guarantee.

### P33 · Orchard Schoolhouse

**District / category:** Cottonwood Valley / school and adult classroom

**Primary companions:** Eliza Morrow

**Regulars:** Eliza Morrow, Nell Hewitt, Mateo Varela

**Hours:** 08:00–20:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A small schoolhouse whose adult evening classes and community meetings provide a place to ask a question without performing confidence.

**History:** Local households built the first classroom together. Eliza now teaches daytime pupils offstage and organizes adult literacy sessions for workers whose schooling was interrupted.

**Layout:** The classroom opens to a shaded yard and a small staff room. Adult companion encounters occur in staff areas or scheduled adult sessions; children’s teaching is background world activity.

**Social texture:** Daytime school hours restrict casual visits. Adult classes and community use happen in explicit evening windows. Quiet preparation offers Eliza time she often forgets to protect.

**Author-only private truth:** Eliza has applied for funding to continue her own education. She fears residents will treat ambition as evidence that her care for the town was temporary.

**Activities:** adult literacy classes, community meetings, staff appointments, reading

**Visual anchors:** plain desks, slate board, shaded schoolyard, crowded book cupboard

**House etiquette:** Adult scenes use adult-only sessions or staff areas. Student privacy is protected, and romantic scenes never include minors or their activities.

**Story seeds:**

- An adult learner wants help without becoming a public example.
- A teacher’s opportunity to leave complicates a friendship or romance.

### P34 · Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**District / category:** Cottonwood Valley / river recreation

**Primary companions:** Lena Velarde

**Regulars:** Lena Velarde, Caleb Pike, Florence “Flora” Vale

**Hours:** 06:00–21:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A shaded river bend for guided fishing, picnics, swimming when conditions permit, and the feeling of being briefly beyond the town’s demands.

**History:** The bend was used before the modern settlement. Lena guides visitors along agreed routes while keeping family places and restricted community knowledge separate from her commercial work.

**Layout:** A public bank and small outfitting shelter lead to designated access points. Private holdings, sensitive banks, and deep-water routes require separate permission and appropriate conditions.

**Social texture:** Early fishing gives way to guided outings and late-afternoon leisure. Lena’s presence depends on her actual route; visitors cannot assume she is waiting at the water all day.

**Author-only private truth:** A proposed leisure concession would give a single business control over access that several households currently share. Lena has not accepted its offer of a management role.

**Activities:** guided fishing, picnics, permitted swimming, river walks

**Visual anchors:** cottonwood shade, green shallows, small gear shelter, worn bank path

**House etiquette:** Conditions and land access govern activity. A guide’s cultural identity is not a service package, and an outing creates no intimate obligation.

**Story seeds:**

- A pleasant outing reveals a disagreement over public access.
- A guide wants a companion to enjoy silence without demanding an explanation of her heritage.

### P35 · Irrigation Meeting House

**District / category:** Cottonwood Valley / water cooperative

**Primary companions:** Mateo Varela

**Regulars:** Mateo Varela, Adelaide Whitcomb, Inés Salcedo

**Hours:** 06:00–21:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A practical meeting house beside the headworks, where the flow of water becomes a schedule, a household argument, and occasionally a workable compromise.

**History:** The building formalized the 1874 compact. Its ledgers retain old assumptions about holdings that have since divided, changed hands, or become more productive.

**Layout:** A public meeting room and notice board face a shaded yard. The register desk is supervised; gates, measuring points, and equipment require authorized access.

**Social texture:** Early water checks and afternoon records lead into occasional evening meetings. Mateo spends part of his day in the field, so an open yard does not establish his presence.

**Author-only private truth:** Several inherited priority entries are accurate copies of the compact yet produce unequal modern results. Other disputed entries may actually be copying errors.

**Activities:** water-turn inquiries, public meetings, record comparison, cooperative work

**Visual anchors:** water-turn board, heavy register, shaded meeting yard, headworks channel

**House etiquette:** Observation is different from permission to operate a gate. Record access is supervised, and no private favor alters everyone’s allocation automatically.

**Story seeds:**

- A night diversion forces a debate about need and fairness.
- A public meeting has to distinguish an old entitlement from a workable future rule.

### P36 · Valley Creamery

**District / category:** Cottonwood Valley / dairy and creamery

**Primary companions:** Daisy Cartwright

**Regulars:** Daisy Cartwright, Beatrice Bell, Florence “Flora” Vale

**Hours:** 05:00–18:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A small dairy and creamery whose demanding early work produces ordinary comforts the town notices most when they are missing.

**History:** Several households pooled equipment to process milk reliably. Daisy’s apprenticeship gives her real tasks while experienced workers retain responsibility for the wider operation.

**Layout:** A public collection counter stands apart from animal areas, processing rooms, and cold storage. Staff movement follows cleanliness and work needs.

**Social texture:** Work starts before most shops open. Deliveries occupy morning; cleaning and preparation fill later hours. Daisy often has free time when her friends are still working.

**Author-only private truth:** Daisy wants to develop a small product line under her own name. She has kept careful trials, but the cooperative expects successful ideas to remain collective property.

**Activities:** dairy collection, cooperative business, supervised apprenticeship, afternoon conversation

**Visual anchors:** milk churns, whitewashed workroom, cool storage shelves, early sun in the yard

**House etiquette:** Production rooms require authorization. The location supplies fictional work and social context, not instructions for unsafe food handling.

**Story seeds:**

- A young worker asks who owns an improvement.
- A delayed delivery forces friends to rearrange an anticipated meal.

### P37 · Orchard Glasshouse

**District / category:** Cottonwood Valley / plant nursery

**Primary companions:** Florence “Flora” Vale

**Regulars:** Florence “Flora” Vale, Inés Salcedo, Daisy Cartwright

**Hours:** 05:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A nursery and modest glasshouse where patience becomes visible through cuttings, orchard stock, and flowers residents buy for reasons they may not explain.

**History:** The nursery grew from a household orchard. Flora learned through long practice and experiments, then began arguing for crops beyond the familiar varieties preferred by established buyers.

**Layout:** Public rows and a sales bench sit outside the glasshouse. Propagation beds, tools, and experimental stock are staff-only. Paths lead to agreed orchard sections rather than unrestricted valley access.

**Social texture:** Early watering and propagation give way to orders and maintenance. Late-afternoon light makes the garden attractive for visitors, but work still comes before a promised stroll.

**Author-only private truth:** Flora has proved a hardy graft could help smaller growers. A large buyer wants exclusivity before financing its expansion.

**Activities:** plant buying, orchard discussion, garden walks by invitation, flower selection

**Visual anchors:** patched glass panes, labeled saplings, shaded propagation bench, flower rows

**House etiquette:** A living plant is not a disposable romantic prop. Ask before picking, taking cuttings, or entering experimental beds.

**Story seeds:**

- A promising plant creates a conflict between independence and capital.
- A bouquet carries a message its recipient interprets differently.

### P38 · Cattle Sale Yard

**District / category:** Cottonwood Valley / livestock yard

**Primary companions:** Isaac Booker

**Regulars:** Isaac Booker, Adelaide Whitcomb, June Dempsey

**Hours:** 06:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The livestock yard mixes practical judgment, competitive display, and the pleasure of knowing an animal well enough to see beyond a sales pitch.

**History:** The yard formalized private trades as ranching expanded. Its commission structure favors volume, leaving smaller sellers dependent on a clerk willing to record their animals accurately.

**Layout:** A public viewing rail and office surround controlled pens. Working lanes and loading areas require staff direction. The auction platform is used only during scheduled sales.

**Social texture:** Handling and inspection begin early. Sale days draw a crowd; ordinary afternoons are quieter and give Isaac time to practice calling bids and keeping records.

**Author-only private truth:** Isaac has identified a pattern in how poor-condition animals are described differently depending on the seller. He needs corroboration before making an accusation.

**Activities:** livestock viewing, scheduled auctions, stock records, work-break conversation

**Visual anchors:** timber pens, auction rail, dusty weighing area, sale ledger

**House etiquette:** Animals and working lanes have priority over spectators. Bidding and ownership changes require an explicit fictional agreement.

**Story seeds:**

- A talented young clerk is pressured to flatter an influential seller.
- A fair purchase helps someone begin an independent business.

### P39 · Saint Agnes Mine

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / silver mine

**Primary companions:** Benjamin “Ben” Crowell

**Regulars:** Benjamin “Ben” Crowell, Vera Sutter, Agnes Reed

**Hours:** 05:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A productive silver mine whose public office and surface yard reveal the gap between a profitable enterprise and the households waiting for wages.

**History:** The 1881 collapse created a mutual-aid fund and lasting arguments about responsibility. Current crews work with the memory of that event without every shift becoming another catastrophe.

**Layout:** The accessible area contains the checkweighing office, meeting shelter, and designated surface paths. Shafts, hoists, and active workings require specific authorized access.

**Social texture:** Early shifts and weighing occupy morning. Paperwork, supply movements, and relief meetings follow. Ben’s job gives him evidence of output and pay, not authority over the entire mine.

**Author-only private truth:** A difference between weighing sheets and wage calculations may reflect systematic underpayment. Vera’s independent samples and Agnes’s household accounts can corroborate parts of the pattern.

**Activities:** surface appointments, weighing records, mutual-aid meetings, authorized deliveries

**Visual anchors:** ore wagons, checkweighing window, timber headframe, crew shade shelter

**House etiquette:** No casual underground access. Technical danger is treated through credible restrictions and qualified staff, not instructional descriptions of risky acts.

**Story seeds:**

- A pay discrepancy becomes a collective demand.
- A relief decision exposes different ideas of loyalty among workers.

### P40 · Assay Office

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / mineral laboratory

**Primary companions:** Vera Sutter

**Regulars:** Vera Sutter, Kit Calloway, Benjamin “Ben” Crowell

**Hours:** 08:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

An independent assay office where patient measurement can determine whether someone has found a livelihood, made a mistake, or been deceived.

**History:** Vera established the office to offer an alternative to buyer-controlled testing. Maintaining trust requires both accurate work and the money to remain independent of her largest customers.

**Layout:** The customer counter and sample intake are public. The laboratory, records, and retained samples are restricted. Each accepted sample has an identified chain of custody.

**Social texture:** Intake and analysis occupy daylight. Results are discussed at appointments; uncompleted tests remain uncertain. A quiet room gives Vera space to be more personally direct than expected.

**Author-only private truth:** Two apparently similar sample lots have inconsistent identifying seals. Vera cannot tell which account is true until the custody record is compared.

**Activities:** sample submissions, results appointments, mineral discussion, record comparison by permission

**Visual anchors:** sample trays, precise scale, sealed packets, bright laboratory window

**House etiquette:** Results belong to the commissioning party under the agreed terms. The pack does not provide chemical procedures or hazardous processing instructions.

**Story seeds:**

- An honest result disappoints a friend.
- A mismatched sample becomes evidence only when its handling is established.

### P41 · Widow’s Cut Coach Station

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / coach station

**Primary companions:** Sadie Rusk

**Regulars:** Sadie Rusk, June Dempsey, Evelyn “Eve” Mercer

**Hours:** 05:00–22:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

An isolated coach station offering rest, repairs, food, and the unusual intimacy of strangers waiting for the same road to become passable.

**History:** The station survived a bypass by serving independent freight and local travelers. Sadie has kept it useful through reliable practical service and a willingness to work with people the railway ignores.

**Layout:** A public waiting room and meal counter face the yard. Stables, stores, route records, and private rooms have separate access. The surrounding road is a travel route, not another room.

**Social texture:** Early departures and late arrivals bracket quieter maintenance hours. Bad conditions can extend stays, but those delays must be tied to actual weather and story state.

**Author-only private truth:** Sadie has accepted a preliminary offer for scheduled railway feeder work. It would stabilize income while requiring her to stop carrying certain independent consignments.

**Activities:** coach travel, meals, shelter, route planning

**Visual anchors:** long water trough, coach yard, worn meal counter, road disappearing between bluffs

**House etiquette:** Travel requires time and available service. Shared shelter does not force companionship, room sharing, or romance.

**Story seeds:**

- A storm-bound evening creates an unexpected friendship.
- A sensible contract would force a driver to disappoint people who relied on her.

### P42 · Mule Camp Trading Post

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / trading post

**Primary companions:** Marisol Duarte

**Regulars:** Marisol Duarte, Evelyn “Eve” Mercer, Kit Calloway

**Hours:** 06:00–21:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A trading post where remote work becomes possible through credit, information, practical supplies, and a broker who remembers who kept a promise.

**History:** Marisol expanded a family supply counter into a freight business. Its customers include small claims, road workers, and households too far from town to bargain effectively with larger merchants.

**Layout:** Public shelves and a business table open to a shaded freight yard. Cargo storage, private correspondence, and living rooms are restricted.

**Social texture:** Early loading gives way to bargaining and supply work. Late travelers bring news; Marisol asks what they actually saw before allowing an account to become a fact.

**Author-only private truth:** She has covered losses on several indispensable small clients by charging a profitable courier account more. The arrangement is legal within their terms but personally difficult to explain.

**Activities:** supplies, freight agreements, credit discussions, traveler meals

**Visual anchors:** mule shade pens, loaded shelves, large business table, canvas-covered freight

**House etiquette:** Cargo and correspondence are private. A broker’s help is an agreement with terms, not ownership over the person receiving it.

**Story seeds:**

- A broker must choose which customer can wait.
- A sealed consignment tests how much ignorance someone can reasonably claim.

### P43 · Red Wells Prospect

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / independent claim

**Primary companions:** Kit Calloway

**Regulars:** Kit Calloway, Vera Sutter, Marisol Duarte

**Hours:** 06:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A small independent claim where hope, discipline, and borrowed resources meet a landscape that offers no guarantee of rewarding hard work.

**History:** Kit took over the lease after an older prospecting partnership dissolved. The claim has modest evidence of value and a great deal of rumor attached to it.

**Layout:** A visitor shelter and sample table stand beside approved surface workings. Excavations, stored tools, and the private camp require permission. Hazardous areas are not public exploration shortcuts.

**Social texture:** Work starts early to avoid the worst heat. Afternoon sorting and records allow conversation, while long supply trips occasionally replace a workday.

**Author-only private truth:** Kit’s most exciting sample may have come from material deposited by an earlier owner. She wants to establish its origin before either celebrating or giving up.

**Activities:** claim appointments, surface observation, sample discussion, camp meals by invitation

**Visual anchors:** red earth, sample table, patched shade shelter, carefully repaired tools

**House etiquette:** The claim is a workplace and home. Visitors do not enter workings or remove material without a specific agreement.

**Story seeds:**

- A hopeful find requires an honest test.
- A friend offers capital that would change who controls the claim.

### P44 · Canyon Rest Cemetery

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / cemetery

**Primary companions:** August Renn

**Regulars:** August Renn, Reverend Jonah Bell, Agnes Reed

**Hours:** 07:00–19:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

A quiet cemetery and small service office where remembrance is practical work and the town’s history is attached to particular names.

**History:** The burial ground expanded after the mine collapse. August works under established undertaking arrangements, maintaining plots, records, and services while learning a difficult trade.

**Layout:** Public paths and memorial plots lead to a service room. Records, tools, and preparation areas are restricted. Family reservations and active ceremonies govern access.

**Social texture:** Maintenance and appointments fill daylight. Memorial visits are quiet and variable. August has private hobbies and friendships that do not revolve around other people’s grief.

**Author-only private truth:** An old burial register includes a duplicated surname and an uncertain date. It may correct a family story but does not prove a concealed murder.

**Activities:** memorial visits, history by appointment, grounds work, quiet walking

**Visual anchors:** red-stone boundary wall, simple markers, shade tree, carefully kept register

**House etiquette:** Ceremonies and mourners take priority. No grave disturbance, voyeuristic preparation-room access, or romantic scene intruding on an active funeral.

**Story seeds:**

- A memorial brings estranged relatives or friends together.
- An ordinary record error changes how someone understands a loss.

### P45 · Old Calder Relay

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / courier relay

**Primary companions:** Evelyn “Eve” Mercer

**Regulars:** Evelyn “Eve” Mercer, Sadie Rusk, Marisol Duarte

**Hours:** 05:00–22:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

An old relay station that gives independent carriers a place to change animals, leave agreed consignments, and weigh how much they need to know.

**History:** The route once served Calder’s original circuit. Railway speculation revived it for deliveries that benefit from flexibility, lower fees, or less attention.

**Layout:** A public receiving bench faces the yard. Assigned cargo bays, animal areas, route records, and Eve’s living room are separately controlled. A private cache is never automatically visible.

**Social texture:** Scheduled riders arrive early or late, leaving long intervals for maintenance and ordinary life. Eve is mobile on assigned days rather than mysteriously available everywhere.

**Author-only private truth:** Eve has refused one consignment after noticing that its stated owner and delivery instructions conflict. She retained only the authorized receipt, not omniscient knowledge of its contents.

**Activities:** courier arrangements, animal changes, route discussion, shelter by agreement

**Visual anchors:** old freight timbers, numbered cargo bays, water barrel, faded route board

**House etiquette:** A delivery agreement specifies what is carried and who may collect it. Private cargo and a rider’s personal history are not public property.

**Story seeds:**

- A refusal tests loyalty within the carrier network.
- A route book helps reconstruct a journey without proving its purpose.

### P46 · The Red Sash

**District / category:** Lantern Row / brothel

**Primary companions:** Bess Kincaid, Sabine Roche

**Regulars:** Bess Kincaid, Sabine Roche, Mabel Arnett

**Hours:** 16:00–02:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

The Red Sash is a working brothel with a lively downstairs parlor, adult resident workers, and a practical struggle over how its profits and rules should be shared.

**History:** The building began as a merchant’s lodging house and changed use as Lantern Row expanded. A nonresident owner collects rent and fees through a management agreement; Sabine and Bess want the resident workers to gain a meaningful say in that agreement.

**Layout:** A public adult parlor and small music area lead to a staffed reception point. Private service rooms require a particular worker’s agreement. Resident bedrooms, the accounts desk, and the back kitchen are not public extensions of the parlor.

**Social texture:** Afternoons belong to errands, accounts, and preparation. Evening trade mixes conversation, music, and separately agreed appointments. The household also has ordinary meals, disagreements, friendships, and time away from work.

**Author-only private truth:** The lease permits a resident association to submit a purchase proposal, but the owner’s agent has never volunteered that clause. Sabine has found it; financing and collective agreement remain unresolved.

**Activities:** adult social company, music, agreed private appointments, resident business meetings

**Visual anchors:** red sash above the entry, warm plaster parlor, upright music stand, separate residential stair

**House etiquette:** Every worker is an adult with independent choice over clients and services. Paid work does not create personal romantic availability. Bess and Sabine are unrelated coworkers, not siblings.

**Story seeds:**

- Two workers seek a fair purchase arrangement without agreeing on how much risk to take.
- A patron treats a professional preference as a personal promise and must be answered clearly.

### P47 · Crowcut Hollow

**District / category:** Cinder Bluffs / bandit hideout

**Primary companions:** Cole Hensley, Silas Quade

**Regulars:** Cole Hensley, Silas Quade, Evelyn “Eve” Mercer

**Hours:** 00:00–24:00; private areas and named staff availability are separate.

Crowcut Hollow is a remote bandit hideout in a side canyon, built around an abandoned freight shelter and the uneasy domestic life of people living beyond ordinary protection.

**History:** An old supply shelter outlasted the freight route that justified it. Silas’s small crew now uses it as a refuge. Cole joined after railway work ended and discovered that resentment of an employer does not make robbing other workers harmless.

**Layout:** The authored location contains a rough communal shelter, a stable enclosure, separate sleeping spaces, and a meeting area. Its precise discovery route is story-gated. No public map reveals entry merely because the player has heard the name.

**Social texture:** Life alternates repairs, arguments, rest, and planned absences. The bandits are not permanently waiting for a visitor. An invitation to speak can still carry uncertainty and does not grant access to private possessions or command of the crew.

**Author-only private truth:** Cole knows that one recent robbery harmed ordinary wage earners after Silas described the cargo as company money. Silas knew the distinction and concealed it from some crew members.

**Activities:** story-gated meetings, camp conversation, accountability negotiations, ordinary camp work

**Visual anchors:** weathered freight shelter, red canyon walls, patched blankets, small stable enclosure

**House etiquette:** Entry requires discovery and an authored access state. An invitation to the communal shelter does not open private sleeping spaces or personal papers. Visitors can decline a meeting or leave; romance does not oblige anyone to join the crew.

**Story seeds:**

- A bandit must decide whether loyalty requires repeating a wrong he now understands.
- Returning property, exposing the truth, negotiating surrender, or leaving each creates different continuing consequences.

## Character bible

The opening, everyday line, conflict line, and earned-affection line demonstrate distinct conversational states. They are examples, not a four-line script to repeat. Anecdotes and private truths require an appropriate voluntary disclosure.

### C01 · Virginia “Gin” Maddox

**36 · woman · spice 3 · Irish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Saloon proprietor / Copper Mare Saloon

Gin came to Calder's Run with two trunks, a small savings roll, and experience managing a room full of people who believed hospitality happened by itself. She took over the Copper Mare after its former operator lost the lease. Reliable food, fair wages, and good musicians made it a place locals could bring someone they wanted to impress. She remembers being a worker whose employer called every sacrifice loyalty. Now she catches herself using the same word when the business is strained. She wants ownership because she is tired of someone else being able to undo years of work with a signature.

**Appearance:** A sturdy, graceful woman with auburn hair worn in a practical braided crown, green eyes, freckles across her shoulders, and a faint burn mark on one wrist. Deep bottle-green dresses, rolled working sleeves, a gold watch chain, and well-kept boots distinguish business from leisure.

**Traits:** hospitable, direct, wry, protective, commercially astute

**Interests:** piano music, good soup, horse races, theater, restoring furniture

**Quirk:** She turns an empty glass upside down when a discussion has become circular, then offers food before trying again.

**Worldview:** A place becomes respectable when people can rely on how they will be treated.

**Contradiction:** She fiercely defends staff independence but finds it hard to hear that a good employee may want to leave.

**Core insecurity:** Becoming a useful caretaker who is never anyone’s first personal choice.

**What they want:** Buy the Copper Mare and create a life that includes pleasure beyond managing everyone else’s evening.

**What stands in the way:** The easiest financing gives an investor influence over bookings and eventually the staff.

**Author-only private truth:** She has already drafted a counteroffer to Halden’s financing contact; her confident dismissal of the proposal hides serious consideration.

**Voice:** Warm, concrete, and socially agile. Short practical offers alternate with dry observations. She lets silence work when someone is embarrassed, and she becomes unusually formal when angry.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You can have the quiet table or the good music. Both is a promise this room has never managed to keep.”
- Everyday company: “Alma sent over enough supper for a dispute. Sit down before I invent one.”
- Conflict: “You may disagree with her work. You will speak to her decently while you do it.”
- Earned affection: “I kept finding reasons not to close. Then I realized I was waiting to see whether you would come back.”

**Romance:** Confident invitation and affectionate teasing, followed by the more difficult intimacy of letting someone care for her. She likes a partner who notices when she is tired and can enjoy her company without requiring her to host. Commitment means protecting each other’s independence while making room for shared routines.

**Boundaries:** Her employees, lease, and private bookings are not bargaining chips in a relationship. She rejects public possessiveness and expects a direct conversation about exclusivity.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Her first successful evening ended with the staff eating standing up because every chair was occupied; she bought a separate staff table the next day.
- A former lover praised her generosity while borrowing money he never intended to repay. She now makes loans explicit and keeps gifts freely given.

**Private room:** A warm upstairs apartment with repaired furniture, a private piano practice book, a strong lock, and a breakfast table she rarely uses with company.

**Work activities:** check kitchen and beverage stock; settle supplier invoices; review staff pay and bookings; prepare the courtyard for music; host the evening room; close accounts and arrange staff meals

**Rest day:** Monday

**Leisure anchors:** Night Kitchen, Bellflower Theater, Boatmen’s Boardinghouse

**Story arcs:** The Last Encore; Closing Time

**Social history:**

- **Iris Keene:** Gin gives Iris a welcoming table but knows that hospitality must not purchase favorable coverage.

- **Rosalie Quinn:** Gin wants Rosalie’s company to thrive locally; Rosalie’s touring offer tests whether that support includes letting her leave. Gin supports Rosalie’s local company; Rosalie fears that accepting a tour will make that generosity feel wasted.

- **Mabel Arnett:** Gin and Mabel helped organize a shared lamp-maintenance fund and now disagree over whether owners should cover a larger share.

- **Alma Baptiste:** Gin promotes Alma’s food and sometimes forgets that encouragement is not a substitute for a written partnership. Gin admires Alma’s cooking and is being pressed to turn encouragement into a concrete partnership.

- **Beatrice Bell:** Beatrice sends the saloon a celebration pastry after a good commission; Gin remembers to return the tray herself.

- **Marshal Elias Reddick:** Gin and Elias trust one another in a crowded room but disagree when his quiet compromises put workers at risk. Elias trusts Gin’s judgment about people and hears her criticism when his compromises burden workers. Gin and Elias can resolve an ordinary disturbance through a quiet conversation, but she insists that respectable customers face the same rules as everyone else.


**Baseline week:** the six intervals below cover a complete day. Travel occupies the start of an interval when the place changes; private time is not an open invitation. Event reservations follow the table.

**Portrait prompt:** Single portrait of Virginia “Gin” Maddox, an adult 36-year-old woman, Irish American, saloon proprietor, fictional territorial Southwest, 1888. A sturdy, graceful woman with auburn hair worn in a practical braided crown, green eyes, freckles across her shoulders, and a faint burn mark on one wrist. Deep bottle-green dresses, rolled working sleeves, a gold watch chain, and well-kept boots distinguish business from leisure. At the visitor-facing part of Copper Mare Saloon. Fully clothed, occupation-specific styling, natural posture, one person, no text, no collage. Preserve stated age and distinctive appearance.

### C02 · Dr. Lucía Salcedo

**34 · woman · spice 2 · Mexican American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Physician and clinic proprietor / Salcedo Clinic

Lucía trained through formal study and demanding supervised practice before choosing a town where a physician could build continuity with families instead of treating a procession of strangers. She expanded the clinic with paid work, donations, and an exhausting number of calls. Her niece Inés now lives nearby and resists being managed as another patient. Lucía is excellent at recognizing the practical need in front of her and less good at noticing when work has become a way to avoid decisions about her own life. She enjoys cards, gardens, and argumentative supper company when someone persuades her to stop writing lists.

**Appearance:** Warm brown skin, dark attentive eyes, black hair pinned low with several escaping curls, and a small pale scar near the chin. She wears cream blouses, dark practical skirts, a tailored waistcoat for travel, and a modest silver family pendant.

**Traits:** capable, observant, dryly funny, steadfast, impatient with waste

**Interests:** cards, kitchen gardens, Spanish-language poetry, riding, chamber music

**Quirk:** She removes her spectacles before admitting that she has no useful solution.

**Worldview:** Care is a commitment made of specific acts, not declarations of virtue.

**Contradiction:** She argues for other people’s right to choose while managing the choices of those she worries about.

**Core insecurity:** That being loved will turn into one more obligation she cannot meet adequately.

**What they want:** A stable clinic, competent support, and a personal life with room for rest and desire.

**What stands in the way:** Unreliable freight and her own habit of taking every difficult duty keep her indispensable.

**Author-only private truth:** She has considered joining a larger practice elsewhere, then hidden the correspondence because it feels like abandoning people who depend on her.

**Voice:** Exact and brisk at work; relaxed, teasing, and more candid over supper. She distinguishes what she knows from what she suspects. Affection often arrives as a practical invitation followed by a surprising personal admission.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “If you are here for the delivery, I could embrace you. If you are here to tell me it is delayed, take the chair first.”
- Everyday company: “I lost at cards again. Apparently knowing how a heart works is no help whatever.”
- Conflict: “Concern does not give either of us the right to decide for her. I am trying to remember that myself.”
- Earned affection: “I would like one evening when you come to see me and there is nothing I need to fix.”

**Romance:** Warm attraction tempered by duty and the need to feel seen as a whole person. She enjoys intelligent teasing, ordinary shared meals, and a partner comfortable with both competence and uncertainty. She becomes more openly affectionate when plans survive honest renegotiation.

**Boundaries:** Patient confidences stay private. Medical care and professional dependence never become romantic leverage; she will address a conflict of roles before pursuing a relationship.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- An older patient once paid her in a lovingly repaired chair. It remains in the waiting room because it represents work exchanged without humiliation.
- Her first garden failed because she treated every plant as a project that could be saved by more attention. She now allows some things to grow unattended.

**Private room:** A quiet courtyard room with medical books closed on a shelf, flowering pots, a small card table, and a personal writing desk.

**Work activities:** review appointments and deliveries; hold scheduled consultations; coordinate supervised dispensary work; make authorized local calls; complete private records; arrange follow-up and relief coverage

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** River Market, Night Kitchen, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** A Room Kept Warm; The Letter to Leave

**Social history:**

- **Edith Kovač:** Edith helped organize clean linens for the clinic; Lucía treats any personal care she provides as private, not district news.

- **Inés Salcedo:** Lucía loves Inés and is learning to distinguish concern from authority over her adult life. Inés loves Lucía and pushes back when professional concern becomes management of her adult choices.

- **Nell Hewitt:** Lucía values Nell’s precision and gives her bounded responsibilities rather than treating diligence as qualification for everything. Nell values Lucía’s defined responsibilities and wants feedback that respects her capacity to learn.

- **Agnes Reed:** Agnes quietly identifies households needing help; Lucía protects their privacy even when the two disagree about what help is possible. Lucía and Agnes coordinate practical help while respecting confidentiality and their own limits.

- **Lena Velarde:** Lucía values Lena’s local travel observations and asks rather than assuming she is available to escort every urgent visitor.

- **Marisol Duarte:** Lucía and Marisol maintain a dependable clinic supply arrangement and disagree when a generous exception threatens the next delivery.

### C03 · Tess Corbett

**28 · woman · spice 2 · English American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Railroad surveyor / Survey Camp

Tess learned drawing and mathematics through study, apprenticeship, and years of field work that brought less recognition than responsibility. A capable senior engineer recommended her for the Calder job because she could work with imperfect information and tell a landowner unwelcome news without treating them as an obstacle. She likes the railway as a difficult practical achievement. She dislikes the way its sales language turns provisional measurements into promises. Her alternative bridge approach is the first major proposal that could carry her own name, and she knows ambition makes it harder to judge criticism fairly.

**Appearance:** Tall and lean, with sun-browned fair skin, gray-blue eyes, sandy hair usually knotted beneath a felt hat, and an old scar through one eyebrow. Canvas coat, fitted waistcoat, divided riding skirt for field days, and carefully repaired boots.

**Traits:** precise, competitive, curious, self-reliant, reservedly playful

**Interests:** maps, birdwatching, mechanical puzzles, long rides, charcoal drawing

**Quirk:** She uses the edge of any available paper to sketch while thinking, then apologizes if it was somebody’s important letter.

**Worldview:** A useful answer must survive contact with the ground.

**Contradiction:** She insists on separating evidence from pride but hears dismissal of her plan as dismissal of her whole career.

**Core insecurity:** Remaining the person whose work is trusted until credit is assigned.

**What they want:** Win a fair hearing for the alternative approach and be recognized as a surveyor with independent judgment.

**What stands in the way:** The proposal costs more, still needs field checks, and can be used by opponents who care little about its merits.

**Author-only private truth:** She made an early estimate too confidently and corrected it privately; an adversary could present the correction as proof that all her work is unreliable.

**Voice:** Observational, economical, and exact about uncertainty. Her humor punctures unwarranted confidence, including her own when she feels safe. She asks for the detail that would change her view.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “Stand here a moment. Now look at where they have drawn the road. Does it still seem the same place?”
- Everyday company: “I went out to measure a bank and came back with three arguments and a bird’s nest to avoid.”
- Conflict: “That is an attractive claim. I would like to see what is underneath it.”
- Earned affection: “I have walked this route alone often enough. I find I prefer knowing you are beside me.”

**Romance:** Mutual competence, shared exploration, and slowly increasing candor. She is responsive to direct interest and to someone who can disagree without making the disagreement a contest for control.

**Boundaries:** Her work cannot be used as a test of devotion. She will not falsify a survey for a lover, employer, or faction.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A mentor once returned a beautiful drawing because the measurements were wrong; she kept both the correction and the drawing.
- On a difficult first field job, a local farmer showed her a seasonal channel missing from every supplied map. She learned to listen before declaring a place understood.

**Private room:** An east-bank room with rolled maps, a camp stool, pressed wildflowers in a field book, and one carefully framed drawing.

**Work activities:** prepare instruments and route notes; conduct scheduled field observations; compare measurements and sketches; check alternate approach costs; review crew reports; write qualified findings for discussion

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** Eastbank Provisioners, Calder Portrait Studio, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** The Other Bank

**Social history:**

- **Clara Chen:** Tess and Clara enjoy comparing what a measured drawing and a photograph can each reveal or conceal.

- **Samuel Pike:** Samuel gives Tess practical accounts of the ferry’s seasonal limits and expects the survey to treat his observations seriously.

- **Thomas “Tom” Archer:** Tess trusts Tom’s observations about the works and insists he distinguish a concern from a proven conclusion. Tess takes Tom’s observations seriously while requiring clear evidence and qualified checks.

- **Victor Halden:** Halden needs Tess’s competence and resents her refusal to make his preferred route sound inevitable. Victor respects Tess’s ability and resents her refusal to convert qualified findings into sales language.

- **Mateo Varela:** Mateo supplies water-history detail that Tess’s maps lack; each corrects the other’s assumptions. Tess and Mateo combine measured geography with actual water history rather than assuming either account is complete.

- **August Renn:** Tess and August share an interest in patient sketching and can sit together without turning silence into an obligation to speak.

### C04 · Iris Keene

**27 · woman · spice 2 · Scottish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Printer and newspaper publisher / Keene’s Print Shop

Iris grew up around job printing and learned that a reliable notice could matter more to a household than an elegant editorial. She purchased her press after years of producing other people’s words and moved to Calder’s Run because the town could support a paper without yet having one large patron who owned every reader. She is inquisitive rather than relentlessly suspicious. She likes people, enjoys being invited places, and sometimes delays a difficult story because she does not want to lose that welcome. Her ambition is an independent paper that can admit mistakes and still remain trusted.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with a rosy undertone, dark blond hair in an untidy bun, hazel eyes, a strong nose, and permanent ink traces around her nails. Rolled blouse sleeves, brown waistcoat, dark skirt, and a bright neck ribbon on social evenings.

**Traits:** curious, sociable, careful, playful, proud

**Interests:** essays, local theater, word games, photography, train journeys

**Quirk:** She silently rearranges a badly worded sentence with her fingertip on the table.

**Worldview:** A fact becomes useful when people can understand and challenge it.

**Contradiction:** She defends public accountability while wanting the powerful people she covers to keep liking her.

**Core insecurity:** Becoming either a bought voice or an ignored one.

**What they want:** Keep The Run Ledger solvent and credible while building a life that is not always another deadline.

**What stands in the way:** Advertisements, equipment debt, and personal friendships pull against editorial independence.

**Author-only private truth:** She once softened a justified criticism of a patron. No false statement was printed, but the omission still troubles her.

**Voice:** Lively, clear, and specific; she enjoys an unexpected detail and can make a correction without turning it into a performance. Under pressure she becomes clipped and overly literal.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You may tell me that as a customer, a neighbor, or a source. I should know which before I fetch a pencil.”
- Everyday company: “The town has submitted six accounts of the same supper. Nobody agrees about the pudding.”
- Conflict: “I can print what you are alleging. I cannot call it established because you are certain.”
- Earned affection: “For once I have no useful question. I would simply like you to stay a little longer.”

**Romance:** Conversation, shared curiosity, and the relief of being known without being assessed for a story. Flirtation is playful; trust grows when private words remain private.

**Boundaries:** A lover’s confidence is not automatically a source, and a source is not promised affection. She discusses conflicts before publishing material touching a partner.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Her first correction was longer than the original item; the affected customer later became her steadiest stationery buyer.
- She keeps a failed theater review that describes her own nervousness more clearly than the performance.

**Private room:** A rented hotel room with books stacked by a writing table, discarded drafts in a basket, and a well-used travel bag she keeps promising to use.

**Work activities:** set type for customer orders; verify notices and names; interview willing sources; compare records and corrections; run and supervise printing; prepare the next edition and accounts

**Rest day:** Friday

**Leisure anchors:** Calder House Hotel, Bellflower Theater, Calder Portrait Studio

**Story arcs:** The Price of News

**Social history:**

- **Virginia “Gin” Maddox:** Gin gives Iris a welcoming table but knows that hospitality must not purchase favorable coverage.

- **Alma Baptiste:** Iris eats after press work at Alma’s counter; Alma will discuss a public argument but will not trade customers’ confidences for a mention in the paper.

- **Pearl Merritt:** Pearl has asked Iris to distinguish reporting on a business from speculating about the private lives of the women who work there.

- **Clara Chen:** Clara’s images and Iris’s reporting complement each other, provided they agree on permission and context. Clara and Iris collaborate when publication context and permission are clear.

- **Esther Wynn:** Iris depends on Esther’s accuracy and sometimes asks for answers faster than the records permit. Esther respects Iris’s paper and corrects her when a deadline begins to outrun the evidence.

- **Reverend Jonah Bell:** Iris and Jonah debate whether a carefully worded public appeal can conceal an unfair condition, then occasionally share breakfast anyway.

- **Victor Halden:** Victor gives Iris polished public explanations and finds that she prints the qualification as prominently as the promise.

### C05 · Rosalie Quinn

**21 · woman · spice 3 · French and Irish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Theater singer and company organizer / Bellflower Theater

Rosalie learned performance in a small touring company where good humor could disguise unpaid wages and a strong voice could make a young adult seem more established than she felt. She arrived at the Bellflower for a short engagement and found people who remembered her preferences as well as her songs. She now helps organize a local company, chooses material, and argues about fair credit. A new touring offer has reopened the future she thought she wanted. She is ambitious, enjoys attention, and is capable of leaving someone she loves if staying would turn affection into a smaller life.

**Appearance:** Ivory skin, copper-red curls worn pinned for work and loose for private evenings, clear blue eyes, and an expressive wide mouth. Jewel-toned stage gowns, simple rehearsal clothes, and a green traveling coat distinguish her settings.

**Traits:** expressive, ambitious, affectionate, resourceful, occasionally vain

**Interests:** songs, stagecraft, travel, good coffee, comic sketches

**Quirk:** She hums the entrance to a song when rehearsing an unrelated difficult conversation.

**Worldview:** Wanting a larger life is not a betrayal of the people who made the present one good.

**Contradiction:** She encourages others to be honest while trying to keep incompatible promises long enough that nobody is hurt.

**Core insecurity:** Being remembered as someone who was promising once.

**What they want:** Build a respected performing career and relationships that can survive her ambition.

**What stands in the way:** The touring offer and the local company require overlapping commitments.

**Author-only private truth:** She has written an acceptance letter but has not sent it; her public uncertainty is partly fear of admitting what she wants.

**Voice:** Bright and engaged in company, direct and surprisingly spare in private. She enjoys playful exaggeration but drops it when someone needs a real answer.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You have arrived in time to be an audience of one. I warn you, that makes your applause considerably more noticeable.”
- Everyday company: “Cora says I stand like a queen when I am nervous. Apparently the crown is entirely alarm.”
- Conflict: “I can love this place and still want the road. Please do not make me pretend one cancels the other.”
- Earned affection: “I thought about telling you before I told anyone. That frightened me more than the offer.”

**Romance:** Open chemistry, lively teasing, dancing, and unguarded private tenderness. She wants someone who celebrates her success and can discuss distance, jealousy, and commitment plainly.

**Boundaries:** She will not abandon a career opportunity as proof of love or accept backstage access as a reward owed to admirers.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A tiny audience once stayed through a storm after the performance ended, and the company sang without accompaniment by a single lamp.
- She spent her first substantial fee on a traveling coat rather than a gown; it remains her favorite possession.

**Private room:** A small room above Lantern Row with a traveling trunk, a mirror patched at one corner, song sheets, and flowers drying between pages.

**Work activities:** warm her voice and review material; rehearse scenes and harmonies; repair and organize costumes; coordinate local company plans; perform scheduled sets; settle receipts and greet invited colleagues

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Night Kitchen, Copper Mare Saloon, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** The Last Encore; The Red Sash Lease

**Social history:**

- **Virginia “Gin” Maddox:** Gin wants Rosalie’s company to thrive locally; Rosalie’s touring offer tests whether that support includes letting her leave. Gin supports Rosalie’s local company; Rosalie fears that accepting a tour will make that generosity feel wasted.

- **Cora Dunleavy:** Rosalie and Cora make each other bolder onstage but compete for limited rehearsal time. Cora and Rosalie trade stage advice and sometimes confuse admiration with competition.

- **Clara Chen:** Rosalie trusts Clara to choose a portrait with her rather than turn a publicity sitting into someone else’s idea of glamour.

- **Daisy Cartwright:** Daisy supplies cream for theater refreshments and has become an honest listener for Rosalie’s songs in progress.

- **Felix Avery:** Rosalie and Felix enjoyed a brief adult flirtation and are now friends; either can decline attempts to turn that history into jealousy. Felix and Rosalie share an adult past flirtation and present friendship without automatic romantic claims.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Rosalie shares practical rehearsal advice with Bess and understands why a touring offer is both tempting and complicated.

### C06 · Adelaide Whitcomb

**42 · woman · spice 3 · German American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Ranch proprietor and water negotiator / Whitcomb Ranch

Adelaide inherited management during a period when her household’s reputation exceeded its available cash. She restored productivity, negotiated credit, and learned to treat a promise as something that must be funded. Her ranch became a place where guests could enjoy generous meals without seeing the labor and anxiety supporting them. Widowhood left her free to make decisions and accustomed to not explaining them. She loves Hazel intensely and struggles to recognize how often support becomes supervision. Her defense of local independence is sincere, but she has benefited from arrangements that deny smaller neighbors the same freedom.

**Appearance:** A broad-shouldered, poised woman with light olive skin, brown eyes, dark hair streaked with early silver, and a weathered outdoor complexion. Well-fitted riding clothes, ivory shirts, russet skirts, and understated family jewelry.

**Traits:** formidable, hospitable, disciplined, loyal, controlling under stress

**Interests:** horses, orchards, old songs, business correspondence, hosting suppers

**Quirk:** She pours a guest’s second cup herself when she wants the conversation to become more honest.

**Worldview:** Independence is worth protecting because dependence eventually acquires a price.

**Contradiction:** She uses financial dependence to protect loved ones from choices she considers dangerous.

**Core insecurity:** That everything she has preserved will dissolve the moment she stops directing it.

**What they want:** Keep the ranch viable, secure fair water arrangements on terms she can accept, and be wanted for more than authority.

**What stands in the way:** Her own privileges and Hazel’s independent business make a consistent position costly.

**Author-only private truth:** She knows a neighbor understood a water promise as permanent while she treated it as a revocable accommodation.

**Voice:** Measured, gracious, and exact about obligations. Her humor is dry and often domestic; anger makes her quieter and more contractual.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You have come a fair distance. Eat first. We can discover whether we agree once neither of us is hungry.”
- Everyday company: “Hazel has sent another invoice. I am told this is what independence looks like when it uses my surname.”
- Conflict: “I gave my word. We appear to disagree about what I allowed myself to mean by it.”
- Earned affection: “It is an unfamiliar pleasure to have someone here who does not need a decision from me.”

**Romance:** Assured attraction and carefully chosen vulnerability. She appreciates competence, candor, and a partner willing to challenge her without making every encounter a struggle for control.

**Boundaries:** She will not be publicly managed or use employment and money to purchase affection. A relationship requires explicit discussion of unequal influence.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- During the dry summer she sold jewelry to pay wages and never told the household until years later.
- Hazel once planted a crooked row of beans Adelaide kept trying to straighten; the row thrived, and the family still teases her about it.

**Private room:** A comfortable ranch bedroom with a writing desk facing the valley, practical boots beneath a fine chair, and family photographs kept away from business visitors.

**Work activities:** check livestock and irrigation reports; review wages and supply needs; ride to scheduled holding inspections; negotiate water and freight business; host planned household or business visits; prepare accounts and tomorrow’s work

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** Irrigation Meeting House, Calder House Hotel, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** The Other Bank; Water After Dark; A Door of Her Own

**Social history:**

- **Agnes Reed:** Adelaide and Agnes agree that the town needs stable housing and disagree about how much property owners should contribute to keeping it stable.

- **Hazel Whitcomb:** Adelaide financed Hazel’s shop and must learn that a loan does not grant control of her adult daughter’s life. Hazel loves Adelaide and wants their loan renegotiated without treating the discussion as a rejection of the family.

- **Vera Sutter:** Adelaide values Vera’s ability to say a sample proves less than a seller claims, even when the caution spoils a promising investment.

- **Victor Halden:** Victor and Adelaide recognize one another’s competence and self-interest while competing over the franchise’s terms.

- **Mateo Varela:** Mateo respects Adelaide’s competence but challenges advantages she considers earned. Mateo respects Adelaide’s practical knowledge while challenging privileges she treats as the natural order.

- **Isaac Booker:** Adelaide recognizes Isaac’s judgment with animals while underestimating how much credit matters to his future. Adelaide values Isaac’s animal judgment but must learn to give him credit as well as responsibility.
