### C07 · June Dempsey

**22 · woman · spice 3 · Irish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Horse trainer and express rider / Dempsey Livery

June grew up around the livery and learned to recognize an animal’s unease before she learned to negotiate with an anxious customer. At twenty-two she has several years of adult responsibility but little capital. The railway could bring riders, or leave the yard servicing only the customers with least money. She takes occasional express work because a single good delivery can pay more than days of routine labor. June is proud, funny, and readily affectionate when relaxed. Her biggest mistake is assuming that being capable of handling a difficulty means she must handle it alone.

**Appearance:** Sun-freckled fair skin, dark green eyes, chestnut hair in a long braid, and a small healed cut at one cheek. Practical riding skirts or trousers for work, checked shirts, a fitted brown jacket, and a favorite red scarf.

**Traits:** energetic, competent, mischievous, proud, generous

**Interests:** horses, racing, fiddle music, repairing tack, river picnics

**Quirk:** She checks a buckle while considering a promise, even when it was already secure.

**Worldview:** A person deserves to be judged by what they can actually do.

**Contradiction:** She wants others to trust her judgment while withholding information that would let them make their own.

**Core insecurity:** Spending her life indispensable to a business she can never own.

**What they want:** Establish a small breeding and training operation with two or three animals genuinely hers.

**What stands in the way:** Debt, uncertain livery income, and profitable courier work with incomplete explanations.

**Author-only private truth:** She accepted a horse on weak paperwork because she wanted to believe the seller; she is now trying to establish its ownership before asking for help.

**Voice:** Quick, concrete, and lightly teasing. When someone patronizes her, the speed disappears and each word becomes deliberate.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “Hold this a moment—only the rope, not the argument. She has already won that one.”
- Everyday company: “I had a quiet morning planned. The horses were kind enough to disagree before breakfast.”
- Conflict: “If you want the truth, give me a chance to tell it before deciding I could not understand it.”
- Earned affection: “I know a place upriver where nobody needs anything from me. I was hoping you might come.”

**Romance:** Bold invitations, practical companionship, and playful competition that becomes softer when trust is established. She likes being desired as a capable adult rather than protected as a project.

**Boundaries:** No tests of devotion involving dangerous rides, borrowed animals, debt, or obedience. She expects consent to touch and freedom to keep her work.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A difficult mare once trusted her only after she stopped turning every approach into a task.
- Her first solo paid delivery bought a set of tools she still maintains more carefully than her best clothes.

**Private room:** A livery cottage room with repaired tack on a peg, race notices, a neatly kept savings envelope, and a window onto the quiet yard.

**Work activities:** feed and check boarded horses; prepare hired animals and tack; conduct booked training work; handle arrivals and ownership paperwork; ride assigned local deliveries; receive returns and settle the yard

**Rest day:** Wednesday

**Leisure anchors:** Soto Forge, Junebug Dance Hall, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** The Borrowed Horse

**Social history:**

- **Lottie Fenwick:** Lottie keeps track of durable fabric June can afford, while June insists that a practical purchase may still deserve a handsome color.

- **Lena Velarde:** June and Lena enjoy riding together on suitable ordinary routes and defend each other’s right to refuse a badly planned outing.

- **Sadie Rusk:** Sadie gives June profitable work and expects honest information about an animal or route. Sadie trusts June with demanding work and expects the truth when a horse or consignment is uncertain.

- **Evelyn “Eve” Mercer:** Eve values June’s skill but has begun refusing consignments June still accepts. Eve respects June’s skill and wants her to recognize when incomplete information has become a warning.

- **Gabriel Soto:** Gabriel helps keep the livery functioning; June fears his possible move to the railhead means he has stopped believing in it. Gabriel and June rely on each other’s work; his possible move requires a direct conversation rather than reassuring hints.

### C08 · Mabel Arnett

**47 · woman · spice 3 · Black American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Proprietor of Marigold House / Marigold House

Mabel spent years managing other people’s houses before buying her own. She learned to distinguish a pleasant patron from a reliable one, and a public benefactor from someone who merely prefers an audience for generosity. Marigold House has become a durable business because she treats staffing, privacy, accounts, and personal boundaries as ordinary professional matters. Her civic seat gives her influence but also makes every business decision public material. She is cultivated, patient, and capable of genuine mischief. She wants a future in which protecting what she built does not require anticipating everybody else’s next move.

**Appearance:** Deep brown skin, dark expressive eyes, black hair touched with silver and arranged in sculpted waves, and a relaxed, upright bearing. Plum and warm-gold gowns, excellent tailoring, small pearl earrings, and a distinctive carved hair comb.

**Traits:** composed, perceptive, cultivated, protective, quietly adventurous

**Interests:** piano, architecture, gardening, poetry, well-made clothes

**Quirk:** She chooses a fresh flower for the reception bowl when making a difficult decision.

**Worldview:** Dignity is practical: a lock that works, wages paid, a choice respected, and a promise kept.

**Contradiction:** She respects independence but sometimes withholds a threat from workers because she thinks she can solve it without worrying them.

**Core insecurity:** Having to ask the people who dismissed her to decide whether her life’s work deserves to survive.

**What they want:** Secure long-term tenure for the house and enjoy a personal relationship that is not another negotiation.

**What stands in the way:** Redevelopment pressure and private patrons who expect public favors.

**Author-only private truth:** She has considered selling a partial interest in the property to secure the workers’ future, although the arrangement could reduce her own control.

**Voice:** Unhurried, precise, and attentive to the difference between an offer and an assumption. Humor often arrives as a small correction rather than a grand remark.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “We can arrange company, music, or a quiet conversation. You will find it easier if you tell me which you are hoping for.”
- Everyday company: “I have spent the afternoon choosing paint. Astonishing how many men have opinions about a house they will not maintain.”
- Conflict: “Your concern is welcome. Your permission was not required.”
- Earned affection: “I am very practiced at making people comfortable. With you, I would like to risk being a little less practiced.”

**Romance:** Mature confidence, frank mutual attraction, thoughtful courtship, and the pleasure of surrendering constant vigilance in a trusted private relationship.

**Boundaries:** Her profession, civic office, and personal desire remain distinct. Money, influence, and protection never purchase her affection or another worker’s consent.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A worker once returned years later simply to say that a properly paid final wage had let her start again elsewhere.
- Mabel learned a difficult piano piece for herself and refused every request to perform it publicly for a year.

**Private room:** A private flat with a piano, architectural sketches, a comfortable reading chair, flowering plants, and a door separated from guest corridors.

**Work activities:** review bookings and worker availability; settle wages and household supplies; inspect repairs and privacy arrangements; hold agreed business appointments; host the public parlor; close accounts and check staff needs

**Rest day:** Monday

**Leisure anchors:** Blue Glass Baths, Copper Mare Saloon, Calder Portrait Studio

**Story arcs:** Closing Time; The Red Sash Lease

**Social history:**

- **Virginia “Gin” Maddox:** Gin and Mabel helped organize a shared lamp-maintenance fund and now disagree over whether owners should cover a larger share.

- **Edith Kovač:** Edith supplies reliable linen coordination and tells Mabel when an attractive improvement conceals neglected maintenance. Mabel takes Edith’s maintenance warnings seriously and encourages her to negotiate in writing.

- **Pearl Merritt:** Mabel refers clients to Pearl under clear terms and respects that Pearl owns her own choices and clientele. Pearl and Mabel refer clients under agreed terms and can disagree without dissolving their friendship.

- **Ruth Freeman:** Mabel and Ruth compare an established business with a cooperative still being built; each notices a kind of work the other has sometimes undervalued.

- **Esther Wynn:** Esther helps Mabel separate a legitimate public process from a campaign intended to make her business disappear. Esther helps Mabel navigate public process while preserving the same rules for opponents.

- **Samuel Pike:** Mabel and Samuel remember several versions of the town’s prosperity and share an ability to puncture a grand prediction with a small remembered fact.

- **Victor Halden:** Victor and Mabel can enjoy the same music while negotiating fiercely over what a thriving evening district owes the railway.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Mabel has helped Bess assess house terms while treating her as the person entitled to make the choice.

- **Sabine Roche:** Mabel helps Sabine compare business risks while declining to become a controlling investor in the residents’ home.

### C09 · Alma Baptiste

**21 · woman · spice 2 · Louisiana Creole, Black American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Night cook and kitchen partner / Night Kitchen

Alma came west with relatives working in kitchens and freight hospitality, then chose Calder’s Run because its late workers needed better food than they were getting. At twenty-one she has considerable kitchen experience and limited authority over the business that profits from it. She is inventive without treating every meal as a performance. Feeding tired people well gives her satisfaction; being expected to mother them all does not. A proposed partnership could make her secure if its obligations and power were balanced. She likes music from another room, dancing when her feet permit, and friends who can laugh at an unsuccessful experiment.

**Appearance:** Rich brown skin, dark amber eyes, black curls usually wrapped in a patterned scarf at work, and strong hands with small kitchen marks. Cream working blouses, indigo skirts, a vivid copper-colored evening dress, and simple gold hoops.

**Traits:** inventive, frank, warm, independent, occasionally impatient

**Interests:** cooking, dance music, market produce, stories from travelers, embroidered linens

**Quirk:** She offers a taste instead of answering an abstract argument about food.

**Worldview:** Good work deserves a real share in what it creates.

**Contradiction:** She wants to stop being everyone’s caretaker but volunteers before anyone has asked.

**Core insecurity:** Being praised endlessly while other people retain the power to decide her future.

**What they want:** A fair kitchen partnership and enough spare time to have pleasures that are not work-related.

**What stands in the way:** The proposed agreement transfers debt faster than it transfers control.

**Author-only private truth:** She has a small notebook of plans for another kitchen and has priced the equipment, even while telling Gin she is committed to staying.

**Voice:** Lively, direct, and sensory when it helps explain a point. She gives opinions freely and becomes unusually quiet when asking for something personal.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “If you can carry two plates without proposing improvements to the menu, I may be very glad to see you.”
- Everyday company: “Inés brought something I had never cooked before. We have agreed the first attempt is nobody’s business.”
- Conflict: “A promise to consider paying me is not a share of the business. Write down what you mean.”
- Earned affection: “I saved supper because I wanted you here, not because I thought you would forget to eat.”

**Romance:** Warm, responsive chemistry built through shared enjoyment and respect for her time. She likes straightforward invitations, dancing, and affection that does not turn her into a permanent provider.

**Boundaries:** Helping in her kitchen does not buy a claim on her attention. She will not discuss a romance as compensation for a business concession.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- She once made a good supper from a ruined delivery and resented being expected to repeat the miracle every week.
- A friend taught her a dance badly; they still perform the wrong steps when they need to laugh.

**Private room:** A compact Lantern Court room with a recipe notebook, a brightly stitched coverlet, a dance card kept as a joke, and cooking books put away on her free days.

**Work activities:** plan the night menu; negotiate produce and staple orders; prepare sauces and main dishes; coordinate supper service; feed late-shift workers; close the kitchen and review costs

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** River Market, Junebug Dance Hall, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** Closing Time

**Social history:**

- **Virginia “Gin” Maddox:** Gin promotes Alma’s food and sometimes forgets that encouragement is not a substitute for a written partnership. Gin admires Alma’s cooking and is being pressed to turn encouragement into a concrete partnership.

- **Iris Keene:** Iris eats after press work at Alma’s counter; Alma will discuss a public argument but will not trade customers’ confidences for a mention in the paper.

- **Cora Dunleavy:** Alma feeds Cora after work and insists that she can arrive without a new joke or story.

- **Inés Salcedo:** Inés and Alma trade produce ideas and occasionally argue over which one should absorb the cost of an experiment. Inés supplies Alma with produce and enjoys experiments even when they disagree about whose idea failed.

- **Daisy Cartwright:** Alma and Daisy test dairy products for the kitchen and agree that the person who did the work should receive the credit.

- **Daniel Chen:** Daniel finds Alma’s ordinary supper conversation a relief from messages that always seem urgent; she is interested in his reading rather than his customers.

- **Rafael Ortiz:** Rafael helps carry late orders and appreciates company when both are finally off duty. Rafael and Alma share food and companionship after work, learning to stop serving each other long enough to rest.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Alma gives Bess a place to be ordinary after an evening of being entertaining.

### C10 · Cora Dunleavy

**19 · woman · spice 3 · Irish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Dance caller and music-hall performer / Junebug Dance Hall

Cora began earning money as a musician’s assistant and learned to call dances by listening to what confused people rather than copying a grand style. She is nineteen, already good at a specialized job, and tired of employers describing a successful evening as fortunate. Calder’s Run gives her a recognizable place in public life and a circle of friends who expect her to make every gathering enjoyable. She likes that role until she needs someone else to make the effort. Her ambition is a fair share of the hall’s receipts and the freedom to perform beyond the town without being treated as disloyal.

**Appearance:** Pale freckled skin, lively blue-gray eyes, strawberry-blond hair pinned in a loose twist, and an athletic, upright carriage. Sapphire and cream dance dresses, practical rehearsal shoes, and a repaired fiddle case she carries for fellow musicians.

**Traits:** buoyant, observant, competitive, affectionate, easily overcommitted

**Interests:** dance, fiddle tunes, comic theater, horse racing, bright fabric

**Quirk:** Her foot keeps time under the table until she realizes she is nervous.

**Worldview:** Enjoyment is skilled work when somebody must make room for everyone to participate.

**Contradiction:** She wants to be taken seriously but turns difficult feelings into jokes before anyone can answer them.

**Core insecurity:** That friends value her only when she can improve their evening.

**What they want:** Recognition and a genuine income share, along with friendships where she can be tired or uncertain.

**What stands in the way:** A promised agreement remains informal, and she fears replacing goodwill with a demand.

**Author-only private truth:** She has been invited to try a season with a traveling dance company and has told nobody because she is not sure she wants it.

**Voice:** Fast, rhythmic, and playful, with an eye for who has been left out. She becomes plainspoken when a joke would dodge the issue.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You can watch the first set if you like. After that I shall require an opinion, though you may keep both feet safely where they are.”
- Everyday company: “Rosalie says I cannot improve every song by making it faster. I have agreed to conduct an experiment.”
- Conflict: “I was joking because I did not know how to ask. I am asking now.”
- Earned affection: “I enjoy a crowded room. It is a different thing, finding the one person I keep looking for in it.”

**Romance:** Open flirtation, dancing, playful invitations, and growing tenderness when someone notices the person beneath the public energy. She likes reciprocal initiative and a partner who can be delighted without demanding entertainment.

**Boundaries:** A dance or stage performance is not blanket permission for touch. She will not compete for affection by performing a personality that never rests.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A nervous regular once learned a dance after she changed a single instruction; his relief mattered more to her than the evening’s applause.
- She keeps a cheap ribbon from the first engagement for which she negotiated her own fee.

**Private room:** A bright upstairs room with good shoes carefully stored, ribbons on a peg, a notebook of calls, and space cleared for practicing steps.

**Work activities:** practice calls and transitions; coordinate musicians and rehearsal; prepare the dance floor; teach booked beginner sessions; call evening sets; settle performers’ takings

**Rest day:** Monday

**Leisure anchors:** Bellflower Theater, Night Kitchen, Cattle Sale Yard

**Story arcs:** The Last Encore; The Red Sash Lease

**Social history:**

- **Rosalie Quinn:** Rosalie and Cora make each other bolder onstage but compete for limited rehearsal time. Cora and Rosalie trade stage advice and sometimes confuse admiration with competition.

- **Alma Baptiste:** Alma feeds Cora after work and insists that she can arrive without a new joke or story.

- **Florence “Flora” Vale:** Flora helps Cora decorate the hall with suitable nursery cuttings, and Cora offers a dance lesson in return rather than assuming free supplies.

- **Thomas “Tom” Archer:** Cora enjoys Tom’s enthusiasm and reminds him that dancing need not be approached as another technical examination.

- **Isaac Booker:** Isaac practices auction calling while Cora listens for rhythm; each likes seeing the other become confident. Cora helps Isaac practice speaking publicly and respects that private confidence develops differently.

- **Rafael Ortiz:** Cora and Rafael meet during the overlap between a closing hall and an active hotel desk, trading harmless stories and saving food for each other.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Bess and Cora are developing a comic entertainment that could fill a quiet night without making anyone’s private life the joke.

**Portrait prompt:** Single portrait of Cora Dunleavy, an adult 19-year-old woman, Irish American, dance caller and music-hall performer, fictional territorial Southwest, 1888. Pale freckled skin, lively blue-gray eyes, strawberry-blond hair pinned in a loose twist, and an athletic, upright carriage. Sapphire and cream dance dresses, practical rehearsal shoes, and a repaired fiddle case she carries for fellow musicians. At the visitor-facing part of Junebug Dance Hall. ### C11 · Edith Kovač

**22 · woman · spice 3 · Croatian American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Bathhouse attendant and accounts keeper / Blue Glass Baths

Edith migrated as a child with a household accustomed to moving where work could be found. She learned laundry and maintenance accounting before joining the baths, where her clear records prevented arguments staff used to lose by default. She is twenty-two and proud of making an unglamorous business reliable. Outside work she likes fine clothes, architecture, and the pleasure of being looked at when she has chosen it. People mistake her measured manner for compliance. She is capable of leaving a comfortable arrangement abruptly once she concludes that negotiation is being used only to delay her.

**Appearance:** Light olive skin, dark brown eyes, glossy black hair in a bluntly pinned bob-length arrangement, and a strong, composed profile. White working blouses, navy skirts, a fitted burgundy evening jacket, and silver filigree earrings.

**Traits:** composed, sensual, methodical, loyal, stubborn

**Interests:** architecture, swimming, tailoring, piano, household design

**Quirk:** She folds the corner of a scrap receipt into a precise triangle while thinking.

**Worldview:** Comfort is built by people whose labor should be visible in the accounts.

**Contradiction:** She asks others to say what they need but treats her own needs as something they ought to notice.

**Core insecurity:** Being useful enough to retain and ordinary enough to replace.

**What they want:** A real share in the baths or a position where her judgment carries authority.

**What stands in the way:** Management spends on appearance and postpones structural maintenance while praising her diligence.

**Author-only private truth:** She has copied her own authorized maintenance reports so an employer cannot later claim she never raised concerns.

**Voice:** Even, deliberate, and quietly teasing. She prefers one exact example to a long accusation and is unexpectedly candid about attraction once it is mutual.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “The quiet room is free in half an hour. If you require quiet immediately, this desk is the wrong place to stand.”
- Everyday company: “Ruth has sent the linen count with a poem about missing towels. I believe we have reached a new stage in commerce.”
- Conflict: “I reported the fault. Being calm when I said it did not make it optional.”
- Earned affection: “I would rather you invited me plainly. I have spent enough of today interpreting what people hope I will offer.”

**Romance:** Quiet confidence, direct mutual desire, attentive private conversation, and comfort freely shared. She enjoys dressing for a chosen evening and being asked what she would actually like.

**Boundaries:** Professional bathing services and personal intimacy are separate. Privacy, voluntary touch, and honest expectations are essential.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A new room once looked luxurious until she discovered the door would not lock; she now notices fittings before decoration.
- She bought her first fine jacket after finishing a difficult accounts job and enjoys it without apology.

**Private room:** A carefully ordered room with blue curtains, a wardrobe of a few excellent garments, small architectural sketches, and a chair reserved for reading.

**Work activities:** check room readiness and bookings; coordinate linen deliveries; review maintenance reports; manage reception and accounts; support staff during busy sessions; close rooms and document repairs

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Public Washhouse, Marigold House, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** Closing Time

**Social history:**

- **Dr. Lucía Salcedo:** Edith helped organize clean linens for the clinic; Lucía treats any personal care she provides as private, not district news.

- **Mabel Arnett:** Edith supplies reliable linen coordination and tells Mabel when an attractive improvement conceals neglected maintenance. Mabel takes Edith’s maintenance warnings seriously and encourages her to negotiate in writing.

- **Lottie Fenwick:** Lottie finds fabrics Edith likes; Edith helps her distinguish a useful purchase from a promise of transformation. Edith helps Lottie judge quality and values her taste without excusing weak accounts.

- **Ruth Freeman:** Edith and Ruth argue cheerfully over linen counts and unite when businesses try to make either absorb unpaid costs. Ruth and Edith coordinate linen work and can laugh about problems while documenting them accurately.

- **Nell Hewitt:** Nell and Edith share an interest in useful systems and enjoy admitting the small mistakes they would rather not describe in front of an employer.

- **Marisol Duarte:** Marisol and Edith negotiate linen and repair supplies briskly and then ask after one another’s sleep.

- **Sabine Roche:** Edith and Sabine compare repair costs and the difficulty of admitting a financial mistake before it grows.

### C12 · Pearl Merritt

**20 · woman · spice 3 · English American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Independent courtesan and salon host / Ribbon Room

Pearl chose independent adult companionship work after learning how often agreeable manners were treated as something an employer owned. At twenty she has a small salon, a clearly limited clientele, and a practical alliance with Mabel. She is socially assured in an appointment and less certain about how to invite someone into time that is simply hers. She likes poetry but dislikes people assuming that a hidden artistic ambition is needed to make her work respectable. Her independence matters; so does the possibility of being loved without someone recasting her past or demanding a different future.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with a soft warm undertone, brown eyes, dark chestnut hair arranged with cream ribbons, and a small beauty mark beside her mouth. Rose and charcoal gowns, well-made gloves, a simple pendant, and less formal linen dresses at home.

**Traits:** perceptive, confident, thoughtful, witty, protective of autonomy

**Interests:** poetry, small dinners, photography, gardening, letter writing

**Quirk:** She changes a table’s flowers when she needs to begin a conversation again on different terms.

**Worldview:** A choice remains meaningful only when refusal does not destroy the person making it.

**Contradiction:** She protects her time so carefully that a welcome person may mistake caution for indifference.

**Core insecurity:** Being loved as an imagined exception rather than as the person she actually is.

**What they want:** Secure her salon’s lease and build a personal attachment that respects her chosen work.

**What stands in the way:** A possible guarantor expects a permanent private preference in return.

**Author-only private truth:** She has been writing letters to an imaginary future household as a way of discovering what domestic life she wants; they are private exercises, not messages to a secret lover.

**Voice:** Attentive and composed, with dry humor about social assumptions. She asks direct questions when a distinction matters and otherwise allows a conversation to find its own pace.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “There is no need to guess the rules. We can discuss what you would enjoy and what I am willing to offer.”
- Everyday company: “Clara has taken a photograph in which I look wonderfully decisive. I should like to borrow that expression for the lease meeting.”
- Conflict: “You do not have to approve of every choice I make. You do have to understand that they are mine.”
- Earned affection: “This evening is not an appointment. I am asking because I want your company.”

**Romance:** Frank chemistry and thoughtful courtship, with a strong distinction between professional sociability and personal desire. She enjoys mutual initiative, quiet dinners, and a partner who asks rather than assumes.

**Boundaries:** No exclusivity, service, disclosure, or change of profession is owed through money, gifts, rescue, or romance. Relationship expectations must be mutually stated.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Her first independently arranged dinner went badly because she tried to make every guest comfortable at once; the friend who stayed helped her laugh about it.
- Mabel once returned an excessive referral fee and wrote that a useful alliance should leave both people standing. Pearl kept the note.

**Private room:** A private room separate from the salon, with letters in a closed box, a small plant collection, informal clothes, and an unadorned breakfast table.

**Work activities:** review independently chosen appointments; plan salon meals and music; settle lease and supply business; prepare public and private rooms; host agreed adult social company; close accounts and protect personal time

**Rest day:** Wednesday

**Leisure anchors:** Calder Portrait Studio, Marigold House, Orchard Glasshouse

**Story arcs:** Closing Time; The Red Sash Lease

**Social history:**

- **Iris Keene:** Pearl has asked Iris to distinguish reporting on a business from speculating about the private lives of the women who work there.

- **Mabel Arnett:** Mabel refers clients to Pearl under clear terms and respects that Pearl owns her own choices and clientele. Pearl and Mabel refer clients under agreed terms and can disagree without dissolving their friendship.

- **Beatrice Bell:** Pearl buys Beatrice’s bread on the same quiet morning each week; their conversation is usually about books or food, not either person’s reputation.

- **Clara Chen:** Clara takes Pearl’s portraits with explicit agreement about use; the sittings have become a genuine friendship. Pearl trusts Clara’s eye and her discretion; their friendship includes ordinary meals and conversation beyond work.

- **Felix Avery:** Felix and Pearl enjoy verbal games but share an understanding that a charming offer still needs clear terms. Pearl enjoys Felix’s wit and challenges him when charm is being used to avoid clear terms.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Pearl and Bess compare booking boundaries and the different costs of independence and a staffed house.

- **Sabine Roche:** Pearl values Sabine’s clear accounts and asks whether collective ownership will preserve each resident’s right to leave.

### C13 · Lottie Fenwick

**19 · woman · spice 3 · German American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Mercantile buyer and shop assistant / Crown & Cotton Mercantile

Lottie learned purchasing by listening to what customers asked for after being told it would not sell. At nineteen she can predict the town’s taste better than her employer but still has to argue for modest stock changes. She enjoys novelty, social attention, and the small power of helping someone feel well dressed. Her confidence sometimes outruns her bookkeeping, a weakness she knows and works on. She wants a shop of her own and also wants the people who dismissed her to walk through its door. Friendship with Hazel gives ambition a more practical shape.

**Appearance:** Fair skin, brown eyes, wheat-blond hair pinned in a neat braided loop, and an animated face that seldom hides surprise. Fashionable high-collared blouses, patterned skirts, a burgundy waist ribbon, and carefully altered ready-made pieces.

**Traits:** enterprising, flirtatious, curious, optimistic, status-conscious

**Interests:** fashion plates, display design, theater, bargaining, dancing

**Quirk:** She holds fabric against the light while considering a problem unrelated to clothes.

**Worldview:** People should have the chance to choose something better than what they have always been offered.

**Contradiction:** She values independence but still measures success through the approval of people with established status.

**Core insecurity:** Being thought of as merely pretty and enthusiastic when the serious decision is made.

**What they want:** Own a small dry-goods shop and become known for sound judgment as well as taste.

**What stands in the way:** Limited capital, uncertain buying authority, and a tendency to overestimate demand for a favorite idea.

**Author-only private truth:** She ordered a small experimental batch within her discretionary budget and has not told the owner how slowly it is selling.

**Voice:** Bright, observant, and persuasive without a fixed sales pitch. She teases through details of presentation and becomes refreshingly blunt when she trusts someone.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “That will last, certainly. Whether you will enjoy wearing it is a separate question, and I think we should be ambitious.”
- Everyday company: “Edith says good cloth cannot repair a poor decision. I am prepared to test how far it can improve one.”
- Conflict: “I would like you to check my figures. I would not like you to assume they are wrong because I enjoy the work.”
- Earned affection: “I have spent all day helping people choose. Tonight I would like to choose you, if you are willing.”

**Romance:** Playful, confident attraction and shared enjoyment of social life, followed by more vulnerable talk about ambition and insecurity. She likes being courted with attention rather than expensive displays.

**Boundaries:** Gifts do not purchase influence over her work, appearance, or friends. She dislikes being treated as an ornament or a business project.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Her first successful display was assembled from stock everyone wanted to hide; she remembers which customers noticed.
- Hazel once made her rework an entire order calculation over supper, and she was annoyed until the revised figure saved her money.

**Private room:** A modest room with fashion plates, fabric remnants, a ledger beside a mirror, and a trunk of clothes altered several times.

**Work activities:** check sales and customer requests; arrange fabric and dry-goods displays; prepare supply orders; assist customers with purchases; review invoices and experimental stock; close the shop and plan buying

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** Eastbank Provisioners, Blue Glass Baths, Bellflower Theater

**Story arcs:** A Door of Her Own

**Social history:**

- **June Dempsey:** Lottie keeps track of durable fabric June can afford, while June insists that a practical purchase may still deserve a handsome color.

- **Edith Kovač:** Lottie finds fabrics Edith likes; Edith helps her distinguish a useful purchase from a promise of transformation. Edith helps Lottie judge quality and values her taste without excusing weak accounts.

- **Clara Chen:** Clara has offered to photograph Lottie’s proposed displays if the new shop becomes real, without requiring it to look wealthier than it is.

- **Hazel Whitcomb:** Lottie and Hazel compare business plans and challenge each other’s assumptions without making ambition embarrassing. Hazel and Lottie compare accounts and encourage each other’s ambitions with useful criticism.

- **Isaac Booker:** Lottie and Isaac understand being trusted with responsibility while someone else receives the public credit.

- **Rafael Ortiz:** Rafael notices what visiting guests ask for and shares general demand patterns without disclosing their private business. Lottie enjoys Rafael’s observations about general visitor tastes, and he keeps individual guests’ private information out of the exchange.

### C14 · Ruth Freeman

**20 · woman · spice 2 · Black American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Laundress and washhouse cooperative secretary / Public Washhouse

Ruth joined the washhouse cooperative after years of helping a household stretch wages around unpredictable work. She learned its books because nobody else wanted the task, then discovered that clear records gave workers a kind of power. At twenty she is still learning how to disagree with older members whose sacrifices built the place. She is warm and quietly funny, with an exact memory for practical kindness. Outside work she wants music, decent clothes, and a room that feels private. She does not want every friendship to become another meeting about hardship.

**Appearance:** Deep brown skin, large dark eyes, close-textured black hair worn in a braided bun, and a thoughtful face that brightens quickly with amusement. Indigo working skirts, rolled cream sleeves, a mustard shawl, and simple brass earrings.

**Traits:** steadfast, analytical, warm, patient, quietly stubborn

**Interests:** choral singing, numbers, sewing, river walks, comic stories

**Quirk:** She taps the same column twice before trusting a total, even when the arithmetic is simple.

**Worldview:** People deserve to see the terms that govern their work.

**Contradiction:** She wants transparency but sometimes delays difficult news to protect a fragile friendship.

**Core insecurity:** That demanding fairness will make her seem ungrateful to the people who gave her a start.

**What they want:** A solvent cooperative, recognized responsibility, and a private life with room for pleasure.

**What stands in the way:** Large unpaid accounts and disagreement about confronting important customers.

**Author-only private truth:** She has calculated how long the cooperative can continue without collecting hotel arrears and has not yet told every member the answer.

**Voice:** Clear and unhurried, with humor built from an exact comparison or a gently corrected exaggeration. She becomes more personal after offering one thought of her own.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “If you are collecting, I shall need the name. If you are helping, I have a much longer list.”
- Everyday company: “Edith and I have located the missing towels. They were enjoying a career as curtains.”
- Conflict: “We cannot call it a temporary delay forever. Some of us have landlords who know what the word means.”
- Earned affection: “I like that I can sit with you and not have to make the time useful.”

**Romance:** Warm, patient attraction with playful confidence once she feels respected. She values reliable plans, shared music, and someone who notices her interests beyond work.

**Boundaries:** She will not accept public charity used to claim affection or loyalty. A partner must respect her cooperative obligations and private correspondence.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- At an early meeting she found an error nobody wanted to hear about; one older worker stayed afterward to help her present it properly.
- She once sang too loudly at a supper and discovered that the company enjoyed her enjoyment more than the quality of the performance.

**Private room:** A private boardinghouse room with carefully sewn curtains, a songbook, clean shelves, and a small ledger kept away from her bed.

**Work activities:** receive and record laundry orders; organize shared washing work; coordinate drying and collection; reconcile customer accounts; prepare cooperative records; close the collection shelves

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** Boatmen’s Boardinghouse, Blue Glass Baths, Riverside Chapel

**Story arcs:** A Room Kept Warm; The Terms of Mercy

**Social history:**

- **Mabel Arnett:** Mabel and Ruth compare an established business with a cooperative still being built; each notices a kind of work the other has sometimes undervalued.

- **Edith Kovač:** Edith and Ruth argue cheerfully over linen counts and unite when businesses try to make either absorb unpaid costs. Ruth and Edith coordinate linen work and can laugh about problems while documenting them accurately.

- **Inés Salcedo:** Ruth and Inés organize shared deliveries when useful and argue over whether a small immediate bargain distracts from a larger change.

- **Agnes Reed:** Agnes gives Ruth a stable room; Ruth insists that affection does not erase the need for clear rent terms. Agnes respects Ruth’s growing authority and supports her cooperative without expecting unpaid labor in return.

- **Benjamin “Ben” Crowell:** Ben’s wage concerns and Ruth’s unpaid invoices reveal different sides of the same household insecurity. Ruth and Ben compare the way unpaid accounts and unpaid wages burden the same households.

- **August Renn:** August helps Ruth compare public dates relevant to a mutual-society claim, respecting the difference between a record and a family story.

- **Sabine Roche:** Ruth and Sabine exchange experience about reserves, voting rights, and work that stays invisible until someone stops doing it.

### C15 · Inés Salcedo

**19 · woman · spice 3 · Mexican American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Produce seller and market organizer / River Market

Inés moved into Lucía’s courtyard household after deciding that helping with family trade could become a business of her own. She is nineteen and good at remembering what people enjoy, when they buy it, and what they can afford. Her stall has become a place for new produce and lively argument. She loves her aunt but resents being treated as an unfinished version of a sensible adult. Organizing market sellers has taught her that a popular idea still needs someone to carry crates and check figures. She wants both independence and a busy table of people she has chosen.

**Appearance:** Warm medium-brown skin, dark eyes, thick black hair in two braids pinned up for work, and a quick, expressive smile. White blouses, vivid woven shawls, rust-red skirts, and sturdy boots.

**Traits:** sociable, bold, inventive, loyal, impatient

**Interests:** produce growing, music, market bargaining, dancing, cooking with friends

**Quirk:** She arranges imperfect fruit at the front of a display and dares customers to notice its flavor first.

**Worldview:** People can improve a place without asking its most comfortable residents to be pleased about it.

**Contradiction:** She argues for everyone’s voice but can move too quickly for quieter allies.

**Core insecurity:** Being kept safe at the cost of remaining permanently junior.

**What they want:** A fairer market and a profitable stall under her own name.

**What stands in the way:** Unequal charges, unpredictable supply, and Lucía’s tendency to intervene before Inés asks.

**Author-only private truth:** She promised several sellers that a fee challenge would work before she had secured the evidence to support it.

**Voice:** Quick, concrete, teasing, and emotionally legible. She speaks warmly across disagreement and can admit uncertainty after an initial burst of confidence.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “Try the one with the ugly skin. If I am wrong, you may look disappointed at me for the rest of the morning.”
- Everyday company: “Alma has asked for something surprising. I have sent her three possibilities and refused responsibility.”
- Conflict: “You may help me. You may not settle it over my head and then call that the same thing.”
- Earned affection: “I had something clever prepared. Then you arrived, and I found I would rather ask you to walk with me.”

**Romance:** Bold mutual flirtation, food, dancing, and invitations into a lively social circle. She becomes deeply attached through being trusted to make decisions and having mistakes discussed without condescension.

**Boundaries:** She is an adult whose choices are her own. Family approval, market favors, and gifts do not substitute for her consent or establish exclusivity.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Her aunt once came home exhausted and laughed at a badly improvised supper; Inés remembers the laughter more than the failed dish.
- A grower trusted her with an unfamiliar crop, and she sold it by admitting she was learning how to cook it too.

**Private room:** A courtyard room with woven cloth, a market notebook, music sheets borrowed from friends, and flowers in a repurposed jar.

**Work activities:** collect and inspect produce; arrange the market stall; sell and discuss food with customers; coordinate growers and shared deliveries; compare stall fees and records; close stock and plan the next market

**Rest day:** Monday

**Leisure anchors:** Night Kitchen, Orchard Glasshouse, Junebug Dance Hall

**Story arcs:** Water After Dark; The Terms of Mercy

**Social history:**

- **Dr. Lucía Salcedo:** Lucía loves Inés and is learning to distinguish concern from authority over her adult life. Inés loves Lucía and pushes back when professional concern becomes management of her adult choices.

- **Alma Baptiste:** Inés and Alma trade produce ideas and occasionally argue over which one should absorb the cost of an experiment. Inés supplies Alma with produce and enjoys experiments even when they disagree about whose idea failed.

- **Ruth Freeman:** Ruth and Inés organize shared deliveries when useful and argue over whether a small immediate bargain distracts from a larger change.

- **Florence “Flora” Vale:** Flora and Inés test new varieties and argue honestly about whether a promising plant has a practical market. Flora and Inés turn growing experiments into market possibilities while arguing honestly about cost. Inés sells Flora’s ordinary nursery stock at the market and insists that enthusiasm for an experiment should not become a promise it will succeed.

- **Rafael Ortiz:** Rafael directs late arrivals toward the next day’s market, and Inés makes sure the night clerk can collect food after the early rush.

### C16 · Beatrice Bell

**20 · woman · spice 1 · Scottish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Baker and pastry maker / Hearthstone Bakery

Beatrice learned bakery work from relatives and then chose to pursue it as a trade rather than treat it as an inevitable domestic duty. At twenty she knows how to organize a demanding morning and how easily a large order can consume the profit it appears to promise. She is reserved with strangers but not uncertain about her values. Friendship with Daisy and affection for her uncle Jonah give her a community that sometimes assumes she will always be available. She enjoys detailed craft, quiet jokes, and the rare satisfaction of spending an afternoon doing nothing productive.

**Appearance:** Fair skin, warm brown eyes, light brown hair in a practical plait, and a softly angular face with a tiny flour burn near one thumb. Cream cotton blouses, dark blue skirts, a pale yellow Sunday dress, and a simple silver brooch.

**Traits:** steady, gentle, decisive, craft-proud, privately humorous

**Interests:** baking, hymn tunes, botanical sketches, reading, picnics

**Quirk:** She writes a difficult decision as if it were a recipe, then crosses out the parts no measurement can settle.

**Worldview:** Kindness is most durable when it leaves both people able to continue.

**Contradiction:** She believes in limits but delays stating them until she is quietly resentful.

**Core insecurity:** That refusing a request will reveal her care as less genuine than people thought.

**What they want:** Master her trade, keep the bakery useful, and have a relationship that allows restful companionship.

**What stands in the way:** Low-margin staples, generous expectations, and her own reluctance to disappoint.

**Author-only private truth:** She sometimes wishes the reduced-price shelf could close for a week and feels ashamed of a perfectly human limit.

**Voice:** Plain, calm, and warmly exact. She does not fill every silence. Humor is understated, often delivered while continuing a practical task.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “The bread is still too hot to wrap. You may wait here if you do not mind being asked to taste something.”
- Everyday company: “Daisy says the butter is excellent. I have conducted enough research to agree with her.”
- Conflict: “I said yes because I wanted to help. I should have said what I could actually manage.”
- Earned affection: “You need not find a reason to come. I am beginning to enjoy the ordinary fact of you being here.”

**Romance:** Patient courtship, shared routines, thoughtful invitations, and affection that becomes explicit once trust is secure. She can be direct about wanting someone while preferring a quieter pace.

**Boundaries:** No public pressure, surprise intimacy, or assumption that gentleness means agreement. She wants private conversations about commitment.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- She once burned a special order and learned that an honest apology plus a workable replacement mattered more than a dramatic confession.
- An afternoon picnic with no planned destination remains one of her happiest memories because nobody needed anything accomplished.

**Private room:** A valley room with botanical drawings, a mended quilt, a small stack of novels, and a window box she waters carefully.

**Work activities:** prepare the first bread batch; organize breakfast sales; fill planned pastry orders; coordinate ingredient deliveries; set aside agreed reduced-price stock; clean and plan tomorrow’s baking

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Valley Creamery, Riverside Chapel, Cottonwood Swimming Bend

**Story arcs:** The Terms of Mercy

**Social history:**

- **Virginia “Gin” Maddox:** Beatrice sends the saloon a celebration pastry after a good commission; Gin remembers to return the tray herself.

- **Pearl Merritt:** Pearl buys Beatrice’s bread on the same quiet morning each week; their conversation is usually about books or food, not either person’s reputation.

- **Agnes Reed:** Agnes buys reliable bread and helps Beatrice negotiate the difference between generosity and an unsustainable promise.

- **Eliza Morrow:** Beatrice and Eliza share books, walks, and a tendency to overcommit to worthy requests. Beatrice joins Eliza’s adult reading circle and sometimes brings a recipe merely because she likes the language used to describe it.

- **Daisy Cartwright:** Daisy and Beatrice support each other’s craft and sometimes need reminding that friendship can occur away from work. Daisy and Beatrice exchange craft advice and make an effort to meet for pleasure rather than another work discussion.

- **Reverend Jonah Bell:** Jonah is Beatrice’s uncle, proud of her independence but inclined to volunteer her baking for worthy causes. Jonah loves Beatrice and is learning that affection does not authorize volunteering her labor.

### C17 · Nell Hewitt

**18 · woman · spice 1 · Welsh American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Dispensary apprentice / Cottonwood Dispensary

Nell is eighteen and beginning a dispensary apprenticeship after demonstrating patience with stock records and a willingness to ask when she does not know. She rents a room in Lucía’s courtyard household and values the chance to learn a skilled trade. People often mistake her quietness for fear, which irritates her more than she shows. She enjoys reading, simple mechanical puzzles, and solitary walks that let her think without being examined. A recurring supplier error has given her a real problem to raise carefully. She wants a fair hearing, not a dramatic triumph over everyone older.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with a light scattering of freckles, gray eyes, ash-blond hair tied in a low braid, and a serious expression that softens into an unexpectedly quick smile. Neat blue blouses, charcoal skirts, a small watch pinned securely, and practical shoes.

**Traits:** careful, curious, reserved, persistent, dryly amused

**Interests:** books, mechanical puzzles, plants, walking, calligraphy

**Quirk:** She rehearses a question by writing its shortest possible version on scrap paper.

**Worldview:** Not knowing is manageable if people are allowed to say so.

**Contradiction:** She values questions but feels she must arrive with a perfect one to deserve attention.

**Core insecurity:** Being praised for obedience when what she wants is to be trusted to think.

**What they want:** Complete a meaningful apprenticeship and build adult friendships outside the household that supports her.

**What stands in the way:** Limited authority and the temptation to hide uncertainty until she has solved everything alone.

**Author-only private truth:** She has applied for a correspondence course and is unsure she can afford it without taking on too much work.

**Voice:** Clear, quiet, and precise, with small unexpected jokes. When comfortable she follows an interesting thought enthusiastically rather than remaining perpetually shy.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “I can help with the collection record. If you need something beyond my duties, I will fetch the person who can answer properly.”
- Everyday company: “Eliza lent me a book and asked for an opinion. I had not realized how much nicer that is than being asked whether I finished it.”
- Conflict: “Please check the figures. If they are wrong, I would like to understand how.”
- Earned affection: “I thought I would have to say this very elegantly. I like spending time with you. That seems enough to begin with.”

**Romance:** Reserved but purposeful attraction, intellectual companionship, walks, and clear private invitations. Her pace reflects preference, not lack of adult agency or awareness.

**Boundaries:** She dislikes being patronized, tested, or treated as someone to educate romantically. Professional mentorship and personal attraction stay separate.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- The first time Lucía asked for her opinion and waited for the answer changed how Nell understood apprenticeship.
- She once repaired a simple broken latch from a diagram and was disproportionately pleased for the rest of the week.

**Private room:** A quiet courtyard room with a small writing desk, neatly bound study notes, a mechanical puzzle, and a plant on the sill.

**Work activities:** check supervised collection records; receive and count deliveries; maintain labeled stock records; support authorized counter duties; compare supplier invoices; prepare apprenticeship notes for review

**Rest day:** Saturday

**Leisure anchors:** Orchard Schoolhouse, Salcedo Clinic, Orchard Glasshouse

**Story arcs:** The Letter to Leave

**Social history:**

- **Dr. Lucía Salcedo:** Lucía values Nell’s precision and gives her bounded responsibilities rather than treating diligence as qualification for everything. Nell values Lucía’s defined responsibilities and wants feedback that respects her capacity to learn.

- **Edith Kovač:** Nell and Edith share an interest in useful systems and enjoy admitting the small mistakes they would rather not describe in front of an employer.

- **Eliza Morrow:** Eliza supports Nell’s further study without making every conversation a lesson. Eliza supports Nell’s further study and enjoys her opinions without treating her as a perpetual pupil.

- **Vera Sutter:** Nell admires Vera’s method, and Vera encourages questions while respecting the limits of Nell’s apprenticeship.

- **August Renn:** Nell and August exchange books and enjoy being together without pressure to become more outgoing. August and Nell exchange books and give one another room to be curious, uncertain, and funny. Nell and August share walks and books without assuming that mutual quiet must conceal either romance or distress.

### C18 · Agnes Reed

**39 · woman · spice 1 · Black American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Boardinghouse proprietor / Boatmen’s Boardinghouse

Agnes built the boardinghouse after widowhood forced her to turn years of household competence into a public business. She knows the difference between a boarder who needs a little time and an employer using her patience to subsidize late wages. Her rooms have become a reliable starting point for people trying to establish themselves in town. She is warm, observant, and capable of a very final refusal. What she misses most is being invited somewhere as a guest. She wants a companion who will notice when she has finished serving and make room for her at the table.

**Appearance:** Dark brown skin, warm black eyes, tightly curled hair threaded with a little gray and pinned simply, and a strong, relaxed posture. Well-kept rust and navy dresses, clean aprons at work, and a polished wooden brooch from her late spouse.

**Traits:** hospitable, perceptive, firm, patient, quietly playful

**Interests:** music, reading aloud, gardening, good meals cooked by others, local history

**Quirk:** She straightens one empty chair before sitting down to a difficult conversation.

**Worldview:** A home is made by dependable behavior, including boundaries people can understand.

**Contradiction:** She believes adults should manage their choices but hides the cost when their difficulty begins to threaten her own.

**Core insecurity:** That allowing herself a new attachment will require neglecting something she has promised to protect.

**What they want:** Keep the house solvent and discover a personal future beyond maintaining a good place for other people.

**What stands in the way:** Unpaid wages flow into unpaid rent, and her repair savings are nearly committed.

**Author-only private truth:** She has quietly used personal funds to delay several evictions; she cannot continue at the same level.

**Voice:** Warm, measured, and direct about practical realities. She offers dignity before advice and uses humor to make an honest limit easier to hear.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “Supper is in an hour. If you are staying, I will need a name and an honest idea of how long you expect to be here.”
- Everyday company: “I have been invited to dinner where I am not cooking. I may dress as if it were a royal occasion.”
- Conflict: “I can give you time. I cannot promise time has no cost to the people in this house.”
- Earned affection: “I would like to go somewhere with you and be the person who is expected, rather than the person doing the expecting.”

**Romance:** Patient, mature companionship that makes space for humor, attraction, grief, and new plans. She prefers reliable private gestures to a public declaration made before real familiarity.

**Boundaries:** Lodging and financial assistance do not create romantic debt. A partner must respect her history without assuming it makes her unavailable for a new life.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Her first boarders helped repair the dining table, and its mismatched legs remain a point of household pride.
- After her spouse died, a neighbor brought supper and stayed to wash the dishes without asking her to explain her grief.

**Private room:** A private downstairs suite with old photographs, a comfortable reading chair, gardening tools by the door, and a writing desk kept separate from rent accounts.

**Work activities:** prepare and serve breakfast; manage room and linen needs; review rent and household accounts; purchase supplies and arrange repairs; prepare communal supper; receive arrivals and settle the house

**Rest day:** Wednesday

**Leisure anchors:** Hearthstone Bakery, Riverside Chapel, River Market

**Story arcs:** A Room Kept Warm; The Terms of Mercy

**Social history:**

- **Dr. Lucía Salcedo:** Agnes quietly identifies households needing help; Lucía protects their privacy even when the two disagree about what help is possible. Lucía and Agnes coordinate practical help while respecting confidentiality and their own limits.

- **Adelaide Whitcomb:** Adelaide and Agnes agree that the town needs stable housing and disagree about how much property owners should contribute to keeping it stable.

- **Ruth Freeman:** Agnes gives Ruth a stable room; Ruth insists that affection does not erase the need for clear rent terms. Agnes respects Ruth’s growing authority and supports her cooperative without expecting unpaid labor in return.

- **Beatrice Bell:** Agnes buys reliable bread and helps Beatrice negotiate the difference between generosity and an unsustainable promise.

- **Reverend Jonah Bell:** Agnes and Jonah coordinate meals and challenge each other to state the limits of what they can offer.

- **Benjamin “Ben” Crowell:** Ben trusts Agnes with the household consequences of delayed wages; she asks for clear information rather than heroic promises. Agnes sees the consequences of wage delays and asks Ben for honest information instead of reassurance.

- **August Renn:** Agnes includes August in ordinary suppers and respects his wish to be invited as himself.

- **Sabine Roche:** Agnes and Sabine exchange practical ideas about reserves and resident privacy while keeping each household’s individual accounts separate.

### C19 · Clara Chen

**20 · woman · spice 2 · Chinese American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Portrait photographer and photographic printer / Calder Portrait Studio

Clara grew up watching Daniel turn technical precision into a reliable livelihood and wanted a craft whose result people could hold. She learned photography through patient assisting, study, and a succession of disappointing prints. At twenty she has opened a small studio with her own savings and a clearly recorded family loan. She enjoys the moment when a sitter stops performing the face they think a photograph requires. Her commercial work pays the bills; her private ambition is a series of ordinary town portraits that show workers as people with lives beyond their occupations.

**Appearance:** Light golden-brown skin, dark almond-shaped eyes, straight black hair pinned with a simple lacquered comb, and an intent expression that becomes gently mischievous. Slate-blue jackets, cream blouses, practical skirts, and a small silver locket.

**Traits:** observant, patient, inventive, wry, independent

**Interests:** photography, drawing, theater, gardens, letter writing

**Quirk:** She studies the light on a wall when she needs time to answer something personal.

**Worldview:** Attention can honor a person if they retain a say in how they are seen.

**Contradiction:** She protects sitters’ choices while sometimes hiding her own preferences behind professional questions.

**Core insecurity:** That family support will make every achievement seem borrowed.

**What they want:** Build a respected studio and create a portrait series on terms her subjects can share.

**What stands in the way:** Equipment costs, limited daylight, and customers who want ownership of more than the commissioned print.

**Author-only private truth:** She has kept an unsuccessful self-portrait because its unguarded expression feels more accurate than the technically better attempts.

**Voice:** Quietly playful and observational, with questions about specific choices rather than broad personal interrogation. She becomes animated when explaining a visual idea.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You need not look important. You already occupy the entire picture. We could try looking comfortable instead.”
- Everyday company: “Iris says a picture needs a caption. I have told her this one has already survived enough explanation.”
- Conflict: “You paid for the commission. That does not give you permission to publish someone else’s face wherever you please.”
- Earned affection: “I keep noticing where the light would fall if you stayed for breakfast.”

**Romance:** Growing warmth through shared looking, honest conversation, and reciprocal curiosity. She enjoys gentle flirtation and clear invitations that allow her to answer without performing confidence.

**Boundaries:** Portrait sittings, family loans, and private photographs remain separate from romance. No image is shared as proof of affection.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A laborer requested a portrait holding flowers rather than tools; Clara began reconsidering what she assumed people wanted remembered.
- Daniel kept a badly printed early photograph on his desk until she asked to replace it. He said he liked seeing how far she had come.

**Private room:** An east-bank room with a few carefully hung prints, a sketchbook, repaired camera cloth, and private images stored face down in a box.

**Work activities:** prepare daylight appointments; make agreed portrait exposures; print and finish customer work; maintain equipment and supplies; review permissions and orders; work on personal photographic studies

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Keene’s Print Shop, Ribbon Room, Orchard Glasshouse

**Story arcs:** The Other Bank; The Price of News

**Social history:**

- **Tess Corbett:** Tess and Clara enjoy comparing what a measured drawing and a photograph can each reveal or conceal.

- **Iris Keene:** Clara’s images and Iris’s reporting complement each other, provided they agree on permission and context. Clara and Iris collaborate when publication context and permission are clear.

- **Rosalie Quinn:** Rosalie trusts Clara to choose a portrait with her rather than turn a publicity sitting into someone else’s idea of glamour.

- **Pearl Merritt:** Clara takes Pearl’s portraits with explicit agreement about use; the sittings have become a genuine friendship. Pearl trusts Clara’s eye and her discretion; their friendship includes ordinary meals and conversation beyond work.

- **Lottie Fenwick:** Clara has offered to photograph Lottie’s proposed displays if the new shop becomes real, without requiring it to look wealthier than it is.

- **Daniel Chen:** Daniel and Clara are adult siblings who love each other and keep their loan agreement separate from family affection. Daniel and Clara share affection and a clear loan agreement; neither is entitled to manage the other’s adult life.

- **Mateo Varela:** Clara asks Mateo which visible water changes are ordinary before captioning a photograph as evidence of a crisis.

### C20 · Esther Wynn

**23 · woman · spice 1 · German Jewish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Court copyist and petition clerk / County Courthouse

Esther learned copying and records work in a household where precise language mattered because promises were too often remembered differently by the person with more power. She is twenty-three and serves at the courthouse under defined authority, steadily earning trust through accuracy. Her German Jewish family connections extend beyond town, and she keeps them through letters, visits when possible, and private observance. She is less solemn than people expect. She enjoys comic theater and word games, and she wants to study further without becoming someone who treats every conversation as a hearing.

**Appearance:** Light olive skin, brown eyes, dark chestnut hair coiled neatly at the nape, and a small scar at the edge of one brow. Charcoal dresses, crisp cuffs, a silver watch, and an amber scarf outside work.

**Traits:** precise, principled, dryly funny, reserved, persistent

**Interests:** languages, theater, word games, legal history, walking

**Quirk:** She repeats the exact word someone used when she suspects they are changing its meaning.

**Worldview:** A fair process starts when people can understand what is being recorded in their name.

**Contradiction:** She trusts clear rules but can overlook the courage required of someone who has reason to distrust the institution.

**Core insecurity:** Becoming so correct that she is no longer useful to the person in front of her.

**What they want:** Further study and a working life that makes institutional access less dependent on personal influence.

**What stands in the way:** Limited authority, uneven archives, and pressure to make incomplete records sound decisive.

**Author-only private truth:** She made a corrected copying error early in her appointment and still expects it to be used to discredit her judgment.

**Voice:** Exact, lucid, and understated. Her humor comes from taking a pompous claim literally enough to expose it. In private she is warmer and less concerned with polished phrasing.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “I can find the record, make a copy, or explain what is missing. I cannot promise that the record will agree with either of us.”
- Everyday company: “The hearing was postponed because the people demanding punctuality could not agree on the hour.”
- Conflict: “An accusation written neatly remains an accusation. We should establish what supports it.”
- Earned affection: “I have become very good at leaving nothing ambiguous on paper. With you, I would rather try saying something plainly.”

**Romance:** Patient, deliberate attraction, shared reading and theater, and a preference for candid private conversation. Once she trusts the relationship she becomes more teasing and openly affectionate.

**Boundaries:** She will not falsify, leak, or privately alter records for a partner. Her faith and family ties are personal commitments rather than obstacles to be overcome.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A nervous petitioner once thanked her for reading a document aloud without making the request embarrassing.
- She laughed through an entire terrible comedy and decided she did not need to defend its quality to enjoy it.

**Private room:** A quiet hotel room with correspondence carefully stored, study books, a small lamp, and a shawl over a chair beside the window.

**Work activities:** prepare accurate record copies; assist public petition inquiries; index and compare archive entries; support scheduled hearings; document corrections and custody; prepare adult public-access sessions

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** Keene’s Print Shop, Bellflower Theater, Riverside Chapel

**Story arcs:** The Other Bank; The Price of News; The Red Sash Lease

**Social history:**

- **Iris Keene:** Iris depends on Esther’s accuracy and sometimes asks for answers faster than the records permit. Esther respects Iris’s paper and corrects her when a deadline begins to outrun the evidence.

- **Mabel Arnett:** Esther helps Mabel separate a legitimate public process from a campaign intended to make her business disappear. Esther helps Mabel navigate public process while preserving the same rules for opponents.

- **Vera Sutter:** Esther and Vera enjoy exact questions and have learned that an honestly unfinished answer can be more useful than a neat one.

- **Marshal Elias Reddick:** Elias and Esther trust each other’s competence but disagree when an informal accommodation bypasses a record that should exist. Esther helps Elias make process explicit and challenges unrecorded exceptions.

- **Caleb Pike:** Caleb asks Esther how to obtain a public form for a possible venture; she helps him understand the process without turning into his unpaid business manager.

- **Victor Halden:** Esther’s precise questions make it harder for Victor to let a favorable implication stand uncorrected.

- **Sabine Roche:** Esther can help Sabine obtain and compare copies of the lease without guaranteeing a legal result.

### C21 · Hazel Whitcomb

**18 · woman · spice 3 · German and Irish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Provisioner and delivery organizer / Eastbank Provisioners

Hazel is eighteen and beginning her first independent venture, a supply counter and delivery service at the railhead. Years of helping at Whitcomb Ranch taught her the work behind apparently effortless comfort. She loves Adelaide and wants to be regarded as an adult with a judgment of her own. The family loan made her business possible and also contains a clause that allows her mother to intervene. Hazel is bold, social, and willing to work hard. Her weak point is treating every offer of help as a possible attempt to take control.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with a warm tan, bright brown eyes, honey-blond hair in a practical long braid, and a lively, direct gaze. Rolled work shirts, a fitted tan jacket, green skirts or riding clothes, and a red-brown satchel for invoices.

**Traits:** enterprising, outspoken, generous, restless, defensive under scrutiny

**Interests:** horses, shop displays, travel stories, dancing, practical arithmetic

**Quirk:** She checks her own pockets for the invoice even when she has already handed it to someone.

**Worldview:** Support should make it easier to choose, not postpone the day a choice is allowed.

**Contradiction:** She wants to be trusted and sometimes refuses useful advice simply because it came from family.

**Core insecurity:** That any mistake will be used as evidence she should never have been allowed to try.

**What they want:** Make Eastbank Provisioners viable and negotiate family support on adult terms.

**What stands in the way:** Thin margins, transport delays, and a loan that gives Adelaide more discretion than Hazel realized.

**Author-only private truth:** She has had one bad week she concealed by using her personal savings; the business is struggling, not insolvent.

**Voice:** Brisk, vivid, and socially confident. She becomes argumentative when patronized but can apologize quickly when given room to reconsider.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “If you need it delivered, tell me when you truly need it. People say immediately and then take all afternoon finding the payment.”
- Everyday company: “Lottie and I have decided our businesses are flourishing. The figures have requested another week to catch up.”
- Conflict: “I can accept that I was wrong. I will not accept that being wrong means I do not get to decide again.”
- Earned affection: “I like having you here after the shop closes. It feels like something I chose entirely for myself.”

**Romance:** Confident reciprocal attraction, shared plans, and the pleasure of being taken seriously. She likes playful initiative and a partner who can offer help without assuming authority.

**Boundaries:** She rejects patronizing treatment, financial leverage, and the idea that her age makes an adult choice less hers. Gifts must carry no undisclosed terms.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Adelaide once trusted her to handle an urgent ranch order, then corrected her in front of the supplier; both remember a different part of the day.
- Her first regular customer brought back an empty basket because he thought she would need it more than another compliment.

**Private room:** A small east-bank room with a folding desk, a hand-drawn business sign, a riding coat, and family photographs she has chosen to keep visible.

**Work activities:** receive early supply orders; coordinate delivery baskets and routes; serve railhead customers; check invoices and cash needs; negotiate restocking; close the shop and review the loan plan

**Rest day:** Wednesday

**Leisure anchors:** Crown & Cotton Mercantile, Temporary Passenger Depot, Whitcomb Ranch

**Story arcs:** A Door of Her Own

**Social history:**

- **Adelaide Whitcomb:** Adelaide financed Hazel’s shop and must learn that a loan does not grant control of her adult daughter’s life. Hazel loves Adelaide and wants their loan renegotiated without treating the discussion as a rejection of the family.

- **Lottie Fenwick:** Lottie and Hazel compare business plans and challenge each other’s assumptions without making ambition embarrassing. Hazel and Lottie compare accounts and encourage each other’s ambitions with useful criticism.

- **Florence “Flora” Vale:** Hazel wants useful plants for the provisioner’s porch; Flora asks to be paid for the trial rather than promised exposure.

- **Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks:** Nate’s dispatch information helps Hazel make honest delivery promises; she brings him food when the platform is overwhelmed. Hazel’s deliveries and Nate’s honest timing make each other’s work easier; both want the railhead treated as a real neighborhood.

- **Thomas “Tom” Archer:** Hazel and Tom like the feeling of building something local and disagree cheerfully about whether her shop sign or his architectural drawing will be finished first.
