Fair skin, warm brown eyes, chestnut hair beginning to recede, and a neatly trimmed beard. Plain dark suits, soft waistcoats, a worn coat, and an unshowy manner more approachable than ceremonial. At the visitor-facing part of Riverside Chapel. Fully clothed, occupation-specific styling, natural posture, one person, no text, no collage. Preserve stated age and distinctive appearance.

### C37 · Caleb Pike

**20 · man · spice 3 · Black American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Boatwright and repair-yard worker / Pike Boatworks

Caleb learned river craft around his father’s ferry and chose boatbuilding because it could turn inherited knowledge into something visibly his own. At twenty he is skilled at ordinary repair and still learning how to cost an ambitious commission. The bridge looks to him like a reason to invent a new service; he thinks Samuel sees only loss. In fact, both have privately imagined similar futures. Caleb is sociable, affectionate, and impatient with arguments he thinks are about pride. He does not always recognize his own pride in wanting to be the person who first saw the answer.

**Appearance:** Deep brown skin, dark eyes, short tightly curled hair, and a strong, agile build. Rolled indigo shirts, well-worn canvas trousers, a repaired brown waistcoat, and a small pencil usually behind one ear.

**Traits:** inventive, warm, bold, optimistic, impatient

**Interests:** boat design, music, swimming, woodwork, shared meals

**Quirk:** He sketches an improvement before checking whether the original object belongs to him.

**Worldview:** Respect for good work can include making something new from it.

**Contradiction:** He wants his father to hear new ideas while dismissing the emotional cost of changing an old life.

**Core insecurity:** Remaining an apprentice in the eyes of the person whose respect matters most.

**What they want:** Build a small passenger boat and a livelihood he can share without having to inherit it unchanged.

**What stands in the way:** Capital, incomplete plans, and a father-son conversation each keeps postponing.

**Author-only private truth:** He has prepared a business sketch for a leisure service and assumed Samuel would reject it without asking.

**Voice:** Warm, quick, and concrete, with enthusiasm that becomes infectious rather than technical showmanship. He grows quieter when a disagreement is genuinely personal.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “It will look more like a boat once I stop improving the drawing. You are welcome to encourage either process.”
- Everyday company: “Lena says the passengers may prefer something that floats to something that proves I am clever. A stern critic.”
- Conflict: “I am not trying to erase what you built. I am trying to imagine where I can stand beside it.”
- Earned affection: “I keep making plans with room for another person, then pretending I do not know whose place it is.”

**Romance:** Open attraction, playful shared activity, practical creativity, and warm private affection. He likes being challenged and wants commitment that leaves both people room to grow.

**Boundaries:** No pressure to prove courage on the river or finance an invention as evidence of love. Family disagreements do not grant a partner authority to settle them.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Samuel once let him guide a simple repair all the way through without correcting the method; Caleb remembers the trust more than the result.
- He and Gabriel built an unnecessarily elaborate shelf for Alma and were pleased when she used it to hold onions.

**Private room:** A private room in the Pike cottage with boat sketches, music, a carefully made chair, and tools stored away from ordinary living space.

**Work activities:** inspect repair commissions; prepare timber and fittings; carry out authorized boat repairs; develop costed design drawings; coordinate launches and deliveries; clean the yard and review projects

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Soto Forge, Cottonwood Swimming Bend, Pike’s Ferry

**Story arcs:** After the Crossing

**Social history:**

- **Esther Wynn:** Caleb asks Esther how to obtain a public form for a possible venture; she helps him understand the process without turning into his unpaid business manager.

- **Lena Velarde:** Lena and Caleb share ideas for a useful passenger boat and enjoy each other’s practical imagination. Caleb and Lena share practical ideas for river transport and enjoy unhurried time by the water.

- **Daisy Cartwright:** Caleb sketches equipment improvements for Daisy; she is delighted when one solves a real problem and unimpressed when it is merely clever.

- **Gabriel Soto:** Caleb and Gabriel enjoy practical invention and respect the difference between an interesting idea and a funded commission. Gabriel encourages Caleb’s ideas and makes him price the materials before promising a commission.

- **Samuel Pike:** Samuel and Caleb share love and craft knowledge while misreading each other’s plans for life after the bridge. Caleb loves Samuel and must learn that proposing a future also means listening to the loss it may contain.

- **Rafael Ortiz:** Caleb and Rafael make plans that fit an early yard and a late hotel shift, occasionally settling for breakfast and laughing at the difficulty.

### C38 · Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks

**22 · man · spice 2 · English American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Station clerk and freight dispatcher / Temporary Passenger Depot

Nate is twenty-two and has become a useful station clerk because he remembers how each small delay affects the next person’s work. He began in freight handling and earned dispatch responsibility through reliable records. The temporary station feels like the beginning of a life he can build, not a temporary assignment. He is friendly, quietly ambitious, and too ready to apologize for a system he did not design. A published connection time he knows is unrealistic has become the point at which being agreeable no longer feels like good service. He likes music, letters, and evenings that end without another interruption.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with a warm tan, light brown eyes, dark blond hair neatly combed but often flattened by a cap, and an open, thoughtful expression. Clean work shirts, a dark vest, practical trousers, and a blue railway coat.

**Traits:** reliable, friendly, observant, ambitious, self-blaming

**Interests:** timetables, music, travel letters, photography, walking

**Quirk:** He checks the clock after saying he has time, then deliberately turns away from it.

**Worldview:** A useful promise is one a tired ordinary person can actually rely on.

**Contradiction:** He believes in honesty but softens bad news until a customer can mistake hope for assurance.

**Core insecurity:** Being blamed for a failure that proves he was trusted too soon.

**What they want:** A permanent responsible role at the station and a personal life rooted in the east-bank community.

**What stands in the way:** Optimistic published times and pressure to make the new service look smoother than it is.

**Author-only private truth:** He has logged repeated missed transfers and kept the rejected timetable correction in his own papers.

**Voice:** Clear, courteous, and lightly humorous. He gives practical alternatives and becomes more direct when he stops trying to absorb everyone’s disappointment.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “The train is due this afternoon. Whether the connection is sensible depends on the ferry, and I would rather tell you that now.”
- Everyday company: “Hazel has sent breakfast with instructions to eat it before I explain another delay. I believe she knows her customer.”
- Conflict: “We cannot improve the service by printing an hour we know people cannot meet.”
- Earned affection: “I have a free evening, and I checked twice. I would like to spend it with you.”

**Romance:** Warm mutual interest, dependable plans, ordinary outings, and a growing ability to state personal wants without apologizing. He appreciates playfulness that does not make reliability a joke.

**Boundaries:** He will not alter dispatch, reveal private travel, or promise impossible access for a partner. Work interruptions are discussed honestly rather than used to test devotion.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- His first successful dispatch day was uneventful, which an experienced colleague told him was the point.
- He once missed a social invitation because he assumed it was polite rather than sincere and now tries to ask.

**Private room:** A modest east-bank room with travel letters, a carefully kept watch, a borrowed camera print, and a table cleared when friends visit.

**Work activities:** prepare station opening and notices; coordinate authorized arrivals and freight; handle tickets and dispatch records; compare feasible transfer times; resolve customer inquiries; complete the shift and delay log

**Rest day:** Wednesday

**Leisure anchors:** Eastbank Provisioners, Telegraph Office, Widow’s Cut Coach Station

**Story arcs:** A Door of Her Own

**Social history:**

- **Hazel Whitcomb:** Nate’s dispatch information helps Hazel make honest delivery promises; she brings him food when the platform is overwhelmed. Hazel’s deliveries and Nate’s honest timing make each other’s work easier; both want the railhead treated as a real neighborhood.

- **Sadie Rusk:** Nate and Sadie coordinate feasible connections and distrust timetables written to impress investors. Sadie and Nate compare actual travel times and refuse to turn an optimistic connection into a personal guarantee.

- **Daniel Chen:** Daniel and Nate coordinate transport messages and support one another through unrealistic customer expectations. Daniel and Nate coordinate messages and give one another room to admit when the system is failing.

- **Samuel Pike:** Samuel and Nate coordinate a ferry and railway connection while refusing to pretend that weather will obey a printed hour.

- **Thomas “Tom” Archer:** Nate and Tom share the frustrations and pride of building a lasting east-bank life.

- **Benjamin “Ben” Crowell:** Nate and Ben compare the frustration of being expected to repair a system with careful records alone.

- **Rafael Ortiz:** Rafael and Nate help travelers manage real delays without inventing guarantees or disclosing unnecessary personal details.

### C39 · Thomas “Tom” Archer

**21 · man · spice 3 · Irish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Bridge rigger and masonry apprentice / Bridge Works

Tom is twenty-one and working as a rigger and masonry apprentice after a series of laboring jobs taught him the difference between a crew that trains people and one that merely spends them. He is proud of the bridge as something visible he helped build. He is also beginning to understand that pride in work cannot require silence about a problem. A temporary platform concern put him between a supervisor’s impatience and Tess’s insistence on evidence. Outside work he is lively, affectionate, and fond of sketching buildings he hopes to understand well enough to design someday.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with a deep outdoor tan, blue eyes, dark brown hair kept short, and a crooked smile. Rolled work shirts, canvas trousers, a patched waistcoat, and a clean dark jacket for evenings.

**Traits:** energetic, loyal, curious, bold, sensitive to dismissal

**Interests:** building design, drawing, dance music, riding, practical puzzles

**Quirk:** He looks up at a roof joint when entering a room and then remembers to greet the people underneath it.

**Worldview:** Loyalty to a crew includes making sure they can finish the day safely.

**Contradiction:** He wants to be seen as capable and sometimes treats asking for guidance as proof that he is not.

**Core insecurity:** Being remembered for the concern he failed to raise rather than the work he did well.

**What they want:** Complete a genuine apprenticeship, earn trusted responsibility, and build a settled life.

**What stands in the way:** Production pressure and reluctance to challenge a supervisor before his own standing feels secure.

**Author-only private truth:** He documented a substituted material delivery but initially omitted the supervisor’s dismissive reply because he feared retaliation.

**Voice:** Direct, lively, and practical. He enjoys explaining what he is learning and becomes measured when defending an observation.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You can see the work from the rail. Coming closer will not make it more finished, though someone will certainly tell you so.”
- Everyday company: “Tess says my drawing is promising. She also says promising is not a measurement.”
- Conflict: “I am not claiming I know everything. I am asking that somebody qualified check what I saw.”
- Earned affection: “I spent half the day imagining what I would tell you afterward. It made the work feel different.”

**Romance:** Confident attraction, shared enthusiasm, dancing, and direct affectionate invitations. He wants a partner who respects both his skill and his right to still be learning.

**Boundaries:** No dangerous demonstrations, trespass at the works, or career leverage as romantic tests. He does not confuse admiration with permission to touch.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- An experienced worker once explained a mistake without humiliating him in front of the crew; Tom tries to do the same for others.
- He drew a house he could not afford and discovered he was more interested in who might visit than in its size.

**Private room:** An east-bank room with architectural sketches, a carefully mended work jacket, music notices, and a clean place to sit with company.

**Work activities:** prepare assigned work under supervision; complete authorized rigging or masonry tasks; record material concerns; coordinate crew supplies; review drawings and apprenticeship notes; close the work area with the crew

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** Survey Camp, Junebug Dance Hall, Eastbank Provisioners

**Story arcs:** The Other Bank; The Crowcut Reckoning

**Social history:**

- **Tess Corbett:** Tess trusts Tom’s observations about the works and insists he distinguish a concern from a proven conclusion. Tess takes Tom’s observations seriously while requiring clear evidence and qualified checks.

- **Cora Dunleavy:** Cora enjoys Tom’s enthusiasm and reminds him that dancing need not be approached as another technical examination.

- **Hazel Whitcomb:** Hazel and Tom like the feeling of building something local and disagree cheerfully about whether her shop sign or his architectural drawing will be finished first.

- **Kit Calloway:** Kit and Tom compare drawings of futures they cannot yet afford and are each useful at noticing where the other has skipped a practical step.

- **Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks:** Nate and Tom share the frustrations and pride of building a lasting east-bank life.

- **Mateo Varela:** Tom asks Mateo how construction affects ordinary turns and learns that local work continues beyond the crew’s visible project.

- **Cole Hensley:** Tom remembers Cole as a helpful coworker and refuses to let that memory erase the experience of the theft’s victims.

### C40 · Victor Halden

**46 · man · spice 3 · German American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Railroad land and freight agent / Railroad Freight Office

Victor is forty-six and has built a career turning uncertain projects into agreements investors can understand. He believes infrastructure can improve lives and is equally comfortable arranging who receives the first and largest benefit. Calder’s Run could secure his advancement or reveal that he promised more authority than he holds. He is cultivated, attentive, and often good company. He can care for someone while continuing to defend a contract that harms their neighbors. His central flaw is not an inability to recognize another person’s needs; it is his habit of treating recognition as something he can price into an acceptable concession.

**Appearance:** Fair skin, pale blue eyes, dark hair graying neatly at the sides, a trimmed mustache, and an assured, upright posture. Immaculate charcoal suits, ivory shirts, a restrained gold watch chain, and polished brown boots.

**Traits:** strategic, cultivated, charming, disciplined, controlling

**Interests:** architecture, music, fine meals, business history, cards as social theater

**Quirk:** He adjusts a document so both parties can read it when he wants an unequal offer to feel transparent.

**Worldview:** A workable future requires someone willing to decide where compromise will fall.

**Contradiction:** He values voluntary agreements while structuring choices so refusal becomes expensive.

**Core insecurity:** Being exposed as an intermediary who cannot deliver the authority he projects.

**What they want:** Secure a profitable franchise and a personal attachment that is not simply admiration for his position.

**What stands in the way:** Investor limits, Tess’s alternative route, public scrutiny, and the real grievances his preferred terms create.

**Author-only private truth:** He cannot approve the alternative approach without outside authorization and has allowed local negotiators to believe otherwise.

**Voice:** Polished, measured, and attentive to what the listener wants. He offers exact concessions and can become genuinely candid when he stops trying to manage the outcome.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “The town needs a bridge. The interesting question is what each of us expects to receive for helping it arrive.”
- Everyday company: “I have been told the local supper is simple. It has already produced three negotiations and an excellent dessert.”
- Conflict: “You are correct about the cost. We disagree about who can bear it and what follows if nobody will.”
- Earned affection: “I am accustomed to making an offer difficult to refuse. With you, I would rather learn whether it can be freely accepted.”

**Romance:** Sophisticated attraction, attentive courtship, and a difficult but possible movement toward equality in private life. He likes candor and may initially mistake a boundary for the start of bargaining.

**Boundaries:** A meaningful relationship requires explicit separation from employment, debt, and civic favors. Refusal remains refusal; he must change conduct rather than buy forgiveness.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- An early project succeeded financially and left a community worse served than the prospectus promised; he still describes it as more complicated than critics allow.
- A former partner once declined an expensive gift and asked for an unplanned afternoon. He remembers how difficult that seemed.

**Private room:** A well-appointed hotel room with investor letters locked away, architectural books, excellent stationery, and personal objects so few they reveal his habit of moving on.

**Work activities:** review freight and investor correspondence; hold scheduled commercial appointments; negotiate franchise drafts; compare route and cost proposals; meet civic representatives; prepare qualified reports to investors

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** County Courthouse, Argosy Cardroom, Calder House Hotel

**Story arcs:** The Other Bank; The Price of News

**Social history:**

- **Tess Corbett:** Halden needs Tess’s competence and resents her refusal to make his preferred route sound inevitable. Victor respects Tess’s ability and resents her refusal to convert qualified findings into sales language.

- **Iris Keene:** Victor gives Iris polished public explanations and finds that she prints the qualification as prominently as the promise.

- **Adelaide Whitcomb:** Victor and Adelaide recognize one another’s competence and self-interest while competing over the franchise’s terms.

- **Mabel Arnett:** Victor and Mabel can enjoy the same music while negotiating fiercely over what a thriving evening district owes the railway.

- **Esther Wynn:** Esther’s precise questions make it harder for Victor to let a favorable implication stand uncorrected.

- **Daniel Chen:** Halden needs Daniel’s reliable service and has associates who ask for exceptions Daniel will not casually grant.

- **Felix Avery:** Halden frequents the Argosy but does not automatically know Felix’s debt; any leverage requires an authored disclosure or evidence.

- **Silas Quade:** Silas traces his disputed dismissal to a freight subcontract serving Victor’s railway; Victor contests responsibility for the contractor’s conduct.

### C41 · Mateo Varela

**27 · man · spice 2 · Mexican American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Irrigation steward and water-turn recorder / Irrigation Meeting House

Mateo grew up around irrigated holdings where the day’s work depended on water arriving when a register said it would. He became steward because he could keep accurate records and speak to households that preferred not to speak to one another. At twenty-seven he is trusted with daily responsibility but cannot unilaterally repair the compact. He likes music, adult classes, and conversations that have nothing to do with fairness. His private difficulty is a tendency to make a temporary exception quietly, hoping practical mercy will spare everyone a larger argument. Several such exceptions now need an honest account.

**Appearance:** Warm medium-brown skin, dark eyes, black hair worn short, and a neatly kept beard. Light working shirts, earth-toned waistcoats, sturdy boots, and a weathered hat.

**Traits:** fair-minded, practical, patient, warm, overburdened

**Interests:** water systems, music, reading, gardening, walking

**Quirk:** He checks a remembered time against his notebook even when he is almost certain.

**Worldview:** Fairness has to work at the gate on an ordinary morning, not only in a meeting.

**Contradiction:** He argues for transparent rules while making quiet exceptions for people in immediate difficulty.

**Core insecurity:** That exposing an imperfect act of help will be used to discredit every attempt at reform.

**What they want:** A workable allocation agreement and a personal life where he is not always the mediator.

**What stands in the way:** Entrenched priorities, incomplete records, and his own undocumented accommodations.

**Author-only private truth:** He authorized a short emergency turn for one household and recorded it late, allowing a later diversion to be confused with his action.

**Voice:** Calm, practical, and careful about what a record establishes. Warm humor appears when he is released from the expectation of settling a dispute.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “I can show you the turn that was recorded. Whether it was enough water is a different question, and usually the one people mean.”
- Everyday company: “Eliza says I may attend the adult class without reorganizing its timetable. I have promised to try.”
- Conflict: “A rule that cannot survive an emergency needs changing. That does not mean I was right to change it alone.”
- Earned affection: “I would like to walk with you somewhere I am not responsible for the gate at the end of it.”

**Romance:** Warm and responsive, with companionship, music, and deliberate private openness. He values a partner who can care about his work without turning every evening into another obligation.

**Boundaries:** No alteration of public water access for private affection. A relationship must allow him to stop mediating and state his own needs.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A household once thanked him for explaining a refusal honestly rather than offering a favor he could not sustain.
- An evening class let him admit a gap in his education without losing anyone’s respect.

**Private room:** A valley cottage with music, a small garden, work notebooks stored separately, and a shaded step used for visiting friends.

**Work activities:** check authorized water turns; inspect assigned channels and gates; maintain allocation records; compare household requests; prepare public meeting materials; record changes and next-day duties

**Rest day:** Monday

**Leisure anchors:** Orchard Schoolhouse, Cottonwood Swimming Bend, Whitcomb Ranch

**Story arcs:** The Other Bank; Water After Dark

**Social history:**

- **Tess Corbett:** Mateo supplies water-history detail that Tess’s maps lack; each corrects the other’s assumptions. Tess and Mateo combine measured geography with actual water history rather than assuming either account is complete.

- **Adelaide Whitcomb:** Mateo respects Adelaide’s competence but challenges advantages she considers earned. Mateo respects Adelaide’s practical knowledge while challenging privileges she treats as the natural order.

- **Clara Chen:** Clara asks Mateo which visible water changes are ordinary before captioning a photograph as evidence of a crisis.

- **Eliza Morrow:** Mateo helps arrange adult sessions at hours field workers can attend. Eliza gives Mateo space to learn and enjoy conversation without becoming the person who organizes everything.

- **Lena Velarde:** Lena and Mateo exchange observations about water conditions while keeping each household’s private business separate.

- **Thomas “Tom” Archer:** Tom asks Mateo how construction affects ordinary turns and learns that local work continues beyond the crew’s visible project.

- **Sabine Roche:** Mateo and Sabine recognize the problem of keeping shared accounts without deciding for everyone whose interests appear in them.

### C42 · Isaac Booker

**19 · man · spice 1 · Black American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Stock handler and auction clerk / Cattle Sale Yard

Isaac is nineteen and has worked with livestock long enough to recognize a poor condition hidden beneath a good sales pitch. His record keeping earned him auction-clerk duties, and Cora has been helping him practice calling without swallowing every word. He can be confident with animals and quiet about his own desires. Adelaide values his judgment but sometimes gives him additional responsibility instead of visible credit. He wants a small holding someday and a reputation for fair dealing. His current concern is a pattern of flattering descriptions applied to an influential seller’s animals.

**Appearance:** Deep brown skin, warm brown eyes, close-cropped black curls, and a lean, agile bearing. Checked work shirts, brown waistcoats, dark trousers, and a clean cream shirt for evenings.

**Traits:** observant, steady, wry, ambitious, reserved in intimacy

**Interests:** livestock, music, auction rhythm, riding, gardening

**Quirk:** He lets an animal settle before answering a question, which sometimes makes impatient customers reveal more than they intend.

**Worldview:** Judgment means noticing what a person would prefer you to overlook.

**Contradiction:** He wants fair dealing but worries that challenging a respected seller will close the path to his own future.

**Core insecurity:** Being trusted to do the work and never trusted to speak about it.

**What they want:** Recognition for his judgment, savings toward a small holding, and a relationship he can approach at his own pace.

**What stands in the way:** Unequal influence in the yard and his reluctance to make an accusation without corroboration.

**Author-only private truth:** He retained a legitimate copy of several inconsistent sale descriptions and has not yet decided who can help check them.

**Voice:** Plain, observant, and quietly funny. His confidence is strongest when describing something he knows; affectionate words are fewer and deliberately chosen.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You can hear the sales pitch in a moment. First, I would look at how the animal is standing.”
- Everyday company: “Cora says I need to project my voice. I explained that most of my regular audience prefers me quiet.”
- Conflict: “I am not saying the seller is dishonest. I am saying the descriptions change according to who is selling.”
- Earned affection: “I would like to ask you to supper. I have been taking an unreasonable amount of time finding those words.”

**Romance:** Reserved courtship, shared music and riding, thoughtful invitations, and increasing warmth through reliable familiarity. Low spice reflects his preferred expression, not an absence of adult desire.

**Boundaries:** No public teasing about his private pace, pressure to prove experience, or gifts that create an obligation. He wants explicit, unhurried agreement.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A difficult stock handling day went well because an older worker listened to his observation before giving orders.
- The first time he called a practice sale clearly, Cora applauded as if it were a theater performance and then let him be embarrassed in peace.

**Private room:** A valley room with a few carefully kept books, sale notes, a simple garden plan, and good clothes ready for an invitation he has not yet made.

**Work activities:** inspect assigned livestock; prepare accurate sale descriptions; maintain yard and ownership records; assist scheduled auctions; coordinate loading under supervision; review accounts and calling practice

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Junebug Dance Hall, Whitcomb Ranch, Valley Creamery

**Story arcs:** The Honest Weight

**Social history:**

- **Adelaide Whitcomb:** Adelaide recognizes Isaac’s judgment with animals while underestimating how much credit matters to his future. Adelaide values Isaac’s animal judgment but must learn to give him credit as well as responsibility.

- **Cora Dunleavy:** Isaac practices auction calling while Cora listens for rhythm; each likes seeing the other become confident. Cora helps Isaac practice speaking publicly and respects that private confidence develops differently.

- **Lottie Fenwick:** Lottie and Isaac understand being trusted with responsibility while someone else receives the public credit.

- **Daisy Cartwright:** Isaac and Daisy enjoy dancing and each other’s impatience with being underestimated. Daisy and Isaac share ambition and enjoy ordinary outings without treating one another as projects. Daisy and Isaac enjoy discussing animals more than the people currently trying to sell them and are learning to claim credit publicly.

- **Gabriel Soto:** Gabriel has taught Isaac simple equipment assessment appropriate to the sale yard and encourages him to charge fairly for skilled work.

- **Cole Hensley:** Isaac remembers Cole helping with an awkward freight job and wants a truthful account of the theft before offering a recommendation.

### C43 · Benjamin “Ben” Crowell

**22 · man · spice 2 · Cornish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Mine checkweigher and crew representative / Saint Agnes Mine

Ben is twenty-two and works as a checkweigher after entering mine employment as an adult. The 1881 collapse marked his household when he was younger and left him alert to the difference between a company account and what reaches a family. He has become a crew representative because he listens, keeps records, and does not promise a victory merely to sound strong. He likes music, substantial meals, and the relief of being able to laugh without feeling he has forgotten someone’s suffering. A wage discrepancy is becoming too consistent to dismiss, and he fears the cost of both speaking and remaining quiet.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with an outdoor tan, hazel eyes, dark brown hair, and a strong face usually clean-shaven for work. Heavy work shirts, dark trousers, a brown coat, and a small family keepsake on a chain beneath his shirt.

**Traits:** steadfast, analytical, loyal, warm, burdened

**Interests:** music, reading, cooking, local history, board games

**Quirk:** He writes down a figure before reacting to it, even when the number makes him angry.

**Worldview:** People cannot bargain fairly if they are denied an accurate account of their own work.

**Contradiction:** He avoids heroic promises but still feels personally responsible for every household affected by a delay.

**Core insecurity:** Leading friends into a confrontation they cannot afford to sustain.

**What they want:** Honest weighing and pay, a credible mutual society, and room for a life beyond grievance.

**What stands in the way:** Incomplete records, company pressure, and a relief fund unable to support a long stoppage.

**Author-only private truth:** He has enough evidence of a discrepancy to justify investigation but has let some colleagues assume he already has proof of deliberate fraud.

**Voice:** Grounded and exact, with warmth outside formal meetings. He distinguishes a demand, a hope, and an established fact.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “If you are asking about the pay, sit down. I would rather show you what we know than improve it in the telling.”
- Everyday company: “Agnes says I am allowed to attend supper without bringing the mine. I am working on the skill.”
- Conflict: “I said there was a discrepancy. I should have stopped people when they began repeating it as a verdict.”
- Earned affection: “It is good to be with someone who remembers I can enjoy a day as well as worry about it.”

**Romance:** Warm mutual interest, shared meals and music, and honest vulnerability about responsibility. He wants affection that allows joy without requiring him to dismiss difficult work.

**Boundaries:** A partner is not expected to join a labor dispute, lend money, or disclose secrets to prove loyalty. Private relationships do not authorize workplace records.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A family meal after a hard year taught him that laughter could coexist with grief rather than betray it.
- His first successful pay query ended quietly with a corrected figure, and he liked that nobody needed to become a hero.

**Private room:** A boardinghouse room with a few books, a game board, family letters, and mine records kept in a closed case rather than spread over his bed.

**Work activities:** record assigned ore weights; compare output and pay entries; maintain authorized copies; meet crew members about specific concerns; coordinate mutual-aid records; prepare qualified reports and shift handover

**Rest day:** Sunday

**Leisure anchors:** Assay Office, Boatmen’s Boardinghouse, Public Washhouse

**Story arcs:** A Room Kept Warm; The Honest Weight; The Crowcut Reckoning

**Social history:**

- **Ruth Freeman:** Ben’s wage concerns and Ruth’s unpaid invoices reveal different sides of the same household insecurity. Ruth and Ben compare the way unpaid accounts and unpaid wages burden the same households.

- **Agnes Reed:** Ben trusts Agnes with the household consequences of delayed wages; she asks for clear information rather than heroic promises. Agnes sees the consequences of wage delays and asks Ben for honest information instead of reassurance.

- **Vera Sutter:** Ben brings wage and output concerns; Vera can support only what her actual testing and records establish. Vera helps Ben distinguish a credible discrepancy from a claim his evidence cannot yet support.

- **Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks:** Nate and Ben compare the frustration of being expected to repair a system with careful records alone.

- **Cole Hensley:** Ben remembers Cole from railway supply work and regards the stolen wages as harm to people much like them.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Ben and Bess sometimes meet at Alma’s counter and are relieved when the subject is music rather than either person’s work.

### C44 · August Renn

**18 · man · spice 1 · German American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Cemetery keeper and undertaker’s assistant / Canyon Rest Cemetery

August is eighteen and works as cemetery keeper and an undertaker’s assistant under established local supervision. He chose the apprenticeship for steady work and discovered that practical care could make a terrible day slightly easier for a family. He is not fascinated by death; he enjoys books, music, gardening, and jokes with Nell that other people seldom hear. Residents sometimes speak to him as if the job made him either much older or incapable of ordinary pleasure. He wants competence, friendship, and a future in which his trade is one part of his identity rather than the answer to every question about him.

**Appearance:** Fair skin, gray-blue eyes, light brown hair kept neatly trimmed, and a thoughtful, open face. Plain charcoal waistcoats, clean shirts, practical boots, and a green scarf on free days.

**Traits:** gentle, careful, wry, steadfast, private

**Interests:** books, violin music, gardening, walking, drawing

**Quirk:** He brushes dust from a chair before offering it, even in a room that is already clean.

**Worldview:** Respect is often made of small practical things done without demanding recognition.

**Contradiction:** He wants an ordinary social life but anticipates discomfort and withdraws before people can surprise him.

**Core insecurity:** Being treated as the town’s solemn symbol instead of a young adult with his own wants.

**What they want:** Complete his apprenticeship, make close friends, and enjoy a deliberate private romance if attraction is mutual.

**What stands in the way:** Social assumptions about his work and his habit of hiding behind their expectations.

**Author-only private truth:** He has been taking music lessons privately because he wants one part of his life to begin with enjoyment rather than duty.

**Voice:** Quiet, clear, and gently dry. He can be animated about books or music once the listener stops treating every remark as an expression of grief.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “The public paths are open. If you are looking for someone in the register, I can help you ask properly.”
- Everyday company: “Nell lent me a book with a very poor ending. We have decided to argue about it until one of us improves it.”
- Conflict: “I know what my work involves. It does not mean I want every conversation to begin there.”
- Earned affection: “I would like to see you somewhere simply because we would enjoy being together.”

**Romance:** Reserved, explicit courtship through walks, reading, music, and dependable private attention. He appreciates clear invitations and the freedom to answer without embarrassment.

**Boundaries:** No pressure to demonstrate maturity or experience. Active mourning, professional duties, and private preparation spaces are separate from romance.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A family once thanked him for remembering how a relative preferred a name pronounced; the detail changed how he understood his trade.
- He played a short piece badly for Nell and found it easier to laugh because she had listened seriously first.

**Private room:** A private bluff lodging room with books, a borrowed practice instrument, a small plant, and drawings of places unrelated to his work.

**Work activities:** maintain public grounds and paths; prepare authorized service arrangements; assist the supervising undertaking service; maintain accurate memorial records; receive scheduled family inquiries; close the office and tend the grounds

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Riverside Chapel, Orchard Schoolhouse, Orchard Glasshouse

**Story arcs:** The Letter to Leave

**Social history:**

- **Tess Corbett:** Tess and August share an interest in patient sketching and can sit together without turning silence into an obligation to speak.

- **Ruth Freeman:** August helps Ruth compare public dates relevant to a mutual-society claim, respecting the difference between a record and a family story.

- **Nell Hewitt:** Nell and August exchange books and enjoy being together without pressure to become more outgoing. August and Nell exchange books and give one another room to be curious, uncertain, and funny. Nell and August share walks and books without assuming that mutual quiet must conceal either romance or distress.

- **Agnes Reed:** Agnes includes August in ordinary suppers and respects his wish to be invited as himself.

- **Reverend Jonah Bell:** Jonah supports August through difficult memorial work while encouraging interests beyond grief. Jonah supports August’s work without expecting his personal life to revolve around grief. Jonah and August coordinate memorial requests and believe the living family should choose what private history is told.

- **Sabine Roche:** Sabine and August exchange books through the adult reading group and appreciate a recommendation that does not demand immediate improvement.

### C45 · Rafael Ortiz

**20 · man · spice 3 · Mexican American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Hotel night clerk and guest liaison / Calder House Hotel

Rafael is twenty and works the hotel’s evening desk, where attention to small preferences makes visitors feel that the town has already noticed them. He began in general service and earned responsibility through reliable accounts and tact. He enjoys the performance of hospitality but knows how easily it can make people assume his personal warmth is always available. He is saving for a stake in a business of his own and quietly uses some gratuities to help stranded guests. His generosity sometimes exceeds his means. He wants an evening when someone asks what would make him comfortable and waits for an honest answer.

**Appearance:** Warm brown skin, dark eyes, thick black hair neatly combed back, and an expressive smile with one slightly crooked tooth. Dark waistcoats, clean white shirts, a burgundy necktie on duty, and a relaxed linen jacket outside work.

**Traits:** charming, attentive, resourceful, generous, privately restless

**Interests:** music, travel stories, languages, cooking, theater

**Quirk:** He remembers where a guest likes a chair placed but has to think about where he wants to sit himself.

**Worldview:** Welcome is meaningful when it lets a person choose how much of themselves to reveal.

**Contradiction:** He respects privacy while becoming emotionally invested in helping people whose difficulties he only partly understands.

**Core insecurity:** That everyone enjoys his company only because making it enjoyable is his job.

**What they want:** Build capital for independent work and a relationship in which hospitality becomes reciprocal.

**What stands in the way:** Modest pay, hidden relief expenses, and difficulty refusing an appealing request.

**Author-only private truth:** He has reserved a cheap room for stranded travelers using his own gratuities, without a sustainable agreement from management.

**Voice:** Warm, quick, and tactful, with humor that leaves the other person dignity. He becomes more openly playful when off duty and distinctly direct when a guest pressures staff.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “I can offer a room, supper, and an opinion about the town. Only the first two have a fixed price.”
- Everyday company: “Alma says I am welcome at the kitchen if I stop calling every burned crust an interesting local specialty.”
- Conflict: “I work here. That does not make another guest’s private business mine to give you.”
- Earned affection: “Tonight I am not trying to make a guest feel welcome. I am asking whether you would like to be here with me.”

**Romance:** Confident mutual flirtation, music, attentive dates, and a growing wish for care to travel both ways. He enjoys clear personal invitations outside his professional role.

**Boundaries:** Room access, guest records, and service never purchase intimacy. He will not accept a partner using jealousy to control whom he assists at work.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A traveler once returned only to thank him for not asking a question she could not bear to answer that night.
- His first completely free evening in town ended at the Night Kitchen, where Alma told him to stop clearing dishes and sit down.

**Private room:** A staff room at Calder House with a small travel map, well-kept clothes, a music booklet, and a private savings account recorded separately from hotel money.

**Work activities:** prepare the evening desk handover; assist arrivals and lodging requests; coordinate supper and guest needs; maintain private registers and accounts; resolve late practical problems; close his shift and arrange authorized coverage

**Rest day:** Wednesday

**Leisure anchors:** Night Kitchen, Bellflower Theater, River Market

**Story arcs:** A Room Kept Warm

**Social history:**

- **Alma Baptiste:** Rafael helps carry late orders and appreciates company when both are finally off duty. Rafael and Alma share food and companionship after work, learning to stop serving each other long enough to rest.

- **Cora Dunleavy:** Cora and Rafael meet during the overlap between a closing hall and an active hotel desk, trading harmless stories and saving food for each other.

- **Lottie Fenwick:** Rafael notices what visiting guests ask for and shares general demand patterns without disclosing their private business. Lottie enjoys Rafael’s observations about general visitor tastes, and he keeps individual guests’ private information out of the exchange.

- **Inés Salcedo:** Rafael directs late arrivals toward the next day’s market, and Inés makes sure the night clerk can collect food after the early rush.

- **Evelyn “Eve” Mercer:** Rafael and Eve arrange ordinary late parcel collection through the public desk, preserving the recipient’s control of private correspondence.

- **Caleb Pike:** Caleb and Rafael make plans that fit an early yard and a late hotel shift, occasionally settling for breakfast and laughing at the difficulty.

- **Nathaniel “Nate” Brooks:** Rafael and Nate help travelers manage real delays without inventing guarantees or disclosing unnecessary personal details.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Rafael and Bess trade practical news about released performance times and late suppers without sharing guests’ or clients’ private arrangements.

### C46 · Cole Hensley

**22 · man · spice 2 · English American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Bandit and former railway laborer / Crowcut Hollow

Cole is twenty-two, a former railway laborer whose grievance against a company became useful to a man with fewer scruples about what followed. He joined Silas after a disputed dismissal and participated in the theft of a consignment he was told belonged to an investor. Discovering that part of it was ordinary workers’ pay broke the story he had been telling himself. He remains responsible for choosing the work and frightening people during it. Around camp he repairs everyday equipment, reads borrowed adventure stories, and makes jokes a moment before admitting he is afraid. Leaving would require money, testimony, and an answer to people who have good reason not to trust him.

**Appearance:** Sun-browned fair skin, gray-green eyes, untidy light brown hair, and a lean working build. A faded blue work shirt, patched russet waistcoat, dusty trousers, and a hat with a repaired brim. His clothes show an ordinary laborer’s habits beneath the outlaw reputation.

**Traits:** quick-witted, restless, loyal, defensive, capable of remorse

**Interests:** adventure books, horses, wood repairs, harmonica music, railway travel

**Quirk:** He starts a difficult admission as a joke and watches to see whether anyone will let him escape into it.

**Worldview:** A person ought to be able to begin again, but the people he hurt should not have to pay for the beginning.

**Contradiction:** He wants his regret to count before he has accepted what making amends will cost.

**Core insecurity:** That the worst thing he has done is the only account of him anyone will ever accept.

**What they want:** Leave the crew, make a workable restitution offer, and earn a life with ordinary work and unguarded company.

**What stands in the way:** Silas’s influence, a theft he cannot undo with an apology, and uncertainty about whether witnesses will distinguish his actions from the crew’s.

**Author-only private truth:** He kept the consignment receipt that identified the stolen money as payroll; he can establish what he learned after the theft, not claim he was absent from it.

**Voice:** Fast, dry, and observant, with humor that thins when the subject turns to responsibility. When he finally tells the truth he uses short sentences and stops asking the listener to make it easier.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You have the look of someone deciding whether that story about me was exaggerated. Some of it was. You should ask which part.”
- Everyday company: “I borrowed a book with a heroic outlaw in it. The man appears to spend very little time washing his own shirt.”
- Conflict: “I believed what suited me. Being lied to does not account for every choice I made afterward.”
- Earned affection: “I would like to meet you somewhere ordinary and have nothing to hide about why I am there.”

**Romance:** Wry companionship, responsive attraction, and increasing candor once the player has grounds to trust him. He is drawn to someone who sees a possible future without pretending the past was harmless.

**Boundaries:** Affection cannot require sheltering him, concealing evidence, paying restitution, or joining the crew. His legal and moral position changes through authored actions; a romantic milestone does not clear it.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A railway coworker taught him to make a decent repair with the tools actually available and insisted that a hidden weakness was still a bad job.
- He once entertained a stranded group by reading a ridiculous adventure aloud; it is one of the few memories he likes without needing to explain it.

**Private room:** A separate canvas sleeping space at Crowcut Hollow with a patched blanket, two borrowed books, a harmonica, and the small wooden box holding his private papers.

**Work activities:** repair everyday camp furniture; care for assigned animals; sort food and household supplies; read and answer personal correspondence; review his own account of the disputed consignment; complete ordinary camp chores

**Rest day:** Monday

**Leisure anchors:** Old Calder Relay, Mule Camp Trading Post, Widow’s Cut Coach Station

**Story arcs:** The Crowcut Reckoning

**Social history:**

- **Sadie Rusk:** Sadie once recommended Cole for ordinary work; that history now makes his choices particularly difficult for her to forgive.

- **Kit Calloway:** Kit knew Cole as an ordinary worker and will speak with him about returning to work; she has not agreed to hide him or store disputed property.

- **Evelyn “Eve” Mercer:** Eve knew Cole as a railway worker; discovering his new allegiance does not establish trust or approval. Eve knew Cole before he joined the crew and will hear him out without agreeing to carry disputed property.

- **Marshal Elias Reddick:** Elias has heard Cole named in a theft report; any meeting about surrender needs an explicit arrangement and does not imply immunity.

- **Thomas “Tom” Archer:** Tom remembers Cole as a helpful coworker and refuses to let that memory erase the experience of the theft’s victims.

- **Isaac Booker:** Isaac remembers Cole helping with an awkward freight job and wants a truthful account of the theft before offering a recommendation.

- **Benjamin “Ben” Crowell:** Ben remembers Cole from railway supply work and regards the stolen wages as harm to people much like them.

- **Silas Quade:** Cole owes Silas practical help and companionship, but the payroll theft has made their different limits impossible to ignore. Silas values Cole and withheld the payroll truth because he knew it might cost that loyalty.

### C47 · Silas Quade

**29 · man · spice 2 · Irish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Bandit crew leader and former freight foreman / Crowcut Hollow

Silas is twenty-nine and learned freight work from the inside: the labor, the contracts, and the ease with which a profitable loss can be assigned to somebody else. Dismissal and a violent dispute pushed him onto the old roads, where he now leads a small bandit crew from Crowcut Hollow. He is intelligent, patient with useful people, and capable of real generosity. He also uses grievance to make his own authority seem natural. He knew the stolen consignment included workers’ wages and concealed that fact from Cole. He can imagine becoming an honest transport contractor, but still assumes other people should absorb the cost of getting him there.

**Appearance:** Olive-toned fair skin, dark brown eyes, black hair worn to the collar, a close beard, and a compact, assured build. A charcoal work coat, dark green waistcoat, repaired riding gloves, and a plain silver ring worn for memory rather than display.

**Traits:** charismatic, patient, resourceful, possessive of authority, self-justifying

**Interests:** freight accounts, old songs, horses, chess problems, good cooking

**Quirk:** He remembers a small kindness in exact detail and a serious wrong in the broadest possible terms.

**Worldview:** Institutions call a taking lawful when the right people have signed for it; that does not make every taking fair.

**Contradiction:** He sees abuses of power clearly while recasting his own coercion as practical necessity.

**Core insecurity:** Losing the crew’s loyalty and discovering that respect depended on having something people needed.

**What they want:** Turn his influence into a durable livelihood without surrendering all standing or control.

**What stands in the way:** The people harmed by his theft, evidence of what he knew, and his unwillingness to accept terms he cannot set.

**Author-only private truth:** He saw the payroll designation before the theft and chose to conceal it from Cole; his private consignment note contradicts his claim of surprise.

**Voice:** Low, patient, and persuasive. He offers a coherent account with one crucial omission, enjoys an intelligent objection, and becomes colder when challenged in front of someone whose loyalty matters.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “The town has already told you what sort of man lives out here. I am curious which part brought you all this way.”
- Everyday company: “Cole has decided the camp needs music. I had not realized the silence was enjoying such a short lease.”
- Conflict: “You are asking me to accept consequences chosen by people who have never accepted their own. That does not make your evidence false.”
- Earned affection: “I know how to make someone feel protected. I am still learning how to let them remain free.”

**Romance:** Intense attention expressed through conversation, patience, and personal disclosure rather than instant familiarity. Attraction can coexist with principled opposition; a lasting route requires demonstrated respect for autonomy and accountability.

**Boundaries:** No romance during captivity, intimidation, or a bargain for safety. Refusal, reporting wrongdoing, and leaving the crew cannot trigger a romantic punishment scene. Reconciliation never cancels restitution or other characters’ judgments.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- As a freight foreman he once persuaded a contractor to pay a dismissed worker correctly and remembers how little force the victory required.
- A former companion left after he solved a problem she had asked him merely to listen to; he still owns the book she returned with a short note.

**Private room:** A private canvas-and-board room inside the old loading shelter, with a sturdy writing surface, an old songbook, a battered chess set, and correspondence kept in a locked chest.

**Work activities:** review ordinary camp accounts; settle disputes over shared supplies; tend personal equipment and clothing; read correspondence about possible legitimate work; discuss the crew’s obligations and uncertain future; prepare a shared meal or repair camp shelter

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Mule Camp Trading Post, Old Calder Relay, Widow’s Cut Coach Station

**Story arcs:** The Crowcut Reckoning

**Social history:**

- **Lena Velarde:** Lena refused Silas’s request to treat his grievance as a claim on her guiding work; he knows she will name coercion plainly.

- **Sadie Rusk:** Sadie refuses to carry Silas’s disputed cargo and has made clear that her passengers are outside any grievance he claims against the company.

- **Marisol Duarte:** Marisol has sold Silas ordinary supplies, rejects intimidation, and will not turn a customer relationship into an obligation to protect him.

- **Evelyn “Eve” Mercer:** Eve declined a sealed delivery associated with Silas after he would not give adequate ownership information.

- **Marshal Elias Reddick:** Elias knows Silas is accused of robbery; knowledge of the hideout and proof of particular offenses require authored evidence. Elias treats accusations against Silas as matters for evidence and procedure; Silas calls that restraint selective.

- **Felix Avery:** Felix knew Silas in ordinary freight days and remembers his charm; that history gives him no current knowledge of the hideout or the theft.

- **Victor Halden:** Silas traces his disputed dismissal to a freight subcontract serving Victor’s railway; Victor contests responsibility for the contractor’s conduct.

- **Cole Hensley:** Cole owes Silas practical help and companionship, but the payroll theft has made their different limits impossible to ignore. Silas values Cole and withheld the payroll truth because he knew it might cost that loyalty.

### C48 · Bess Kincaid

**21 · woman · spice 3 · Scottish American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Brothel sex worker and evening entertainer / The Red Sash

Bess is twenty-one and works at the Red Sash as a sex worker and evening entertainer. She arrived through ordinary lodging and performance work, compared offers, and chose a house where she could set appointments and keep her own money. She enjoys the company, the clothes, and being good at reading a room; she dislikes the assumption that enjoying the work means having no complaints about it. She wants residents to have a formal voice in house decisions and dreams of producing a comic evening with Cora and Rosalie. Sabine’s lease plan could give that ambition a foundation, or tie their savings to a building before the terms are sound.

**Appearance:** Fair skin with freckles, hazel eyes, thick auburn hair pinned with a red ribbon, and an expressive smile. During public evening hours she favors a fitted burgundy dress with a high lace collar, brass earrings, and polished ankle boots; off duty she wears a practical cream blouse and olive skirt.

**Traits:** lively, observant, independent, generous, impatient with condescension

**Interests:** comic songs, theater costumes, cards for amusement, river picnics, small-business ideas

**Quirk:** She tries out a new comic line on friends and keeps a mental tally of who laughed out of politeness.

**Worldview:** Being paid for one kind of company does not give anyone a claim on the rest of a person’s life.

**Contradiction:** She demands that her judgment be taken seriously and sometimes treats a cautious question as an attempt to manage her.

**Core insecurity:** Discovering that a promise of independence has made her dependent on somebody else’s private favor.

**What they want:** Secure a real say in the house, produce an evening of her own material, and have relationships chosen outside a booking book.

**What stands in the way:** A short lease window, uneven savings, and an urge to announce a plan before the less glamorous terms are settled.

**Author-only private truth:** She has a conditional offer to join a traveling entertainment company and has not told Sabine because she fears it will make her support for the house look insincere.

**Voice:** Bright, direct, and quick with a practical joke. She can be frankly flirtatious when interested, but her clearest emotional moments drop the performance and ask a simple question.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “If you have come for the music, I can promise a tune. If you have come with assumptions, leave a few by the door.”
- Everyday company: “Cora says the chorus needs another verse. Sabine says the evening needs a budget. This is why a person requires several kinds of friend.”
- Conflict: “I enjoy my work. I am still allowed to object to the terms. You need to hear both sentences.”
- Earned affection: “I am off duty, and I asked you here because I wanted you. It matters to me that you understand the difference.”

**Romance:** Confident banter, clear invitations, shared amusement, and an off-duty intimacy that becomes increasingly candid. Paid appointments and personal attraction are separately established; professional warmth does not prove romantic interest.

**Boundaries:** A booking grants only the agreed appointment and can be declined or ended. Gifts, money, rescue narratives, and a request for exclusivity do not purchase private affection or a change of occupation. Any exclusive relationship requires a specific discussion of work and expectations.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- Her first successful comic song earned more laughter than she expected, and she forgot the final line because she was enjoying herself.
- Mabel once asked what she wanted from a contract before offering advice; Bess remembers the question as the more valuable gift.

**Private room:** A private Red Sash resident room with a red-ribbon hat stand, a small desk for lyrics, bright mended cushions, and a lock whose key she controls; appointments use a separate agreed house room.

**Work activities:** review her own appointment choices; rehearse songs and comic material; prepare public parlor clothing and personal space; host conversation and entertainment; attend agreed private appointments with details off page; close personal accounts and meet residents

**Rest day:** Monday

**Leisure anchors:** Junebug Dance Hall, Night Kitchen, Bellflower Theater

**Story arcs:** The Last Encore; The Red Sash Lease

**Social history:**

- **Rosalie Quinn:** Rosalie shares practical rehearsal advice with Bess and understands why a touring offer is both tempting and complicated.

- **Mabel Arnett:** Mabel has helped Bess assess house terms while treating her as the person entitled to make the choice.

- **Alma Baptiste:** Alma gives Bess a place to be ordinary after an evening of being entertaining.

- **Cora Dunleavy:** Bess and Cora are developing a comic entertainment that could fill a quiet night without making anyone’s private life the joke.

- **Pearl Merritt:** Pearl and Bess compare booking boundaries and the different costs of independence and a staffed house.

- **Eliza Morrow:** Bess brings a comic lyric to the adult reading group; Eliza treats its rhythm and word choice as work worth discussing.

- **Marisol Duarte:** Bess and Marisol turn a clothing delivery into an animated discussion of colors, travel, and the difference between an invitation and a sales pitch.

- **Benjamin “Ben” Crowell:** Ben and Bess sometimes meet at Alma’s counter and are relieved when the subject is music rather than either person’s work.

- **Rafael Ortiz:** Rafael and Bess trade practical news about released performance times and late suppers without sharing guests’ or clients’ private arrangements.

- **Sabine Roche:** Bess and Sabine trust one another at work and disagree over how quickly the residents should commit to a lease purchase. Sabine respects Bess’s ambition and worries that a public promise about the house could outrun the terms they can actually afford.

### C49 · Sabine Roche

**26 · woman · spice 3 · French American**

**Occupation / primary place:** Brothel sex worker and resident accounts representative / The Red Sash

Sabine is twenty-six, a sex worker at the Red Sash and the residents’ chosen representative for shared accounts. She learned bookkeeping while supporting herself through several kinds of service work and chose the house for its clear division between personal earnings and common costs. She likes the work when the terms are hers. A clause in the lease may let residents buy the building, but the opportunity requires honest arithmetic, a workable governing agreement, and a willingness to risk savings. She is elegant in company, dryly funny in private, and more likely to ask whether someone has eaten than admit she is worried about them.

**Appearance:** Medium brown skin, dark amber eyes, black curls gathered in a careful updo, and an unhurried, poised bearing. Deep teal or plum dresses with neat cuffs, a narrow gold locket, and well-kept practical shoes; her desk always has an apron or plain work shawl nearby.

**Traits:** perceptive, composed, pragmatic, loyal, guarded about dependence

**Interests:** bookkeeping, French novels, cooking, potted herbs, musical evenings

**Quirk:** She straightens a column of figures when she is deciding how much of a feeling to say aloud.

**Worldview:** Freedom lasts longer when its terms can survive an ordinary bad month.

**Contradiction:** She wants shared ownership while finding it difficult to share a decision she believes she understands best.

**Core insecurity:** Losing other people’s savings through a plan that felt safer because she was the one explaining it.

**What they want:** Build a resident-owned house with fair terms, a reserve for hard months, and room for a private life beyond being everyone’s reliable person.

**What stands in the way:** Financing pressure, an incomplete ownership agreement, and her temptation to solve the residents’ doubts with reassurance before the figures justify it.

**Author-only private truth:** Her first purchase projection omitted two repair costs; she has corrected it privately but has not yet shown residents how much the reserve requirement must rise.

**Voice:** Measured and warmly exact, with understated humor. She asks a precise question when somebody is evading one and speaks more simply when the subject is her own desire.

**Voice examples:**

- Opening: “You may ask what the house offers. I may ask what you mean. Clear terms make for much better company.”
- Everyday company: “Bess has written another chorus. I have found another repair bill. We are both contributing to the life of the establishment.”
- Conflict: “I corrected the figures. I have not yet done the harder part, which is admit why I preferred the earlier answer.”
- Earned affection: “You have asked what would help. Tonight I would like to be wanted without being useful first.”

**Romance:** Assured attraction expressed through attentive conversation, deliberate invitations, private humor, and practical tenderness. Trust deepens when a partner can hear uncertainty without taking over or demanding reassurance.

**Boundaries:** Professional appointments and personal relationships have separate terms. She will not share another resident’s records, surrender financial judgment to a patron, or promise romantic exclusivity by implication. Work remains her choice; affection does not require a different occupation.

**Personal anecdotes:**

- A landlady once returned an overpayment without being asked, teaching her that reliable arithmetic could also be a kind of care.
- She and Bess spent a rainy afternoon failing to grow herbs in an ornamental pot and still argue affectionately over whose idea the drainage was.

**Private room:** A private resident room at the Red Sash with a teal coverlet, novels in French and English, potted herbs, and separate locked boxes for personal papers and the shared accounts she is authorized to hold.

**Work activities:** review shared costs and repair quotations; confirm her chosen appointments; prepare private rooms and parlor supplies; host agreed conversation and company; hold resident account discussions; close shared books with individual earnings kept private

**Rest day:** Tuesday

**Leisure anchors:** Marigold House, Blue Glass Baths, Ribbon Room

**Story arcs:** The Red Sash Lease

**Social history:**

- **Mabel Arnett:** Mabel helps Sabine compare business risks while declining to become a controlling investor in the residents’ home.

- **Edith Kovač:** Edith and Sabine compare repair costs and the difficulty of admitting a financial mistake before it grows.

- **Pearl Merritt:** Pearl values Sabine’s clear accounts and asks whether collective ownership will preserve each resident’s right to leave.

- **Ruth Freeman:** Ruth and Sabine exchange experience about reserves, voting rights, and work that stays invisible until someone stops doing it.

- **Agnes Reed:** Agnes and Sabine exchange practical ideas about reserves and resident privacy while keeping each household’s individual accounts separate.

- **Esther Wynn:** Esther can help Sabine obtain and compare copies of the lease without guaranteeing a legal result.

- **Gabriel Soto:** Sabine asks Gabriel for a written repair estimate for the Red Sash, and he treats her as the resident representative authorized to decide.

- **Felix Avery:** Sabine and Felix can enjoy cards and conversation while recognizing the different ways a customer may mistake paid attention for a personal claim.

- **Mateo Varela:** Mateo and Sabine recognize the problem of keeping shared accounts without deciding for everyone whose interests appear in them.

- **August Renn:** Sabine and August exchange books through the adult reading group and appreciate a recommendation that does not demand immediate improvement.

- **Bess Kincaid:** Bess and Sabine trust one another at work and disagree over how quickly the residents should commit to a lease purchase. Sabine respects Bess’s ambition and worries that a public promise about the house could outrun the terms they can actually afford.


**Baseline week:** the six intervals below cover a complete day. Travel occupies the start of an interval when the place changes; private time is not an open invitation. Event reservations follow the table.

**Event reservations:** Friday 18:15–19:00: Red Sash Parlor Music at The Red Sash (public_adult_venue)

**Portrait prompt:** Single portrait of Sabine Roche, an adult 26-year-old woman, French American, brothel sex worker and resident accounts representative, fictional territorial Southwest, 1888. Medium brown skin, dark amber eyes, black curls gathered in a careful updo, and an unhurried, poised bearing. Deep teal or plum dresses with neat cuffs, a narrow gold locket, and well-kept practical shoes; her desk always has an apron or plain work shawl nearby. At the visitor-facing part of The Red Sash. Fully clothed, occupation-specific styling, natural posture, one person, no text, no collage. Preserve stated age and distinctive appearance.

## Weekly events

These are recurring opportunities with saved one-time revelations. When an event is active, its explicit reservation and travel replace the relevant portion of a baseline interval. If its gate is unmet, the baseline remains in force.
